Albares supports Andorra's rapprochement with the European Union during his trip to the country
Spain
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
15:56:02 CEST
22:56:02 KST

The Minister for Foreign Affairs, European Union and Cooperation, José Manuel Albares, visited the Principality of Andorra this Monday, 14 September, at an excellent time for bilateral relations. The Minister was received by the Head of Government of the Principality, Xavier Espot Zamora. He also met with his Andorran counterpart, Imma Tor Faus, with whom he signed three bilateral agreements.
During his meeting with the Minister for Foreign Affairs of Andorra, the Minister conveyed Spain's support for Andorra's rapprochement with the European Union. In the words of the Minister: "A more integrated Andorra in Europe will be a more prosperous Andorra, and that prosperity will have positive effects not only for the Principality, but also for the neighbouring regions of the Alt Pirineu".
During the meeting, the main issues on the bilateral and international agenda were also discussed, including matters of cross-border cooperation between the two countries.
Following the meeting, the Minister held a joint press conference with his counterpart, in which he referred to the main issues addressed during the meeting, highlighting economic measures and the improvement of infrastructures and communications, as well as initiatives in favour of the populations on both sides, in both the healthcare and educational fields.
The Minister also visited the emblematic Radio Andorra building, where he delivered a speech and held a meeting with authorities and relevant figures from civil society, as well as with economic actors.
Catalan in Europe
During his speech, Albares, who also delivered a few words in Catalan, pointed out that "Andorra is the only country in the world where Catalan is the sole official language". In this regard, the Minister underlined the relevance of linguistic diversity in Europe and stressed the importance of making progress in the recognition of the official status of Catalan, together with Basque and Galician, within the European Union.
Bilateral agreements
During his visit, the Minister signed three agreements with his Andorran counterpart: an agreement on the sharing of confiscated instruments and proceeds of crime, which establishes criteria for the sharing and prioritises the compensation of victims; and two other agreements focused on the cultural and educational fields.
The agreement between the Instituto Cervantes and the Department of Cultural Promotion of the Government of the Principality of Andorra will allow Andorra to make use of the network of Instituto Cervantes centres for cultural promotion activities. The third agreement, on diplomatic and consular training and cooperation between the Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Andorra and the Diplomatic School, will reinforce cooperation in the field of professional development and continuous training of diplomatic and consular staff.
Visit to La Seu d'Urgell
The Minister concluded his trip by travelling to La Seu d'Urgell to meet with the Episcopal Co-Prince, the Bishop of Urgell, Monsignor Josep Lluis Serrano i Pentinat, to whom he conveyed his appreciation for Andorra's participation at the highest level in the upcoming Ibero-American Summit, which will be held in Madrid on 4 and 5 November.

Romanian farmers clash with police in Bucharest over EU livestock ban
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:51:32 UTC
22:51:32 KST

Footage shows protesters pushing and scuffling with security forces, throwing objects and knocking down barriersbefore gendarmes deployed tear gas.
The demonstration began peacefully in Victory Square (Piața Victoriei), where farmers gathered to protest problems affecting the livestock and farming sectors. Tensions later escalated when some protesters tried to block roads around the square and confronted gendarmes, who responded with tear gas.
The protest was organised by Romanian sheep farmers and other agricultural producers, with several hundred to more than 1,000 people taking part. Organisers had called on protesters to bring sheep, tractors and hay bales to the square, but restrictions prevented live animals and heavy machinery from entering the demonstration area.
Farmers were protesting an EU-wide ban on exports of live sheep and goats from Romania, extended until 31 December 2026 following outbreaks of peste des petits ruminants and sheep pox. They also called for better compensation for animals culled because of the outbreaks and for crops affected by drought.
Protesters further demanded the resignation of Alexandru Bociu, head of Romania’s National Sanitary Veterinary and Food Safety Authority (ANSVSA), over his handling of the epidemiological situation.
At least three protesters required medical attention after being exposed to tear gas and were treated at the scene. No law enforcement officers were reported injured, while two participants were taken to a police station for questioning.
Traffic around the square, including the Victoria Underpass and Kiseleff Boulevard, was blocked for several hours before being reopened.

More than four million Russians apply to vote online in State Duma elections
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:50:25 UTC
22:50:25 KST

MOSCOW, September 15. /TASS/. More than four million voters have applied to participate in remote electronic voting in the upcoming elections to the State Duma, Russian Central Election Commission Chairwoman Ella Pamfilova said.
"Data are currently being processed, and we will have the full picture later. At present, more than 2.9 million applications have been submitted through the ‘Mobile Voter’ system. More than four million [applications] have been submitted for remote electronic voting. So our voters are very active," Pamfilova said.
Registration for remote electronic voting in 32 regions ran from August 3 to September 14, and the applications received are still being processed. In total, residents of 33 regions will be able to vote online in the upcoming elections. Moscow residents do not need to submit a preliminary application to participate in electronic voting. Elections to the ninth State Duma will be held over three days, September 18, 19 and 20. Regional elections at other levels will also be held as part of the nationwide election day.

Understanding Your Credit Score: What Affects It and Why  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:29 UTC
22:49:29 KST

Have you ever wondered why some people breeze through financial applications while others face constant rejections? The secret usually lies in a simple three-digit number: your credit score. This vital financial metric acts as a report card for your adult financial life, signaling to lenders how responsibly you manage borrowed money. Whether you are aiming to buy your dream home, lease a new vehicle, or secure a fast loan approval online, that three-digit number is the ultimate gatekeeper to your financial goals. Understanding how it works is the first step toward taking control of your financial future.
At its core, a credit score is a mathematical prediction of how likely you are to pay back money you borrow. When you apply for a credit card, a mortgage, or a simple loan online, banks and financial institutions pull this score to assess their risk. A higher score means you are viewed as a low-risk borrower, which translates to better interest rates and more favorable terms. Conversely, a lower score can lead to high interest rates, requiring you to pay significantly more over the life of a debt, or even outright denial of credit.
The Building Blocks of Your Credit Score
Most scoring models break down your financial behavior into five distinct categories. Understanding these components is the key to mastering your financial profile. Here is what actually moves the needle:
1. Payment History (35%): This is the heaviest hitter in your credit profile. Lenders want to know one thing above all else: do you pay your bills on time? Even a single payment that is thirty days late can drag your score down significantly and remain on your record for years.
2. Credit Utilization Ratio (30%): This sounds complicated, but it is simply the amount of credit you are currently using compared to your total available credit limits. If you have a credit card with a $10,000 limit and a $9,000 balance, you appear financially stretched. Financial experts highly recommend keeping this ratio below 30% across all your revolving accounts.
3. Length of Credit History (15%): Older is better when it comes to credit accounts. Lenders like to see a long track record of responsible management. This is why financial advisors often suggest keeping your oldest credit card open and active, even if you rarely use it.
4. Credit Mix (10%): Having a variety of account types—such as a credit card, an auto loan, and a student loan—shows lenders that you can juggle different types of debt successfully.
5. New Credit (10%): Every time you apply for a new line of credit, it results in a "hard inquiry" on your report, which can temporarily ding your score. Opening several new accounts in a short period signals financial distress to potential lenders.
Why You Should Care About Your Score
Your credit score impacts much more than just your ability to borrow money from a bank. It weaves its way into various aspects of your daily life, often when you least expect it.
- Housing opportunities: Landlords routinely check credit reports to determine if a potential tenant will reliably pay rent on time.
- Insurance premiums: In many regions, auto and homeowner insurance companies use credit-based insurance scores to help set your monthly premiums.
- Employment prospects: While employers do not see your actual score, they can often request a modified version of your credit report during the hiring process, especially for roles involving financial management or high security.
- Utility deposits: A strong financial track record can often help you bypass hefty security deposits when setting up electricity, water, or internet services at a new address.
Actionable Steps to Build and Protect Your Credit
Improving your credit profile is not an overnight process. However, adopting consistent, positive habits will yield excellent results over time.
- Set up automatic payments: Since payment history is the largest factor, ensure you never miss a due date by automating at least the minimum payments on all your accounts.
- Pay down revolving debt: Focus on lowering credit card balances to improve your credit utilization ratio. Lowering your balances is often the fastest way to see a noticeable bump in your overall score.
- Review your reports annually: Errors happen. You might find a late payment reported incorrectly or an account you never even opened due to identity theft. Dispute these inaccuracies immediately with the credit bureaus to have them removed.
- Limit new applications: Only apply for new credit when you genuinely need it, pacing out your applications to avoid accumulating too many hard inquiries.
Your credit score is a living, breathing reflection of your financial habits. It is not static; it fluctuates based on the decisions you make every single month. By understanding the forces that drive this number up or down, you can make informed choices that build a solid financial foundation. Cultivating good credit takes patience and discipline, but the ultimate reward—a lifetime of financial flexibility and peace of mind—is absolutely worth the effort.

Wallester appoints CTO and CRO to its Management Board and strengthens AML oversight  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:29 UTC
22:49:29 KST

Wallester AS, an Estonian-licensed payment institution and Visa Principal Member, has appointed its Chief Technology Officer, Andrei Gornostajev, and Chief Risk Officer, Rein Ojavere, to its Management Board, effective 1 September 2026. In parallel, the company has formally assigned Management Board member Fred Sooläte responsibility for anti-money laundering (AML), counter-terrorist financing and international sanctions compliance. Together, these changes provide the five-member Management Board with direct representation in technology, risk, compliance, and AML.
"As Wallester grows, the structure of the company needs to grow with it. Our Management Board now represents technology, risk, compliance and AML directly. That changes how decisions get made: the right expertise sits in the room earlier, and responsibilities are clearer across the business," said Sergei Astafjev, CEO of Wallester.
Andrei Gornostajev, Chief Technology Officer
Andrei Gornostajev joins the Management Board with responsibility for technology and R&D. He was part of the team that built Wallester's Visa card issuing platform from the ground up in 2017 and returned as CTO in January 2024. His career also includes senior engineering roles at Microsoft, and he now leads Wallester's 70+ person R&D organisation spanning technology, product and design.
"As Wallester scaled, reliability, security and compliance developed into increasingly specialised engineering and governance disciplines. That evolution is also why decisions about providers, cloud infrastructure and key technology partners can become board-level concerns. That is why technology increasingly needs a seat at the table, and that is how I see my role on the Management Board," said Gornostajev.
Fred Sooläte, Chief AML Officer
Fred Sooläte joined Wallester in 2020 as Chief Compliance Officer, leading the company's anti-money laundering, counter-terrorist financing and sanctions compliance work. In May 2021, he was appointed to the Management Board and, from then, held the statutory Management Board responsibility for that area until September 2023. He resumed the remit from 1 September 2026 and also holds responsibility for Legal and data protection.
"Wallester today is a bigger and more complex business than when I first took this on – more products, more partners, a broader footprint. My task is to ensure that our AML framework continues to develop alongside the company's growth. Having helped build much of it gives me the context to do that quickly," said Sooläte.
He is a trained lawyer who has held a range of roles across other financial institutions, spanning legal, compliance and risk responsibilities, and has led much of Wallester's regulatory groundwork, including its cross-border passporting and AML/CFT framework.
Rein Ojavere, Chief Risk Officer
Rein Ojavere is taking on the role of Chief Risk Officer. He joined Wallester in May 2025 to lead the enterprise-wide risk management function and now holds executive responsibility for the second line of defence, including the compliance, risk management and information security governance functions and the MLRO function within compliance. Fred Sooläte, in turn, is the Management Board member designated under Estonian law as responsible for anti-money laundering, counter-terrorist financing and sanctions compliance.
"Unifying risk, compliance and information security under one board seat breaks down operational silos. In practice, this means unified oversight, synchronised reporting and streamlined processes – allowing us to move faster while bringing more depth and quality to our overall risk management framework," said Ojavere.
He brings more than two decades in financial services, including board-level roles at Bondora and Finora Bank, and is a CFA charterholder and holder of the Financial Risk Manager certification.
About Wallester
Wallester is an Estonian-licensed payment institution and has been a Visa Principal Member since 2018. Founded in 2016, Wallester helps companies across the European Economic Area to run their finances and launch their branded card programmes. Its team of more than 200 professionals is based in Estonia, Latvia, and France.
Wallester was named Top Startup in Estonia at the 2024 Äripäev TOP100 Gala, and ranked 38th overall in the 2026 FT1000 Europe's Fastest-Growing Companies list – first among Estonian companies and first in the Fintech, Financial Services & Insurance category.
Website: www.wallester.com

Kaunas hosted second Pride event calling for freedom for LGBTQ+ community and others  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:29 UTC
22:49:29 KST

VILNIUS – The second Kaunas Pride event took place in Lithuania’s second-largest city on Saturday, bringing together around 500 people to support freedom of self-expression and stand up against hatred and discrimination.
Participants marched from St. Michael the Archangel Church along Laisves Avenue and back, demanding more rights for LGBTQ+ people. They also expressed their support for Palestine and other marginalized groups in society.
The march ended with the reading of the community’s manifesto.
Participants carried LGBTQ+, Palestinian, Ukrainian and European Union flags. Many wore Pride symbols and carried signs calling for equality and greater recognition of LGBTQ+ rights.
A banner at the front of the march read “We were, we are, we will be”, a slogan repeatedly chanted by participants during the entire march. They also chanted slogans calling for solidarity and social justice.
The organizers of this year’s event called for greater representation of LGBTQIA+ people in Lithuania’s education system, legal recognition and protection of same-sex families, and accessible, inclusive health care.
“We are protesting transphobia and homophobia,” Beata Starodubova, spokeswoman for Kaunas Pride, told BNS before the march.
She assumed before the event that the protest was likely to draw fewer people than in 2021, when the march brought together about 3,000 participants.
“A protest is a protest – it’s not just a march or a celebration. We believe there will be fewer people because this protest has a political mission,” Starodubova said.
According to the organizers, the rights of LGBTQIA+ people are also linked to the rights of other marginalized groups, which is why the march aimed to draw attention to manifestations of hatred and discrimination in society.
“Whether we’re talking about workers’ rights, migrants’ rights, or civilians being systematically killed in the Palestinian territories, we are all fighting for life and the right to freely be ourselves,” the Kaunas Pride manifesto reads.
The march was accompanied by a counter-protest staged in downtown Kaunas by people opposing LGBTQ+ ideas and a religious gathering.
About 10 Catholics gathered near the intersection of Laisves Avenue and Vytauto Avenue, where they prayed the Rosary and sang hymns throughout the march.
Meanwhile, 13 people gathered outside Kaunas City Municipality to demonstrate against what they called “LGBT ideology,” holding Lithuanian flags and signs opposing LGBTQ+ issues in schools, and shouting “shame” at marchers.
The march was overseen by a large police presence, and, reportedly, there were some arrests during the event.
The Kaunas Pride march was first held in 2021.

Estonia helps establish educational center in Ukraine  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:28 UTC
22:49:28 KST

TALLINN - A new education and innovation center, EDUHUB, a result of cooperation between Estonia and Ukraine, was opened in the Ukrainian city of Zhytomyr on September 12.
The goal of EDUHUB is to offer teachers a modern learning and development environment and to support the implementation of STEAM education, technology education, and innovative teaching methods in the Zhytomyr Oblast.
The contribution of the Tallinn Teachers' House to the creation of EDUHUB has been multifaceted. Within the project, the Tallinn Teachers' House has been responsible for planning and organizing the training program, participating in the selection of necessary STEAM equipment and furnishings for the center, and supporting its substantive development. In collaboration with the company Insplay, practical STEAM learning materials and modern educational tools have been developed to support both teacher training and children's learning activities.
Hele Leek-Ambur, the director of the Tallinn Teachers' House, noted that it is difficult to put into words how much warmth and gratitude filled their days in Zhytomyr. "Thanks to the support of ESTDEV, the Tallinn Teachers' House was able to carry out a truly meaningful undertaking - the creation of the innovative education and competence center EDUHUB. We equipped the center with modern teaching aids and initiated training courses that provide local teachers with new knowledge and opportunities."
Over the coming months, the focus will be on supporting the professional development of teachers, preparing local trainers, and promoting the wider use of the center's facilities. A key goal of the project is to create a strong local network of trainers in the Zhytomyr Oblast. To this end, a core group of 19 Ukrainian teachers is participating in the project; their task, after completing the training, is to share the acquired knowledge and experience with other teachers in the region. This approach helps ensure the project's sustainability and creates a system that will continue to function even after the project concludes.
"The dedication of Ukrainian educators deserves great recognition - they continue their work at a time when their country is at war. This makes it all the more important to maintain and develop cooperation and professional exchange of experiences between our educational institutions," said Tallinn Deputy Mayor Andrei Kante. "The opening of EDUHUB provides an opportunity to share the practical experiences of Tallinn's educational institutions with our Ukrainian colleagues and to expand our cooperation network. I thank the specialists of the Tallinn Teachers' House and our partners, whose work has made this possible. We also have a lot to learn from our Ukrainian colleagues. By visiting schools and kindergartens in Zhytomyr, we gained valuable knowledge on how to manage educational institutions and ensure the continuation of studies in a crisis situation," Kante added.
The opening of EDUHUB was also attended by Kaidi Klaas, project manager at the Tallinn Teachers' House, partners from ESTDEV, and representatives from the Ukrainian education sector. The EDUHUB project is an excellent example of how international cooperation, mutual trust, and a shared desire to contribute to education can help create a lasting impact on teachers, learners, and communities as a whole.

Seimas speaker: Lithuania has full range of measures to deal with airspace violators  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:26 UTC
22:49:26 KST

VILNIUS - Lithuania and its allies have a full range of measures to eliminate airspace violators, and the air defense system is being developed consistently, Seimas Speaker Juozas Olekas stated on Tuesday.
"I believe that last night’s events demonstrated that we have a full range of measures at our disposal to eliminate airspace violators," Olekas told reporters at the Seimas.
His remarks followed an incident where a drone that had flown into Lithuania’s airspace from Belarus was shot down in Kaisiadorys District by Italian fighter jets, stationed at the NATO Siauliai air base, in the early hours of Tuesday.
"We see that air police pilots carrying out air defense missions are capable of destroying such airspace violators. We have a whole range of measures, including ground-based, that can be used by both our military personnel and members of domestic (security) services... So if there were more (drones), more would probably be shot down," the parliamentary speaker asserted.
According to him, Lithuania has been acquiring air defense capabilities for 15 years now, and its readiness is only increasing, aided by the experience of the Ukrainians who are resisting the Russian invasion.
"Our services are preparing for that (airspace violations). Could they prepare better? Yes, they could. And they are moving in that direction. I believe that with every order and every project we implement, we are better defended than we have been so far," Olekas said.
Early Tuesday, as the unmanned aircraft flew over Lithuanian territory, potential air threat alert notifications were sent to residents in Vilnius and Kaunas Counties.
As Russia’s invasion of Ukraine continues, potential air threat alerts have been issued repeatedly in Lithuania over the past few months. However, Tuesday’s incident marked the first time a drone had been shot down in the country’s airspace.
A NATO fighter shot down a Ukrainian drone over Estonia in May, in what was the first interception of a foreign drone in the airspace of a Baltic state by NATO's air police since the war began. In June, French fighter jets participating in NATO's Baltic Air Policing mission downed a drone over Latvia, which was the first time a drone had been shot down in that country‘s airspace.
Parts of southeastern Lithuania were last warned about a potential air threat on Sunday. However, this turned out to be a false alert triggered by a flock of birds.
Earlier that same day, military radars detected an object near Varena in southern Lithuania. The military said that the object, which likely came from Belarus, traveled across the country’s territory before entering the airspace of Russia’s Kaliningrad region.
However, officials did not issue an air threat alert over that incursion, citing insufficient information.

US bankruptcy court to rule on authorizing restructuring of airBaltic's debts  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:24 UTC
22:49:24 KST

RIGA - The United States Bankruptcy Court for the Southern District of New York will today rule on authorizing the restructuring the financial obligations of Latvia's airBaltic airline under the Chapter 11 procedure established by U.S. law, according to publicly available information.
The court’s docket includes an order allowing the debtor to receive financing after the commencement of the Chapter 11 process.
The Latvian national airline has secured a binding commitment from financiers to provide EUR 350 million in new financing to strengthen the company’s liquidity during the Chapter process (debtor-in-possession, or DIP, financing).
The financing was arranged by Strategic Value Partners, and Barclays, Hayfin Capital Management, Morgan Stanley, Oaktree Capital Management, and Strategic Value Partners have committed to providing it. The interest rate is SOFR plus 8 percent, or currently approximately 12 percent.
The court’s docket also includes an order ensuring adequate protection for creditors whose claims were secured prior to the commencement of the Chapter 11 process.
The petition to commence the Chapter 11 process was filed by airBaltic and its subsidiaries, Air Baltic Training and Baltijas Kravu Centrs. Accordingly, the court plans to consider the petition for joint administration of the case.
The court also plans to consider the petition to grant protection under the automatic stay, an application regarding the management of funds, including a plan to allow the debtor to continue using its existing cash management systems and bank accounts, as well as an application regarding employee salaries and benefits, including a plan to allow employee benefit programs to continue under normal business operations and to pay employee obligations related to these programs that arose prior to the commencement of the Chapter 11 process.
The court’s docket also includes a motion seeking permission for airBaltic to pay claims arising prior to the commencement of Chapter 11 process to suppliers and secured creditors based outside the U.S., as well as a tax motion seeking permission for airBaltic to pay certain taxes and fees.
Also scheduled for consideration is a motion regarding the customer loyalty program, which would allow airBaltic to maintain its existing loyalty programs and fulfill related customer obligations that arose prior to the commencement of the Chapter 11 process, as well as a motion regarding critical aviation contracts, an insurance motion, and a case administration motion.
The court also plans to consider a motion to extend the deadline for the submission of schedules and financial statements, a motion regarding the submission of a consolidated list of creditors, as well as a motion appointing Epiq Corporate Restructuring as the agent for the administration of claims and notices by airBaltic debtors.
airBaltic CEO Erno Hilden previously noted that securing the planned financing of EUR 350 million is still subject to court approval, which is expected to be granted in the coming days.
In the coming months, airBaltic will engage all stakeholders in an effort to reach sustainable terms and an agreement that would allow the company to remain competitive in the future.
Hilden summarized that, following this process, the final outcome will be a so-called reorganization plan, which will need to be approved by the court. The process is expected to be completed by mid-2027.
Jurgis Sedlenieks, vice chairman of the airBaltic board, told LETA that the decision was made to initiate this procedure in the U.S. because the U.S. judiciary has the most extensive experience working with the aviation industry. According to him, U.S. courts have handled debt restructuring cases of airlines larger than airBaltic, so they have a clear roadmap and know the necessary steps.
As LETA reported, airBaltic has voluntarily initiated a U.S. Chapter 11 process to restructure its financial obligations while continuing its operations. At the same time, the airline has secured a binding commitment from financiers to provide EUR 350 million in new financing to strengthen the company’s liquidity and operations at an interest rate of approximately 12 percent.
Consequently, the previous plan to raise up to EUR 257 million at an annual interest rate of 25 percent from bondholders will not be implemented.
The airline indicates that Chapter 11 protection shields airBaltic from creditors’ claims until the restructuring of its liabilities is completed.
Chapter 11 is a well-established U.S. legal framework, recognized internationally and used regularly by U.S. and international airlines, that allows companies to restructure their finances and raise capital while continuing day-to-day operations under the supervision of the U.S. Court. airBaltic is using this process to address its financial obligations and achieve a more sustainable capital structure. airBaltic notes that Chapter 11 is not the legal equivalent of the Latvian legal protection process.
The company also reports that flights and customer service will continue as usual. All flights will operate according to the scheduled flight plan; existing tickets and reservations remain valid, and passengers can continue to purchase tickets and travel with airBaltic as before.
The airline states that airBaltic has received a binding commitment from financiers to provide EUR 350 million in new financing to ensure the company’s liquidity and operations during the Chapter 11 proceedings (debtor-in-possession, or DIP, financing).
The financing was arranged by Strategic Value Partners and funded by Barclays, Hayfin Capital Management, Morgan Stanley, Oaktree Capital Management and Strategic Value Partners, to provide liquidity and support operations during the Chapter 11 process. The interest rate is SOFR plus 8 percent (i.e., approximately 12 percent).
The company tentatively plans to complete the Chapter 11 proceedings by June 2027, subject to the necessary U.S. Court approvals and other conditions of the proceedings.
The restructuring process is taking place under the supervision of the United States Bankruptcy Court for the Southern District of New York (the U.S. Court).
The company will seek customary U.S. Court approval to access this financing in the coming days. Together with cash generated from ongoing operations, the financing is expected to provide sufficient liquidity for airBaltic to continue its operations during the Chapter 11 process.
During the Chapter 11 proceedings, airBaltic will continue its day-to-day operations, and the company’s current management board and supervisory board will continue to fulfill their duties. The company will continue to meet its obligations to suppliers, service providers, and other business partners in the usual course of business.
As previously reported, under the previous plan, airBaltic bondholders were expected to meet on Tuesday, September 15, to decide on the terms of bridge financing of up to EUR 257 million at a 25 percent interest rate.
airBaltic indicated at the time that it had reached an agreement with certain existing bondholders and third-party financing providers on the terms of bridge financing of up to EUR 257 million; however, approval from airBaltic’s bondholders and shareholders is still required to receive the planned financing.
The airBaltic board has approved a business plan that initially called for raising EUR 225 million in interim financing to improve liquidity, using the existing collateral package for the 2029 senior bonds.
In the long term, the company is expected to be recapitalized with more than EUR 225 million in new debt and EUR 100 million in new equity, while a portion of the existing 2029 bonds will be converted into company equity, with the remainder replaced by new, smaller debt of up to EUR 125 million.
In August, airBaltic bondholders approved the capitalization of the interest payments on the next two bond maturities, as well as several other measures that provide the airline with additional flexibility in implementing its revised business plan.
Bondholders supported the capitalization of interest payments due on August 14 and November 14 of this year, adding them to the principal amount of the bonds rather than paying them in cash. A decision was also made to grant a temporary exemption from minimum liquidity requirements through November 2026.
The airBaltic group’s revenue last year increased by 4.2 percent compared to 2024, totaling EUR 779.344 million. Meanwhile, airBaltic group’s losses last year totaled EUR 44.337 million, which is 2.7 times less than in 2024. The airline carried a total of 5.2 million passengers in 2025, which is 1 percent more than in 2024.
Last summer, Germany’s national airline, Lufthansa, became a shareholder in airBaltic. Currently, the Latvian government owns 88.37 percent of airBaltic’s shares, Lufthansa holds 10 percent, the company Aircraft Leasing 1, owned by Danish businessman Lars Thuesen, a financial investor, holds 1.62 percent, and 0.01 percent is held by other shareholders. The company’s share capital is EUR 41.819 million.

Lithuanian president: drone downing shows that 'we can when we want to'  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:24 UTC
22:49:24 KST

VILNIUS – Actions taken in the Kaisiadorys district to shoot down a drone that had violated Lithuanian airspace demonstrate that if there is a genuine need, it is possible to do so, President Gitanas Nauseda has said.
However, according to the Lithuanian leader, capabilities for detecting airspace violators must continue to be developed.
“This is an example that shows that if we truly want to, we can and are capable of doing so, and we will continue to improve our detection capabilities as much as possible and, without a doubt, respond very quickly to ensure the safety of our people,” Nauseda, who is currently visiting Finland, said in video comments released by the presidential office.
He said that he currently has no information on whether the downed drone was carrying explosives, but he expects to receive more details from the relevant agencies by Wednesday morning, when a previously scheduled meeting of the State Defense Council will take place to discuss the development of air defense capabilities.
Nauseda also said he had discussed the incident with Finnish Prime Minister Petteri Orpo.
Early Tuesday, a drone that had flown into Lithuania’s airspace from Belarus was shot down in Kaisiadorys District, near the Kruonis Pumped-Storage Power Station, by Italian fighter jets stationed at the NATO air base in Siauliai.
As the unmanned aircraft flew over Lithuanian territory, potential air threat alert notifications were sent to residents in Vilnius and Kaunas Counties.
As Russia’s invasion of Ukraine continues, potential air threat alerts have been issued repeatedly in Lithuania over the past few months. However, Tuesday’s incident marked the first time a drone had been shot down in the country’s airspace.
A NATO fighter shot down a Ukrainian drone over Estonia in May, in what was the first interception of a foreign drone in the airspace of a Baltic state by NATO's air police since the war began. In June, French fighter jets participating in NATO's Baltic Air Policing mission downed a drone over Latvia, which was the first time a drone had been shot down in that country‘s airspace.

====================================================================================================

Reduction in VAT rate on fuel would lower prices by about 15 cents per liter - Economics Ministry  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:19 UTC
22:49:19 KST

RIGA - A reduction in the value-added tax (VAT) rate on fuel from 21 percent to 12 percent would, at current price levels at gas stations, lower the price of diesel fuel by approximately 15.6 cents per liter and the price of gasoline by approximately 14.6 cents per liter, the Ministry of Economics (EM) told LETA.
EM notes that such a reduction in fuel prices would be possible if the full amount of the tax cut were reflected in retail prices.
VAT is a tax on final consumption. Businesses registered as VAT payers deduct input tax on fuel used in their economic activities, so a reduction in the tax rate does not change their costs. For freight transport, agricultural, logistics, and manufacturing companies, fuel costs excluding tax would remain unchanged, explains the Ministry of Economics.
In assessing the impact on the state budget, the Ministry of Economics notes that the reduction in state budget revenue stems solely from fuel sold for final consumption. The Ministry of Economics estimates this at approximately EUR 6.3 million per month, assuming that final consumption accounts for 39-42 percent of the total fuel volume. If the measure were applied for three months, the cost would be approximately EUR 18-20 million. In the event of incomplete pass-through, budget costs do not decrease in proportion to consumer benefits.
If only a portion of the rate reduction is reflected in the retail price, the pre-tax price increases, thereby reducing the budget loss per liter by approximately four percent, while the benefit to end consumers decreases in proportion to the pass-through rate. With a 61 percent pass-through, budget costs would be approximately EUR 6 million per month, of which approximately EUR 3.9 million would reach end consumers, while approximately EUR 2.2 million per month would remain within the supply and distribution chain, according to the Ministry.
For a household that purchases 100 liters of fuel per month, the savings in the event of a full pass-through would be approximately EUR 15.60 per month. On average, savings per end consumer in the country would be no more than approximately EUR 7.90 per month, but the actual figure per household is lower, as final consumption also includes refueling by non-residents and partially deductible light vehicle costs for VAT payers, the Ministry notes.
The Competition Council’s analysis of the excise tax reduction in April of this year shows that 58-64 percent of the planned reduction was actually reflected in diesel fuel prices. If the pass-through of the VAT reduction were similar, the savings for a household consuming 100 liters would be approximately EUR 9.50 per month, while the average savings per end consumer would be approximately EUR 4.80 per month.
According to EM calculations, the share of fuel in the household consumption basket will be 4.1 percent in 2026. A 7.4 percent reduction in fuel prices, if fully passed on, would lower the consumer price level by approximately 0.3 percentage points, while with the previously measured pass-through rate, it would lower it by approximately 0.19 percentage points.
The Ministry of Economics notes that the second-round impact on the prices of other goods and services would be limited, as businesses’ pre-tax costs would remain unchanged. The impact of higher fuel prices on the prices of transportation, food, and logistics services would thus persist.
At current prices, the Latvian economy is spending approximately EUR 64 million more per month on fuel than in January and February of this year - EUR 54 million for diesel and EUR 10 million for gasoline. On an annual basis, this amounts to approximately EUR 770 million, or 1.7 percent of gross domestic product, according to the Ministry.
The Ministry of Economics explains that the majority of this increase is due to rising fuel purchase prices - approximately EUR 53 million per month. The pre-tax component of diesel prices has risen by EUR 0.56 per liter since the beginning of the year, while that of gasoline has risen by EUR 0.38 per liter. This component is determined by global market prices, and tax measures do not affect it.
The Ministry emphasizes that the VAT rate reduction represents a domestic redistribution from the state budget to end consumers. This alleviates price pressure on households but does not change the amount of cash flow that is flowing out of Latvia due to rising fuel prices.
The Ministry of Economics continues to monitor fuel prices and reports to the Cabinet of Ministers twice a month on the situation in the fuel market.
As reported, the Greens/Farmers will propose reducing the value-added tax (VAT) rate on fuel from 21 percent to 12 percent until the end of this year, to mitigate the impact of rapidly rising fuel prices on residents and the national economy.
The Greens/Farmers notes that the situation on the global oil market remains tense, while rising fuel prices in Latvia are significantly affecting the expenses of both households and businesses. The party emphasized that rising fuel prices increase costs in transportation, manufacturing, agriculture, logistics, and services, thereby contributing to overall price increases and inflation.
According to the Greens/Farmers, data from the Central Statistical Bureau show that in August, compared to July, the average consumer price for fuel in Latvia rose by 8.8 percent. Diesel prices rose by 11.5 percent, while gasoline prices increased by 2.9 percent. Over the course of the year, fuel prices have risen by 22.1 percent overall.
The Greens/Farmers believes that the excise tax reduction on diesel fuel introduced this year is not enough on its own, so additional measures are needed to alleviate price pressures. The party notes that a VAT reduction would be a temporary measure, effective until the end of the year.
Minister of Economics Viktors Valainis (Greens/Farmers), commenting on the rise in fuel prices, told LETA that the Ministry of Economics’ proposed "excess profit" or "solidarity" levy on fuel retailers should be reinstated. According to the minister, such a measure would ensure that the benefits resulting from the state’s reduced excise tax reach consumers rather than remaining in the hands of fuel retailers.
On April 14, the government endorsed the Ministry of Economics’ proposal to impose a solidarity levy on fuel retailers in cases the actual retail price exceeds the indicative retail price by more than 3 percent. The regulation was planned to remain in effect until the end of 2026, with the funds raised to be allocated toward strengthening fuel supply security.
However, business organizations and several government agencies raised objections to the bill, while fuel retailers pointed to a potential conflict with free-market principles and legal risks. Later, the draft law on the solidarity contribution for fuel retailers, prepared by the Ministry of Economics, failed to gain support in the Saeima.

Stargate secures 21 million Euros from EU Innovation Fund to further scale up its operations  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:09 UTC
22:49:09 KST

The funding will support the expansion of Stargate's manufacturing capacity in Europe, enabling the production of large-scale electrolyser stacks and systems, thereby increasing the company’s ability to supply Power-to-X projects with EU-made equipment.
TALLINN, ESTONIA, August 15th, 2026.
Stargate Hydrogen announced today that it has signed the grant agreement with The European Climate, Infrastructure and Environment Executive Agency (CINEA) for 20.6 million Euros as part of an EU Innovation Fund grant. The funds will be used for the expansion of the company's manufacturing facility in Tallinn, Estonia.
The new manufacturing plant will be developed in phases and will produce alkaline electrolyser electrodes, pressurised alkaline stacks, and electrolyser systems with an annual production capacity of 250 megawatts (MW), 150 MW, and 66 MW, respectively.
The project addresses one of the key barriers to wider green hydrogen adoption: the price of the green molecule. Stargate Hydrogen's innovations include proprietary stack design, and electrodes coated with a patented ceramic-based catalyst that replaces precious metals, reducing the reliance on imported critical raw materials while enabling highly efficient hydrogen production.
Over its first ten years of operation, the project is expected to avoid approximately 3 million tons of CO₂ equivalent emissions by supporting broader deployment of affordable green hydrogen across hard-to-abate industries, including chemicals, steel, and other energy-intensive sectors.
The grant, financed by revenues from the EU Emissions Trading System, is a major investment into the Estonian clean technology sector. The project is expected to create approximately 150 direct high-value jobs, while supporting hundreds of additional jobs across construction, logistics, maintenance, and the wider European supply chain. The new facility will also strengthen regional manufacturing capabilities for strategic clean technologies and improve the resilience of Europe's hydrogen value chain.
"This grant marks an important milestone for Stargate Hydrogen and demonstrates confidence in our technology and manufacturing strategy," said Marko Virkebau, Chief Executive Officer of Stargate Hydrogen. “With the help of the Innovation Fund, we truly step up our game. After the factory expansion is finalised, Stargate will become one of the largest electrolyser manufacturers in Europe. Such manufacturing capability is important in order for us to be able to deliver our products to large industrial electrolysis projects, such as projects delivering green ammonia, synthetic natural gas, as well as sustainable aviation fuel."
Last year, Stargate Hydrogen closed a successful Series A financing round, which included Repsol, one of the major hydrogen producers in Europe, and SmartCap, an Estonian state-owned venture capital firm, among others. The company is trusted by clients including Fortum, Utilitas, ABB, and Rockfin, among many others across Europe, Turkey, and India.
The development of advanced alkaline electrolysis technology by Stargate has been acknowledged by the European Commission as an Important Project of Common European Interest (IPCEI). The company has recently deployed a turn-key electrolysis system to Fortum in Finland and has supplied electrolysis stacks to several countries in Europe and beyond.
The Innovation Fund-backed project supports the European Union's Hydrogen Strategy and aligns with key policy initiatives, including REPowerEU, the Net-Zero Industry Act, and the Clean Industrial Deal, which consider electrolysers as strategic clean technologies for Europe's industrial future.
Disclaimer: “Funded by the European Union. Views and opinions expressed are however those of the author(s) only and do not necessarily reflect those of the European Union or the European Climate, Infrastructure and Environment Executive Agency (CINEA). Neither the European Union nor the granting authority can be held responsible for them.”
About Stargate Hydrogen
Stargate Hydrogen is a European electrolyser technology company developing industrial hydrogen production systems for infrastructure and energy resilience projects. The company deploys precious-metal-free alkaline electrolysers for energy-intensive industries transitioning away from grey hydrogen, including refining, ammonia, methanol, e-methane, and steel production. Stargate Hydrogen is backed by committed investors such as Repsol, and trusted by clients including Fortum, Utilitas, ABB, and Rockfin, among many others across Europe, Turkey, and India.
For more information, visit www.stargatehydrogen.com.

Education minister promises smoother high school admissions next year
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:49:00 UTC
22:49:00 KST

Education minister promises smoother high school admissions next year
Minister of Education and Research Kristina Kallas (Eesti 200) says high school admissions should run more smoothly next spring under a unified schedule and revamped system.
The changes follow a chaotic admissions season this year, when nearly 1,900 basic school graduates still weren't enrolled in either high school or a vocational school a week before the new school year began.
While each school will continue to set its own admission requirements, for the coming admissions cycle, high schools and vocational schools across the country will follow a common schedule. Students will apply through SAIS, Estonia's unified admissions information system, ranking their school choices by preference.
SAIS was also supposed to handle admissions this year, but the plan was scrapped in February after problems with the latest version, SAIS3. Kallas said the system has since undergone load testing, and schools can now practice entering their admission requirements ahead of time in a test environment.
"We're currently working on developing the preference system's functionality," she said, adding that the full system will open for schools in January.
Next spring, students can apply to schools from March 29 through May 18. Estonia's system will allow students to apply to any number of schools, though the minister acknowledged that unlimited choices can make the process confusing for kids.
The possibility of setting a limit, she said, warrants broader public discussion. Ranking preferences, however, will also help schools identify applicants who have placed them first.
"They can, for example, start interviews with those who listed the school as their top choice and don't have to interview everyone," Kallas said.
Chance to change application
Another change will allow students to review and adjust their preferences after receiving their basic school exam results. Exam results will be entered into the Estonian Education Information System (EHIS) on May 17, and students will have one day to revise their applications before schools begin making offers.
"We wanted to give them three days, but school principals weren't on board with that, so we're giving them one," the minister said.
Schools must begin making their admissions offers by June 11, with the main admissions round running through July 2. Kallas urged students to accept offers promptly so that enrollment does not remain tied up while other applicants wait.
"The problem this year was that very strong students received admissions offers from several schools and took a long time to accept one," she explained. While others received rejections right away, many students in between waited in limbo and uncertainty over the summer.
The minister said schools should keep rolling admissions going in July to avoid a repeat.
Admissions to end earlier
Following an interim review by July 5 to identify remaining vacancies, rolling admissions must begin no later than July 15 and will end August 23, a week earlier than this year. All students not yet accepted to a high school or vocational school will then be able to reapply for remaining spots on equal terms.
"That's a week earlier, so kids won't have to wait as long for schools to make their decisions," Kallas said.
She acknowledged the earlier cutoff may mean shorter summer vacations for teachers, but urged schools to rotate staff vacations so admissions can be completed on time. Teachers' total number of vacation days, she added, will not change.
The education minister said the longer-term goal is to automate as much of the admissions process as possible, reducing the workload on teachers. She pointed to Finland, where students can rank up to seven schools and an algorithm handles much of the matching, issuing offers automatically.
"Everything is settled within a month and teachers don't have an endless stream of lists and interviews to handle," Kallas said.
The new SAIS3 system is also intended to make rolling admissions easier for students by showing enrollment availability all in one spot.
This means that instead of having to call around to various schools individually to find out if they are still accepting more students, applicants will be able to see a consolidated list of schools that still have spots available for new students.
--
Editor: Karin Koppel, Aili Vahtla

Building new movie theaters in Latvia is possible with significant investment - Forum Cinemas  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:48:52 UTC
22:48:52 KST

RIGA - There are opportunities to open new movie theaters in Latvia, but this requires significant investment, said Dinars Ozols, head of the Latvian branch of Forum Cinemas, in an interview with LETA.
Commenting on Forum Cinemas’ prospects for expanding its operations in Latvia, Ozols noted that the Competition Council (KP) spent quite some time evaluating Forum Cinemas’ desire to become an operator at the Akropole Alfa shopping center and acknowledged that competition exists in the movie theater market. Ozols pointed out that there is room in the market. "The nature of the movie business, however, is such that starting to screen films requires fairly large investments. Therefore, opportunities exist, but they are limited," Ozols said.
Meanwhile, commenting on the Competition Council’s calculations that the combined market share of Forum Cinemas and Apollo Kino in Riga’s multiplex cinema market will reach 90 to 100 percent, Ozols pointed out that smaller movie theaters are also well regarded.
"Personally, I think 90 percent is an exaggerated estimate. If we look at it that way, then there is really only one large cinema chain in Riga, and the rest of us are competing with it. I think it will be a good thing if we can provide this largest chain with some worthy competition," Ozols emphasized.
At the same time, he noted that a new movie theater opened in Daugavpils in August and that there is also potential in the regions. In his view, people need to be encouraged to watch movies in theaters. In Riga, on the other hand, there are many underserved neighborhoods that may hold potential.
Ozols noted that convenience is often an important factor for moviegoers - namely, the ability to easily get to the movie theater or combine a trip to the movies with another activity. In his view, this is one of the reasons why movie theaters in shopping centers are so successful. Therefore, when considering new movie theaters, it is very important to assess audience habits. Ozols pointed out that shopping centers are potentially good locations for movie theaters.
At the same time, Ozols mentioned that cinema operators must pay rent to the building owner, so it is very difficult - if not nearly impossible - to stay afloat by screening movies alone. He noted that it is necessary to consider additional services and products that moviegoers are willing to pay for. This includes food and beverages, movie merchandise, and other offerings. Ozols explained that the main source of revenue is ticket sales, but the proceeds from these must also be shared with film distributors. Therefore, movie theaters must explore additional sources of revenue to ensure a positive bottom line.
Commenting on the company’s revenue from movie tickets and the sale of other products, Ozols said he would not disclose specific figures, as they can vary depending on the film.
"For example, when we screened the movie Minecraft, you could say that the young people ate all the popcorn we had. On the other hand, there are viewers who come to see, for example, Latvian films and believe that a movie is meant to be watched without snacking during the screening. Of course, these are extremes, and there is a middle ground, but it is hard for me to give a specific figure," he noted.
At the same time, Ozols noted that, overall, the share of revenue from these additional products and services is large enough that it would be difficult for a movie theater to operate without them.

European Parliament: Foreign interference is an attack on all Europeans  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:48:48 UTC
22:48:48 KST

TALLINN - The European Parliament has adopted a report drafted by the Special Committee on the Defence of European Democracy, offering specific recommendations on how to better protect the EU's democratic institutions. The report emphasizes the importance of combating fragmentation within the EU, as member states cannot counter such threats alone.
The special committee was established in December 2024 to assess the escalation and impact of hostile foreign activities that harm the EU, such as foreign information manipulation, interference, hybrid attacks, and coordinated disinformation campaigns.
The report highlights Russia's frequent hybrid attacks on critical infrastructure. These include cyberattacks, physical sabotage, arson, espionage, and signal jamming. Some of these activities also originate from Belarus, China, Iran, and North Korea. The Parliament recommends sanctioning both those who enable disinformation and those who assist in circumventing sanctions. Additionally, the report condemns Russia's political decision to create a blacklist of European officials, journalists, and Members of the European Parliament as part of its hybrid warfare.
As the attacks largely occur in the digital sphere, Parliament calls for the strict enforcement of existing digital regulations, such as the Digital Services Act and the AI Act, and for increased platform accountability. Platforms must be required to take greater action to protect democratic discourse and prevent election interference by bots. The report notes that freedom of speech and information should protect people, not machines.
The Parliament strongly urges the EU to reduce its dependence on foreign technology and hardware in critical sectors, particularly from the US and China, and to prioritize European products. Furthermore, critical raw materials should only be sourced from trusted partners.
To protect the integrity of elections and prevent funding and interference by proxies, the Parliament deems it necessary to classify election infrastructure as critical. For cryptocurrency-based political donations, a 'know your donor' principle must be followed. The report also calls for stronger action against malicious deepfakes and fraudulent advertising, as well as increased protection for female candidates.
Close cooperation and support for candidate and partner countries in enhancing their resilience are crucial. However, the exploitation of migration flows to exert political pressure on the European Union is entirely unacceptable.
A strong and independent civil society, whose members are media literate and understand the risks of artificial intelligence, is of central importance. In the long term, the EU should invest more in protecting media freedom, investigative journalism, and civic activism, and in combating the use of illegal spyware.
A strong democracy means that society can function effectively even in crisis situations. To achieve this, extensive regular drills should be conducted and an EU-wide crisis alert application should be created. The Parliament also proposes that February 24, the anniversary of Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine, be designated as an annual European Crisis Preparedness Day.
The Parliament supports the establishment of a European Centre for Democratic Resilience, which would serve as an independent center of excellence to combat foreign information manipulation, interference, and disinformation, and to coordinate member states' actions. The center should not duplicate existing efforts but instead consolidate EU-level measures already underway into a cohesive whole. It must have a clear vision, mandate, budget, and operational capacity. All member states should be required to participate in the new agency's work, with oversight provided by the Parliament.
In the plenary vote, the report was adopted with 420 votes in favor, 220 against, and 26 abstentions.
The report's author, Tomas Tobé (EPP, Sweden), said before the plenary that this report does not regulate political opinions but protects democracy from foreign influence. "Freedom of speech does not mean allowing propaganda from Russian chatbots or foreign interference. And freedom of speech most certainly does not mean that foreign countries can interfere in European elections."

====================================================================================================

Judge blocks Trump limits on how long foreign students and journalists can stay in US
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:48:35 UTC
22:48:35 KST

A federal judge on Monday blocked the Trump administration from implementing a new rule that would limit how long foreign students and journalists can remain in the US without applying for extensions.
F Dennis Saylor, the US district judge in Boston, ruled in favor of a coalition of unions and higher education advocacy groups a day before the federal Department of Homeland Security (DHS)’s rule was set to take effect.
Saylor said DHS adopted the policy based on “exceptionally weak” rationales. The agency had cited national security and the need to prevent fraud in the visa program, but the judge found it had not adhered to its legal obligations to engage with concerns about a policy change or consider less burdensome alternatives.
In a statement, James Percival, the DHS general counsel, criticized Saylor’s ruling, saying that under it US Immigration and Customs Enforcement (ICE) must “permit this rampant abuse of our immigration system”.
“Come on a student visa, take one class per semester, and stay here for decades,” he said.
Saylor, an appointee of George W Bush, wrote that the rule upended a system through which the United States for nearly five decades had issued visas to foreign students for their “duration of status”.
That system, he said, has permitted tens of millions of foreign students and researchers to come to the US, leading to “groundbreaking research in science, medicine, and technology, substantial economic growth, and a host of other benefits, often on a vast scale”.
Saylor said the rule DHS adopted in July seeks to replace that system with one that would substantially restrict the overall number of foreign students, professors and journalists in the US.
Under the rule, F visas for international students and J visas that allow visitors on cultural exchange programs to work in the US would be capped at four years, while I visas for journalists, which now can last years, would be up to 240 days.
About 1.6 million people currently have F visas and another 500,000 have J visas. Major research universities like Massachusetts Institute of Technology (MIT) and Harvard have large percentages of foreign students, particularly at the graduate level, the judge noted.
If the rule took effect, such universities would probably suffer hundreds of millions of dollars in costs and enrollment would decline, Saylor said.
“The damage to the higher education system and to the economy of the United States is likely to be catastrophic,” Saylor wrote.
Miriam Feldblum, the head of one of the plaintiffs, the Presidents’ Alliance on Higher Education and Immigration, said the judge’s ruling recognized the harm the rule would cause to international students, universities and the economy if it was allowed to take effect.
“While the case continues, this relief preserves the longstanding system that enables our colleges and universities – and our country – to attract, educate, and retain global talent,” Feldblum said.

Chronicler of the Unseen: Vivian Maier retrospective to open at Fotografiska Tallinn  - The Baltic Times
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:48:30 UTC
22:48:30 KST

This week, Fotografiska Tallinn opens its major exhibition of the season, Vivian Maier: Unseen Work, arriving in the Baltic and Nordic region for the first time following successful runs at Fotografiska New York and Shanghai. The French-American photographer Vivian Maier remained unknown during her lifetime, but the chance discovery of her photographic archive brought her extraordinary talent to the world and posthumously established her among the great names of 20th-century photography.
Vivian Maier’s (1926–2009) story is one of the most remarkable in the history of photography. During nearly 40 years working as a nanny, she walked the streets of New York and Chicago with a camera around her neck, often accompanied by the children in her care. At the height of the American Dream, Maier belonged to an invisible working class and remained a quiet witness to the world around her. Where others saw ordinary daily life, Maier saw an endless source of images, at times through a child’s eyes: colourful characters, fleeting encounters, strange coincidences and the tragicomic moments of everyday life. A quiet observer, she explored and documented post-war America from the 1950s to the late 1980s with an extraordinary instinct, creating a unique visual portrait of the era, with life itself in the leading role.
Maier documented in detail the urban fabric of North America’s metropolises and the sociopolitical changes taking place around her, but she was equally drawn to the margins of everyday life: people who had stepped out of the constant flow of the streets and remained at its edges, waiting, watching or simply dozing. Her visual language brings together humanist photography and the American street photography tradition, interspersed with striking self-portraits in which Maier herself occasionally enters the frame. Colour and film became part of her later work – she experimented with movement and kinetic sequences, bringing elements of cinematic language into her photographic work while also experimenting with moving images.
Maier’s vast archive, comprising more than 120,000 negatives as well as her own documentary films and audio recordings, only came to light after her death. Its chance discovery revealed both the extraordinary scale of her work and the strength of her talent. The unknown nanny became an international phenomenon posthumously, and Maier is now regarded as one of the most important street photographers of the 20th century, alongside major figures such as Henri Cartier-Bresson, Diane Arbus and Robert Frank.
The remarkable discovery of Maier’s body of work is also the subject of the acclaimed documentary Finding Vivian Maier (2013), which traces the chance discovery of her negatives and offers a glimpse into the photographer’s enigmatic character, pieced together largely through speculation and from the perspectives of those around her.
“Vivian Maier’s story is extraordinary, but what matters even more is how powerfully her photographs speak even without knowing that story,” says Maarja Loorents, Head of Exhibitions and Co-Founder of Fotografiska Tallinn. “The exhibition offers an excellent overview of the work of one of the most gifted street photographers of the last century, from her iconic images of the streets of Chicago and New York to her more abstract work and colour photography.” Loorents adds that perhaps the greatest value of Maier’s work today lies precisely in its humanity: “The world around us is full of stories, if only we take the time to notice them.”
The exhibition is a co-production between the international art platform diChroma photography and Fotografiska, in collaboration with the John Maloof Collection, Chicago, and Howard Greenberg Gallery, NY. It is curated by Anne Morin, Director of diChroma photography and one of the leading researchers and advocates of Vivian Maier’s legacy. The curator responsible for the exhibition at Fotografiska Tallinn is Maarja Loorents.
The exhibition’s opening weekend begins on Friday with a focus on the theatre of everyday life – Maier’s world, where everyone unknowingly plays a part and can become the main character, even for a fraction of a second. At the opening event on 18 September at 20:00, a gallery of characters will come to life, embodied by actors Eliis-Maria Koit, Laura Kukk, Hardi Möller and Taavi Tõnisson. During the evening, curator Anne Morin will also lead a tour of the exhibition. From Saturday, 19 September, the exhibition settles into Fotografiska’s regular opening hours, and Anne Morin will lead a separate curator’s tour in English at 13:00.
Vivian Maier’s world will remain open at Fotografiska Tallinn until 17 January. The exhibition is supported by Women In Motion, founded by the Kering Group in 2015 to highlight inequalities in the field of culture and the arts and to change perceptions.
                    2026 © The Baltic Times /Cookies Policy Privacy Policy

Expanding authority, strengthening private sector can pave way for increased investment in free zone
Unknown

2026-09-15

22:48:21 KST

Expanding authority, strengthening private sector can pave way for increased investment in free zone
TEHRAN- The Secretary of the Supreme Council of Free and Special Economic Zones, emphasizing the capacity of free and special economic zones to strengthen the country’s economy, said: Increasing the authority of free zones and making broader use of the capacity and experience of the private sector can provide the basis for attracting investment, developing economic activities, and making more effective use of these zones’ capacities.
According to IRNA, citing the Secretariat of the Supreme Council of Free and Special Economic Zones, Hossein Forouzan said at a meeting of the deputies of the Secretariat and the CEOs of organizations of free and special economic zones, attended by Mohammad Jafar Ghaempanah, Executive Deputy of the President: The enemies expected Iran, like some other countries, to surrender to pressures, but the bravery, planning, and measures taken prevented this goal.
Forouzan added: Today, economic war is the issue, and in these conditions we must place greater trust in free zones. We ask the government to give free zones enough time and authority, and in return for these authorities, it should be confident that the managers of free zones will strive to achieve the set goals and will not disappoint the government.
He continued: We will work with all our strength and lovingly for the development of free zones and the country’s economy, and we believe that if more authority is given to free zones, existing capacities can be used to pass through difficult economic conditions.
The Secretary of the Supreme Council of Free and Special Economic Zones proposed that, if possible, the private sector’s share in the decision-making structure related to free zones be increased.
Forouzan said: I propose that, if such a possibility exists, in the five-member decision-making composition, at least two members be from the private sector, because private-sector actors can greatly help attract investment and solve economic problems.
He added: Instead of government managers seeking to attract investors, the capacity of private-sector actors should be used. The private sector has the experience, connections, and motivation needed to attract investment and can play a more effective role in this regard.
The Secretary of the Supreme Council of Free and Special Economic Zones, emphasizing the capacity of special economic zones, stated: Special economic zones are an important opportunity for the country, and this capacity must be used in the current economic conditions.
Forouzan, criticizing some restrictions created in the path of activity of special economic zones, said: Unfortunately, every day we face new issues in various areas, including land and banking issues, which make the activity process of these zones difficult.
He continued: The Central Bank, in some cases, instead of helping special economic zones use their capacities, has created restrictions, and even at times the cutting of resources of special economic zones has been raised.
The Secretary of the Supreme Council of Free and Special Economic Zones at the end called for the government to provide more opportunity for the activity of free and special economic zones and said: At least give us this opportunity that for a specified period we can use the capacities of free zones, so that it becomes clear to what extent these zones can play a role in the country’s economy.
Forouzan at the end emphasized: Our expectation is that the government trusts free zones and, in return for the authorities it gives them, evaluates their performance. We assure that we will use this trust for the development of the country and will try not to disappoint the government and the blood of the martyrs.
In continuation of this meeting, Morteza Safdarizadeh, CEO of Birjand and Sarv Urmia Special Economic Zone? referring to activities carried out in the Birjand and Sarv Urmia special economic zones, stated: Private-sector activity in these zones has provided employment for about 7,500 people in various industries, and this shows that there are considerable capacities for investment and employment in special economic zones.
Safdarizadeh, appreciating the government and the country’s officials for measures taken in the recent difficult conditions, added: At this time, when the country faced foreign pressures and special conditions, the government and officials tried to bring the country out of this bottleneck, and we hope that after passing through this passage, the position of Islamic Iran in the international arena will be strengthened more than before.
He further, referring to the issue of the incomplete implementation of Article 119 of the Development Plan Law, said: In this law, emphasis has been placed on elevating the position of the Secretariat of the Supreme Council of Free and Special Economic Zones, but the necessary mechanism for realizing this issue has not been fully implemented.
The CEO of the Birjand and Sarv Urmia Special Economic Zone continued: The position of the Secretariat of the Supreme Council of Free Zones has undergone changes at different times; in the past, the Secretary of the Supreme Council of Free Zones was at the position of deputy and advisor to the President, but later this body was defined under the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Finance.
Safdarizadeh, emphasizing the need for an expert review of the position of the Secretariat of the Supreme Council of Free and Special Economic Zones, stated: It must be examined to what extent these changes have been in the interest and expediency of the country. If a decision is to be made about the position of the Secretariat, its effects and consequences must be precisely examined.
Referring to the capacity of the country’s free and special economic zones, he stated: Free and special economic zones can be the best passage and path for solving part of the problems the country faces today, and the capacities of these zones must be used for the development of trade, investment, and relations with neighboring countries.
The CEO of the Birjand and Sarv Urmia Special Economic Zone, referring to the performance of the South Khorasan Special Economic Zone at the Iran-Afghanistan border, said: According to statistics, more than 55 percent of goods exported through Iran to Afghanistan or transited from the country toward Afghanistan pass through this route.
Safdarizadeh added: In this zone, we tried to create the needed infrastructure and facilities and, by governing the private sector’s view and management, direct the attention of Iranian and Afghan traders to this border.
Comparing this border with older borders of the country, he said: Compared with borders such as Dogharoun, which have several decades of activity, this border in a shorter time has been able to find its place and become one of the important routes of trade with Afghanistan.
The CEO of the Birjand and Sarv Urmia Special Economic Zone noted: Given the boom created at this border, the government also entered to complete road infrastructure and took necessary measures in this regard. On this path, the Afghan side also cooperated appropriately, and Afghan authorities were on board for the development of trade and border interactions.
Safdarizadeh, emphasizing the necessity of entrusting economic affairs to the private sector, said: Our view is that if we prepare a suitable ground, the private sector can carry out many affairs with more speed and productivity. Experience has shown that wherever the private sector has entered, in addition to creating benefit for itself, it has brought more benefits for the country.
He added: This model can be implemented in various sectors of the country’s economy. Today Iran has export relations with about 40 countries, and this shows the country is full of economic and trade opportunities.
