Drone Strikes Force Half of Russia’s Top Diesel Refineries to Cut Output
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:27:08 UTC
22:27:08 KST

Half of Russia’s six largest diesel-producing refineries have been forced to significantly reduce or halt production this month after sustaining damage in Ukrainian drone attacks, according to Reuters calculations based on data from fuel market participants.
The disruption comes as Ukrainian strikes on Russia’s energy infrastructure continue to hit fuel production, prompting Moscow to restrict exports of gasoline, diesel and jet fuel.
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
US President Donald Trump on Sunday called on Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky to stop targeting Russian diesel infrastructure, saying the strikes were causing a fuel shortage that was “hurting the world” – a concern he has pinned on Ukrainian attacks on Russian refineries, rather than his own war in Iran.
Ukraine says Russian refineries are legitimate military targets.
Six refineries – Omsk, Kirishi, Taneco, Volgograd, NORSI and Perm – account for about half of Russia’s diesel production.
Kirishi has been fully shut down, while the Volgograd and NORSI refineries are operating at about one-quarter of their nameplate capacity.
Ukrainian strikes cripple Russian refining
Ukrainian drones have struck Russian oil refineries at least 70 times since the beginning of 2026, pushing Russia’s refining output to its lowest level in two decades.
By the end of August, gasoline production covered only around 70% of domestic demand, leaving a shortfall of roughly 30,000 tons per day.
Drone Attacks in the Ukrainian-Polish Border Region
At least 17 Russian regions have introduced restrictions on fuel sales, including limits of 30-40 liters per vehicle and “odd-even” systems based on license-plate numbers.
At least seven Russian refineries fully or partially halted production in August following drone attacks.
On Sept. 2, the KINEF refinery in Leningrad Oblast – Russia’s second-largest refinery by capacity – completely stopped processing.
Taneco was also hit on Sunday, although the full impact of the attack has not yet been assessed.
The disruptions have contributed to Moscow’s decision to restrict fuel exports. Before the restrictions, Russia exported less than 1 million metric tons of diesel in June, compared with around 2.5 million tons a year earlier. Including lower-quality gasoil, monthly exports had previously reached 3.3-3.4 million tons.
Turkey and Brazil were among the largest buyers, taking at least half of the available cargoes for several months before the restrictions were introduced.
The International Energy Agency said a Russian refinery was successfully hit, on average, once every three days during the first eight months of 2026.
The pressure on fuel markets is also being felt outside Russia. The US national average diesel price rose above $6 per gallon on Sept. 10 for the first time, according to GasBuddy.
Diesel is widely used to power trucks, trains, ships and agricultural machinery, making disruptions to supply particularly significant for transport and farming.

Opposition parties dismiss king’s speech as road to stagnation
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:27:07 UTC
22:27:07 KST

Opposition parties dismiss king’s speech as road to stagnation
See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results
Opposition parties have accused the Dutch government of failing to make choices and leading the country into a “stagnation” following a king’s speech packed with appeals to the Dutch traditions of compromise and co-operation.
In a long and wide-ranging speech, King Willem-Alexander said “communality” could be an “antidote to polarisation” and said solutions needed to be found on issues such as nitrogen pollution, the housing crisis and social unrest around asylum.
But opposition parties said the cabinet had failed to set out a clear vision or take the initiative in coming forward with ideas to fix the problems.
“Politics is about making choices,” said Joost Eerdmans, leader of the right-wing conservative JA21. “The king can say democracy isn’t 50% plus one, but that’s how it works in practice. They’ve forgotten to turn left or right, or else they can’t.”
Jesse Klaver, leader of the largest left-wing opposition party Pro, said the king’s “fine words” could not disguise the fact “that the cabinet is rudderless.”
“We are in a permanent process of forming the government because the minority government can’t agree on which way to take the country in.”
Lack of direction
In the weeks before the opening day of parliament the three coalition parties D66, CDA and VVD were unable to agree what concessions to make to opposition parties in the budget deal that finance minister Eelco Heinen will deliver on Tuesday.
Instead the minority government will have to keep negotiating with opposition leaders to find the 10 seats it needs in the lower house and the 16 seats in the Senate to pass its laws.
Mirjam Bikker, leader of the ChristenUnie, said the king’s speech showed a lack of direction from the coalition parties.
“If you want to make progress, you have to decide which way to go,” she said. “We’ve seen a few concessions on minor things like cuts to healthcare, but my concern is that without a sense of direction it’ll just be a few trinkets.”
Geert Wilders of the far-right PVV criticised the speech for failing to address people’s concerns about fundamental issues such as the rising cost of living.
”Lots of money is being spent on Europe, development aid, nitrogen and the climate, while we see the country is changing and we can’t pay the bills,” he said. ”The sooner this cabinet falls, the better.”

Russian Foreign Minister Lavrov Claims US at Fault for Kremlin Peace Failure
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:25:20 UTC
22:25:20 KST

Russian Foreign Minister Sergey Lavrov claimed Monday that the war in Ukraine could have ended a year ago if the United States and Europe had not abandoned what he described as agreements reached at a 2025 summit in Alaska.
“If the Americans had not backed away from the Anchorage agreements, if Europe had not derailed those agreements, we would have been living without war for a year already,” Lavrov said during a roundtable discussion on Russia’s war against Ukraine.
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
“That is an obvious fact, so draw your own conclusions,” Lavrov said, according to Russian state news agencies TASS and RIA Novosti and the Russian outlet RBC.
However, no peace agreement was announced after US President Donald Trump met Russian leader Vladimir Putin in Anchorage on Aug. 15, 2025. Trump left the summit without securing a ceasefire or an agreement to end the war. Ukraine did not participate in the talks. Additionally, agreements by US presidents are not official, binding government policy unless ratified as a treaty by the US Senate.
Secretary of State Marco Rubio said there had been proposals and discussions in Alaska but no formal agreement.
Lavrov nevertheless accused Washington and European governments of prolonging the war and repeated Moscow’s claim that Russia had never rejected negotiations.
“We have never refused talks. And all the negotiations that took place were interrupted not at our initiative. Therefore, if our colleagues are ready, I think they can certainly be resumed. We have something to say.”
Kremlin Calls Trump’s Energy Truce ‘Good Idea’
Lavrov said Moscow was open to both bilateral and trilateral negotiations. At the same time, he said Russia would not suspend its military operations against Ukraine while talks were underway.
Russia launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022 and continues to occupy parts of the country. Moscow has repeatedly rejected calls for an unconditional ceasefire while demanding Ukrainian territorial concessions and restrictions on Kyiv’s military and foreign policy.
Lavrov claimed Russia had not altered its position since the beginning of the invasion and remained prepared to seek what he called “reasonable compromises.”
“We are ready, within the framework of this position, to seek reasonable compromises,” Lavrov said.
He added that Moscow would not abandon its demands to prevent NATO from expanding to Russia’s borders and to guarantee what he described as the rights of Russian and Russian-speaking residents of Ukraine.
Lavrov also repeated Kremlin claims that Russia was seeking the “denazification” and “demilitarization” of Ukraine. Moscow has used those terms to justify its invasion, though Ukraine is a democracy whose president, Volodymyr Zelensky, is Jewish.
The Russian foreign minister’s claim that Moscow has consistently supported negotiations contrasts with its refusal to stop fighting during diplomatic talks.
The Anchorage summit was promoted as an opportunity to advance efforts to end the conflict. Putin and Trump described the meeting as productive, but neither announced a ceasefire, peace framework or binding agreement afterward.

====================================================================================================

Pre-war status quo in Hormuz must be restored as soon as possible: Türkiye's Fidan
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:24:54 UTC
22:24:54 KST

Türkiye's Foreign Minister Hakan Fidan has called for safe, uninterrupted, and free passage through the Strait of Hormuz and a swift return to the pre-war status quo, while warning against the further spread of the Russia-Ukraine war.
Speaking at a joint news conference with his Azerbaijani counterpart Jeyhun Bayramov in Baku on Tuesday, Fidan said developments in neighbouring Iran directly concern both Türkiye and Azerbaijan.
“Iran’s security, stability, and prosperity naturally have a direct impact on our region. In this regard, we want the current conflict to be resolved through diplomatic means as soon as possible,” he said.
Fidan said increasing reciprocal attacks have seriously undermined peace efforts, and called for an immediate end to actions fueling regional tensions.
“At the same time, safe, uninterrupted and free passage through the Strait of Hormuz must be allowed, and the pre-war status quo must be restored as soon as possible,” he said, adding that Türkiye remains in continuous contact with all relevant parties to help resolve the crisis.
Turning to the Russia-Ukraine war, Fidan said Türkiye has made major efforts since the beginning of the conflict to keep the Black Sea out of the fighting.
He said Ankara had largely succeeded in doing so through its strict implementation of the Montreux Convention and efforts such as the Black Sea Grain Initiative.
“However, increasing attacks on civilian vessels in the Black Sea in recent months have seriously harmed the safety of life, property, navigation, and the environment in the region,” Fidan said.
Noting that Turkish and Azerbaijani sailors had also lost their lives in the attacks, he said: “The spread of the war into the Black Sea and the threat it poses to the lives of our citizens and the activities of our companies is unacceptable.”
Fidan said Türkiye has conveyed the necessary warnings to the parties and believes common ground could be found if there is sufficient political will and good faith.
“We will continue to make every effort for peace,” he said, recalling President Recep Tayyip Erdogan’s position that “even the worst peace is better than war itself.”
Risk of wider war
Responding to questions, Fidan warned that the Russia-Ukraine conflict has intensified significantly over the past year and carries the risk of spreading beyond its current geography.
He said Türkiye’s position has remained unchanged throughout the more than four-and-a-half-year war, arguing that it was “a war that should never have started, should not spread, and should end immediately.”
Fidan pointed to Türkiye’s diplomatic efforts, including its role in the Black Sea grain deal, prisoner exchanges, and talks between Russia and Ukraine in Istanbul.
He said Ankara is also working with the parties on proposals aimed at preventing attacks on energy infrastructure and improving security in the Black Sea.
Fidan described the conflict as having transformed over the past year from a “war of attrition” into a “war of destruction,” with commercial facilities and infrastructure increasingly being targeted and civilian casualties rising.
“From time to time, there are statements about the possibility of the war spreading not only in the Black Sea but also to other European countries,” he said.
"We do not even want to think about it, let alone talk about it. The geographical expansion of the war is the last thing we want to see.”
Fidan warned that unless the war ends, energy prices, food security, and the environment will face greater risks, while the danger of the conflict spreading will increase.
Türkiye-Azerbaijan ties, regional connectivity
Fidan also highlighted the strategic relationship between Türkiye and Azerbaijan, saying the two countries remain bound by the principle of “one nation, two states.”
He said economic and commercial cooperation, as well as energy, transportation, and connectivity, constitute key pillars of bilateral ties.
Fidan described the Baku-Tbilisi-Kars railway as one of the most important components of the Middle Corridor and stressed the importance of ensuring its effective use.
He also highlighted plans to strengthen connectivity between Türkiye and Azerbaijan via Nakhchivan, including the Zangezur Corridor and the Kars-Igdir-Aralik-Dilucu railway.
“God willing, the Kars-Dilucu railway will be completed toward the end of 2029. Thus, a train coming from Azerbaijan will be able to reach Türkiye without interruption,” he said.
Fidan said such infrastructure would provide a significant logistical opportunity and strengthen the Turkic world’s access to Anatolia and Europe, while facilitating Türkiye’s access to Central Asia across the Caspian Sea.
‘Constructive approach’
On the South Caucasus, Fidan said Türkiye welcomed recent progress toward a final peace agreement between Azerbaijan and Armenia and Baku’s “constructive approach.”
“We want the South Caucasus to be associated not with conflicts, but with peace, transportation, energy, trade, and economic development,” he said.
Fidan also said Türkiye and Azerbaijan would continue deepening defence and security cooperation through joint exercises, training, experience-sharing, and growing cooperation between their defence industries.
He added that the Organization of Turkic States foreign ministers would meet in Ankara on October 29, followed by the organization’s 13th summit on October 30.
“We see this summit as a historic step that will consolidate the Turkic world’s common vision for the future,” he said.

‘Last Breath’: The campaign giving Iran’s condemned a face and a name
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:24:24 UTC
22:24:24 KST

For Iranians, the disappearance of mass demonstrations from the streets does not mean the end of the regime’s crackdown. Far from it.
Since the turn of the year, more than 950 people have been executed in Iran, including at least 29 people arrested in connection with January’s protests, which left thousands dead across the country. Months later, scores of those detained remain at risk of execution.
Now, a new campaign is spreading among Iranian opposition activists.
Known as “Last Breath” (akharin nafas in Persian), the campaign has begun appearing across Iranian social media over the past several days, and its premise is relatively straightforward: take the name and photograph of somebody facing execution, tell their story and push it as widely as possible among politicians, journalists, human rights organizations and the wider public.
It puts a face to each name and allows individual stories to be told, rather than allowing those condemned to become merely another statistic.
“They breathe behind prison walls, not knowing which sunrise will be their last,” read one Persian-language appeal circulated under the campaign. It urged supporters to call, send emails, publish prisoners' photographs and names, and demand action from those contacted.
Another of the campaign's early posts contained an expanding list of people said to be facing execution, including Ehsan Hosseinipour, Matin Mohammadi, Erfan Amiri, Hamidreza Fathi, Abdolreza Fathi, Saeed Zarei Kordshouli, Peyman Ganji, Erfan Khalili, Ali-Akbar Mahlouji and others.
“These are not just names,” the appeal said. “They are lives in imminent danger.”
Campaign spreads with posts in English, French, German
The campaign has already moved beyond Persian, with posts carrying the Last Breath hashtag appearing in English, French and German, suggesting that participants are trying to take the cases beyond the mobilized Iranian diaspora and into the wider international conversation.
One of the central images of the Last Breath campaign is that of sunrise.
For most, dawn represents the beginning of another day. In Iran's prisons, however, the early morning has long carried the association with executions, which are frequently carried out in the Islamic Republic at or around dawn. Iranian human rights organizations routinely record executions taking place in the early hours, while two protesters, Abolfazl Sepahi Badjani and Amir Hossein Safari Hosseinabadi, were publicly hanged at dawn in Isfahan's Alikhani Square on July 28.
That contrast - between sunrise as a symbol of hope and the executions carried out by the Islamic Republic – lies at the heart of the campaign.
“Sunrise should bring a new day, not a final breath,” one campaign appeal said of Alireza Sepahi and Mahdi Zarif, both facing execution.
Sepahi was arrested in connection with the January 8 protests at Alikhani Square in Isfahan and sentenced to death alongside a group of other defendants. Five of those co-defendants have already been executed. Sepahi himself came extraordinarily close. According to human rights organizations, he suffered a heart attack shortly before his scheduled public execution and was rushed to hospital. He was later returned to prison.
On September 12, Iran Human Rights reported that Sepahi had been moved from the general ward of Isfahan Central Prison to solitary confinement, where prisoners condemned to death are frequently transferred before execution. The organization said he was consequently at imminent risk.
Another whose name has become prominent in the campaign is Benyamin Naghdi, a 26-year-old kickboxer and mixed martial arts athlete arrested during the January protests in Shiraz.
“Medals belong around Benyamin Naghdi's neck, not a noose,” one Last Breath appeal said.
Iran Human Rights reported in June that Naghdi, then 26, had been arrested on January 3 during a protest in Shiraz and sentenced to death by Branch One of the city's Revolutionary Court after being convicted of “corruption on earth.”
The organization said Iranian state media broadcast what it described as a forced confession only a day after his arrest.
Campaign aims to internationalize individual cases
His story also brings to the fore the campaign's attempt to internationalize individual cases.
Iranian-French chess grandmaster Mitra Hejazipour used the Last Breath hashtag in a French-language appeal describing Naghdi as a kickboxing champion who “must live,” tying his case once again to the campaign's recurring image of sunrise and execution.
Perhaps the most immediately personal story circulating through Last Breath is that of Taraneh Rahimi, a 20-year-old woman who has been sentenced to death following her arrest after the January protests.
Rahimi and her twin sister, Romina, were arrested after participating in demonstrations in Isfahan.
According to reporting based on accounts from their family, both women were subjected to severe abuse in detention. Their US-based cousin said their mother barely recognized them when she was eventually allowed to see them in prison. Taraneh has been sentenced to death, while Romina received a 25-year prison sentence.
In a French-language post circulated thousands of times, Hejazipour in particular highlighted the person behind the name: Taraneh is 20, she rides horses, and while most people of that age might think about their next competition or future plans, she is waiting under a death sentence.
Last Breath’s strength lies in simplicity - one does not need to be an Iran expert, understand the complexities of the revolutionary courts or opposition politics to comprehend that the person you see in front of you is at imminent risk of execution.
It makes the victims difficult to ignore, which is the point.
That can already be seen in the multilingual posts circulating online. Taraneh Rahimi becomes the 20-year-old who loves horses. Benyamin Naghdi becomes the fighter whose medals, campaigners say, should hang around his neck instead of a noose. Alireza Sepahi becomes the man who has already suffered a heart attack once at the foot of the gallows.
In a country where information from prisons can be difficult to verify, the legal details remain important, but the campaign is built on the calculation that statistics eventually lose their power to shock.
Hundreds of executions can become a generality, while the life story of a 20-year-old woman waiting to learn whether she will be hanged is harder to ignore. For the prisoners whose names and photographs are now being circulated around the world, every sunrise means another day survived, and, campaigners fear, one of them may be the last.

====================================================================================================

When journalism crosses the line from reporting to mind reading - opinion
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:24:06 UTC
22:24:06 KST

“I, God, probe the heart, search the mind” (Jeremiah 17:10) is a resounding theme in Rosh Hashanah and Yom Kippur liturgy, underscoring God’s unique access to man’s inner thoughts and true intentions.
Some journalists behave as if they share God’s privileged insight. They seemingly fancy themselves in possession of the divine ability to see beyond pronouncements and actions, and interpret the private reflections and genuine desires of leaders.
The very human discipline of ethical journalism, authors Bill Kovach and Tom Rosenstiel explain in The Elements of Journalism, rests on transparency and reporters’ humility about the limits of verified facts and their own knowledge.
In coverage of Israel’s conflict with its neighbors, leading news agencies have repeatedly acted with arrogance, reporting as fact their personal understandings (either correct or not) about leaders’ inner wishes.
By playing God, they defy journalism’s commitment to the hard work of objective news reporting, which values verification and discourages editorializing, or the insertion of the reporters’ own views.
Thus, a gratuitously inflammatory September 7 Associated Press heading declared: “Muslim nations condemn Israeli proposal to empty Gaza.”
The text went on to explain that “Any Israeli plan or action to remove Palestinians from Gaza would violate international law and undermine the US-backed plan for drawing down the war, the foreign ministers of Egypt, Saudi Arabia, Jordan, and five other governments said in a statement.”
In fact, there is no “Israeli proposal to empty Gaza” or to “remove” Palestinians from Gaza. Israel’s National Security Minister Itamar Ben-Gvir and Defense Minister Israel Katz have separately floated plans – their own and not the government’s – to enable Palestinians who want to emigrate to do just that.
Therefore, the characterization of Katz’s statement as “Israel is prepared to facilitate the removal of Palestinians” is likewise problematic. That language – “removal” – suggests forcible expulsion, and not emigration by choice.
As reported by Ynet, Katz said: “There is no real solution for Gaza in the end. The Migration Directorate is ready to do this by sea, by air, and by any means. Egypt is not willing for it to go through its territory.”
Ynet further reported that Katz said, “80% want to emigrate. We are constantly checking this in surveys. Hamas does not allow it, and there are no countries in the world willing to take them in. What is delaying this is that the countries willing to take them in want US support.”
According to a May 2026 Jerusalem Post report, the Coordinator of Government Activities in the Territories (COGAT) shared with senior Israeli officials its survey finding that 80% of Gaza Strip residents were “interested in receiving information about mechanisms for relocating to a third country through the Rafah and Kerem Shalom crossings.”
Reasonable observers can disagree as to whether Katz and Ben-Gvir are genuine about the expressed desire to facilitate “voluntary emigration,” or whether in their hearts they secretly long for forced removal. But unless AP has ditched news reporting for mind-reading, it’s inappropriate to frame speculation about a plan to “empty Gaza” as fact.
The sin of arrogance
THE AP ISN’T the only news agency guilty of the sin of arrogance, assuming the godly role of prober of hearts. In June 2025, Reuters, too, departed from its declared mandate to provide “unbiased and reliable news,” with an unexpected detour into dream deciphering.
Reuters’ Crispian Balmer wrote, “Fast forward five weeks and the United States has bombed Iran’s main nuclear installations, fulfilling a decades-old dream of [Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin] Netanyahu to convince Washington to bring its full military might to thwart Tehran’s atomic ambitions.”
As media watchdog CAMERA asked at the time: “How does a news reporter purport to know Prime Minister Netanyahu’s dreams? While we can’t know Netanyahu’s dreams, we do know his statements concerning US action on Tehran’s nuclear ambitions. And they all point towards encouraging the US to lock into a stricter deal with Iran and tougher sanctions, not war.”
Reuters’ own coverage over the years documented that Netanyahu repeatedly pushed for a better deal, not a US war with Iran. For instance, Reuters reporting in 2012 cited “urgency to [Netanyahu’s] demand that [p]resident Barack Obama set a ‘red line’ for Tehran,” and noted “Netanyahu’s sharpened demands for a tougher US line against Iran.”
In 2013, Reuters accurately reported: “Netanyahu said continued economic pressure on Iran was the best alternative to two other options, which he described as a bad deal and war.” The news agency’s reporting consistently cited Netanyahu’s statements urging a better deal in 2015, 2019, 2020, 2021, and 2024.
Reuters’ 2025 claim, then, that Netanyahu dreamed for decades of convincing Washington to wage war on Tehran says more about the reporter’s imagined view of the prime minister and less about the historical record and verified reality.
It’s not only Israeli figures whose secret thoughts have loomed large in journalistic divination dressed up as news reporting.
One month after the October 7 massacre, Agence France Presse (AFP) maintained that Hamas leader Yahya “Sinwar dreams of a single Palestinian state bringing together the Gaza Strip, the occupied West Bank – controlled by Mahmoud Abbas’s Fatah party – and annexed east Jerusalem.”
As CAMERA previously noted, “The article doesn’t explain how AFP has access to Sinwar’s dreams, but there’s no question that he heads the Gaza branch of a terror organization with a guiding mission which rejects the existence of Israel within any borders.”
In response to communication from CAMERA, AFP subsequently clarified: “The same year [Sinwar] was elected, Hamas for the first time accepted in principle a Palestinian state in the pre-1967 borders, while not recognizing Israel and retaining the ultimate goal of ‘liberating’ all of historic Palestine.”
“Tell the audience what you know and what you don’t know. Never imply that you have more knowledge than you actually do,” media scholar Rosentiel advises journalists. In other words, do your job as a good journalist. Don’t play God.
The writer is director of research and media analysis at the Committee for Accuracy in Middle East Reporting and Analysis and director of CAMERA’s Israel office.

====================================================================================================

Von der Leyen’s failures on Israel must end, starting with a settlement trade ban
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:22:59 UTC
22:22:59 KST

On 16 September 2026, the President of the European Commission Ursula von der Leyen will give her annual State of the European Union address. Here, Caroline Hansen, EU Foreign Policy Advocate at Amnesty International, and Claudio Francavilla, Associate Director for EU Advocacy, for Human Rights Watch, write that this year’s speech must be an opportunity to make good on past promises and finally present a ban on trade with illegal Israeli settlements.
Last year’s “State of the European Union” speech made headlines when European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen finally proposed suspending the trade part of the EU-Israel Association Agreement and sanctioning Israeli ministers and settlers. One year on, almost none of those measures have been adopted, and as more and more EU countries and allies adopt bans on trade with Israel’s illegal settlements, von der Leyen’s Europe is lagging behind.
These failures come with consequences: not only for her own credibility and that of the EU, but most of all for the lives of millions displaced, killed or wounded in Palestine and across the region.
Von der Leyen should use this year’s address to remedy that, by firstly making good on last year’s proposals, then by ensuring the EU joins the ranks of those banning trade with Israel’s illegal settlements.
Von der Leyen’s proposals last year came only after an official declaration of famine in the Gaza Strip, where Israeli authorities unlawfully blocked humanitarian aid as part of their genocidal campaign
Her proposals followed mass protests across Europe demanding an end to EU inaction and complicity, and calls by several EU member states and civil society groups to suspend the EU-Israel Association Agreement, whose essential human rights clause the EU found to have been breached by Israel.
“It truly pains me to say these words”, she confessed before announcing her proposals, which also included targeted sanctions against “extremist” Israeli ministers and settlers, and a suspension of a few million euros worth of EU funding to Israeli projects.
But there she stopped. As Germany and Italy obstructed the suspension of the Agreement and Hungary and Czechia vetoed sanctions against Israeli ministers, von der Leyen was reticent to speak out, in stark contrast to her approach on Hungary’s repeated vetoes on EU sanctions against Russia.
And while the EU repeated its condemnations of Israel’s illegal settlements, von der Leyen resisted growing calls to introduce a ban on the EU trade fueling the settlements’ economy and related abuses.
Von der Leyen also remained silent as Israel’s foreign minister Gideon Sa’ar lashed out at EU foreign policy chief Kaja Kallas, nor did she comment when EU Commissioner Dubravka Šuica met him in Tel Aviv shortly after, exposing a growing division and lack of solidarity in her Commission mirroring those among EU states.
As Europe showed weakness, divisions and its inability to deliver more than a handful of sanctions against Israeli settlers, the situation in the region kept deteriorating.
Israel’s spiralling abuses
When von der Leyen’s delivered her 2025 speech, at least 64,000 Palestinians had been killed in the Gaza Strip. That number is now close to 74,000 despite a so-called “ceasefire” in force since October 2025. Israeli authorities continue to impose severe, arbitrary and unlawful aid restrictions as they have confined the population into approximately 30 percent of the territory, and are once again openly discussing plans to ethnically cleanse Gaza of its Palestinian population.
In the West Bank, including East Jerusalem, despite protests by the EU and other close allies, Israeli authorities have turbocharged the expansion of illegal settlements. This resulted in expedited home demolitions, land seizure, fragmentation and dispossession of Palestinians – hallmarks of Israel’s ethnic cleansing and apartheid against Palestinians. At least 88 Palestinians, including 22 children, have been killed in the West Bank this year alone, bringing the total since October 2023 to nearly 1,100.
Israeli authorities also adopted a death penalty bill that de facto applies to Palestinians only and continue to detain thousands of Palestinians without charges or trial, including doctors like Hussam Abu Safiya, subjecting many to systematic torture.
In Lebanon, despite ceasefires that went into effect on April 17 and June 20, Israeli attacks have killed at least 4,383 people since the beginning of the March 2026 escalation, including 261 children, 399 women, and 135 medical workers, according to Lebanon’s Health Ministry. Israeli forces continue to cause widespread destruction and mass displacement, committing acts that Amnesty International has found to constitute war crimes.
Von der Leyen’s responsibilities
As President of the European Commission, von der Leyen has the duty to ensure that all EU policies – including its policies on trade and foreign affairs – comply with international law.
She can’t hide behind the EU’s humanitarian aid to Palestinians while doing nothing to address the reasons why such aid is so desperately needed. Nor can she blame member states for not approving her half-hearted proposals: she should actively and vocally engage with them and urge them to comply with the legal obligations under the UN Genocide Convention.
By the time of last year’s speech, Amnesty International, Al-Haq, B’Tselem, UN experts, and the International Association of Genocide Scholars were among those who had already concluded Israel was committing genocide against Palestinians in the Gaza Strip. Shortly after, a UN independent Commission of Inquiry confirmed those findings.
But whether von der Leyen agrees with the genocide qualification is irrelevant: under the Genocide Convention, awareness of a “serious risk” of genocide triggers the “duty to prevent”, compelling all states parties to that treaty – including all EU states – to “employ all means reasonably available to them, so as to prevent genocide so far as possible”. For the EU, that means suspending the Association Agreement and sanctioning Israeli officials.
Von der Leyen should also reaffirm the EU’s commitment to international justice and propose the use of the EU blocking statute to protect the International Criminal Court and those cooperating with it from crippling sanctions, adopted by the US as part of its campaign to dismantle the Court and shield its own and Israeli officials from accountability.
Von der Leyen should use this speech to propose an EU-wide ban of trade with Israel’s illegal settlements Much as she did with the Association Agreement, she doesn’t need preemptive support by the EU Council. No matter how much it might “pain” her, she should put the proposal on the table, and actively engage with member states, especially Germany and Italy, to ensure she has the necessary qualified majority to approve a measure that is required by international law.
Amid global turmoil and instability, Europe and the world desperately need principled, committed leaders determined to uphold the rules, especially those devised to prevent, punish and redress the worst atrocities, and adopt all necessary measures to that end.
For the EU, another year of failures, inaction and half-hearted proposals is blow to its credibility. For Palestinians and so many others in the region, it’s a matter of life and death.
What you can do to help
Suspend EU-Israel Trade Agreement
Send a message and demand the leaders of Germany and Italy support suspending the EU’s trade agreement with Israel.
There is no time to lose.

‘It makes you feel helpless’: patients and doctors cast doubt on standard treatment for endometriosis
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:22:50 UTC
22:22:50 KST

“It’s really frustrating to be in this cycle of continuous surgery. It makes you feel helpless,” says Natalie Greenwood, of a condition that affects her so badly she is “non-functioning most of the time because of the constant fatigue”.
Greenwood, 36, needs to have an operation every few years to limit the impact of the endometriosis that gives her chronic pelvic pain, as well as extreme tiredness and brain fog.
She says the trouble with depending on surgery is not just having an invasive procedure and then having to recover afterwards. It’s more that the benefit gained – the relief from persistent pain – is brief.
She is not alone in her lack of belief in surgery and hormonal medication being effective for chronic pelvic pain. A study has found that dual approach, which is the NHS’s usual way of treating the condition, does not improve quality of life for female patients.
Greenwood was diagnosed with endometriosis 13 years ago, after leaving university. It is a condition in which tissue similar to the lining of the uterus – the endometrium – is found outside the uterus, most commonly in the pelvic cavity, and can cause inflammation and pain.
Greenwood, who is a languages teacher in West Yorkshire, has already had surgery three times: when she was 23, 28 and 32. In the first two operations she underwent ablation, in which the endometriosis is removed using heat or cold. The last procedure involved excision, in which the surgeon cuts out the endometriosis.
“The surgery is effective in that it can keep the pain at bay for a few more years. But I’ve never come out of it feeling better,” she said. “And in between surgeries I’ve still had to have hormone treatments, like a hormonal coil, or been put into chemical menopause. That keeps the endometriosis at bay by shutting down the hormones that cause it. But the endometriosis keeps growing. So you might feel better for a while but it’s always there in the background.”
Even after surgery, Greenwood has still needed what she calls her “pain management toolkit” – Tens machines, peppermint teabags, cooling patches, hot-water bottles and CBD oil.
Endometriosis is one of the main causes of chronic pelvic pain, a condition that affects between 6% and 27% of women worldwide. Others include vulvodynia and bladder pain syndrome.
“The surgery has never been a magic fix for me,” said Greenwood. “I don’t think it’s a magic fix for many people. You don’t just suddenly feel completely better. A life of surgery every five years is just not something you want to do. It’s not sustainable. It’s like a constant rut.”
Greenwood has a boy of five and is keen to have another child. But endometriosis has affected her fertility so much that she will soon start a course of IVF.
Whatever the outcome of that treatment, she is sure of one thing: “I won’t have surgery again. It’s not manageable to keep having surgery every five years. Whatever happens with the IVF, after it I’m going to have a hysterectomy and my ovaries removed because that might give me some relief from endometriosis.”
Dr Jasmine Hearn, a reader in health psychology at Manchester Metropolitan University who co-led the research in the new study, said: “Sadly patients are often left struggling due to current pain management strategies not sufficiently alleviating pain.
“Chronic pelvic pain is often invisible but its impact can be profound. Our findings highlight that women with pelvic pain report poorer quality of life when they’ve had surgery and hormonal medication for pelvic pain and better quality of life when psychological support is part of treatment.”
The 147 women in the study, who were aged 18 to 66, all reported that their pelvic pain had a major impact on both their physical and psychological quality of life and ability to function normally.
Surgery was found to be associated with lower physical quality of life while painkillers were associated with women having poorer physical, psychological and environmental quality of life.
In contrast, women who received psychological help as part of their treatment to help them manage their pain said their quality of life was better. The evidence for its effectiveness was so great that all those who suffer pelvic pain should be offered it, Hearn added.
Dr Lucy Whitaker, a researcher with the charity Wellbeing of Women and a clinical lecturer in obstetrics and gynaecology at the University of Edinburgh, agreed. She said the same suggestion was made in 2020 by Westminster’s all-party parliamentary group on endometriosis and by a confidential national inquiry into the condition in 2024.
Whitaker added: “Chronic pelvic pain conditions like endometriosis carry a well-documented psychological burden and integrating psychological support alongside medical management reflects a more holistic approach to a condition that has long been under-resourced.”
A spokesperson for NHS England said: “Chronic pelvic pain can have a significant impact on women’s lives and mental health. That’s why psychological professionals provide specialist pain-management support as part of wider physical health services – including for chronic pelvic pain – while women can also self-refer to NHS talking therapies for support with long-term physical health conditions.”

Culinary Historian Pavel Syutkin Sentenced to 6 Years in Prison Over Bucha Posts

A Moscow judge on Tuesday sentenced culinary historian and former Moscow Times columnist Pavel Syutkin to six years in prison on charges of spreading “fake news” about the Russian military.
State prosecutors sought an eight-year prison sentence for Syutkin over Telegram posts about alleged atrocities committed against civilians in the Kyiv suburb of Bucha during the opening weeks of Russia’s full-scale invasion. Russia denies committing war crimes in Bucha.
A judge at Moscow’s Zyuzinsky District Court found Syutkin guilty of spreading “false information” about the Russian Armed Forces, the court’s press service said in a statement on Telegram.
In addition to the six-year prison term, the court banned Syutkin from managing websites for four years.
Syutkin’s attorney Yevgeniya Grigoryeva told the state-run news agency TASS that the defense plans to appeal the ruling, which she said will likely be heard in the Moscow City Court.
The trial was held behind closed doors. Ahead of the verdict, Syutkin told reporters the secrecy was maintained due to an FSB witness in the case.
“But that’s just the formal pretext. I think the real reason is that the court itself feels some discomfort about handing down a sentence in this kind of political case,” Syutkin told reporters in a video shared by the Mediazona outlet.
“This verdict is honestly a certain mark of quality for the work I’ve been doing, an effort… aimed at reviving civil society in our country,” Syutkin said in another video, followed by applause from those who went to hear the verdict in court.
Syutkin was arrested last October and added to the Russian financial monitoring agency Rosfinmonitoring’s list of “terrorists and extremists,” a move that froze his bank accounts.
He has published several books on the history of Russian and Soviet cuisine and hosted programs on national television and radio devoted to the history of food. In 2015, Syutkin represented Russia as an official ambassador at the World Expo in Milan.
Syutkin and his wife, Olga, wrote a weekly cooking column for The Moscow Times until Russian authorities banned the news outlet as “undesirable” in July 2024.

'BJP, RSS trying to impose Hindu law on non-Hindus': Owaisi on Amit Shah's UCC push

NEW DELHI: AIMIM supremo Asaduddin Owaisi on Tuesday turned to the Constitution to counter Union home minister Amit Shah's push to implement the Uniform Civil Code in all 21 NDA-ruled states by 2029. Hyderabad MP said that the Bhartiya Janata Party is using the proposed law to target Muslims and arguing that it could undermine key constitutional protections.Speaking to the media in Bhopal, Owaisi said the proposed law would conflict with several provisions of the Constitution.“This is against Articles 25, 26, 14 and 29 of the Constitution, and you cannot implement such a law by abolishing freedom of religion,” AIMIM leader said.His remarks came a day after he attacked Union home minister Amit Shah and the BJP over their push for the UCC.Speaking to reporters in Hyderabad on Monday, Owaisi alleged that the BJP was not seeking the UCC in the interest of equality but to target India’s Muslim population."First, you need to understand that the BJP is not pursuing the UCC for the sake of equality. They have introduced the UCC in their states and at the government level solely to target India's 18 crore Muslims; in doing so, the BJP and Amit Shah are acting in contravention of Articles 25 (Freedom of Religion), 14 (Right to Equality), 26, and 29 of the Indian Constitution. Advertisement
US-China ties haunted by lack of mutual trust, Beijing Xiangshan Forum told
No ‘illusion that things will be easy’ ahead, former Chinese ambassador to US says before President Xi Jinping is expected in Washington
3-MIN READ3-MIN

A lack of mutual trust and confidence haunts Sino-US relations, a major security conference in Beijing has heard, just a week before Xi Jinping travels to America for his first state visit there in over a decade.
“[While] we have the agreement on this constructive strategic stability, we don’t have the illusion that things will be easy going ahead. There are always problems, sometimes very big problems,” said Cui Tiankai, a former Chinese ambassador to Washington, at a panel of the Xiangshan Forum on Tuesday.
Cui praised the stability in bilateral ties since Xi hosted US President Donald Trump in May, including in terms of regular exchanges in diplomacy, economics, the military and law enforcement.
“I expect things will remain the same in the next few months,” he said.

Kim pro quo: North Korea is emerging from international isolation by backing Russia’s war in Ukraine
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:20:38 UTC
22:20:38 KST

In early September, Russia and North Korea opened a road bridge across the border river Tumen. Analysts note that the structure could become a key gateway for transporting North Korean troops and weapons for the war against Ukraine. Since 2023, military aid deliveries to Russia have become a key source of revenue for North Korea’s budget, fueling unprecedented economic growth. As Pyongyang has drawn closer to Moscow, China has also entered the competition for influence over North Korea — even though it backed sanctions against the country together with Russia back in 2017. Combined, these factors have strengthened Pyongyang’s position enough that it no longer needs to seek a thaw with the United States. At the same time, Donald Trump has shown readiness for talks, which, should they take place, could cement North Korea’s status as a nuclear power.
In July, the Bank of Korea estimated North Korea’s economic growth at more than 3% for a third straight year. The country has passed the peak recorded in 2017, shortly before the entry into force of what was then the UN most sweeping sanctions package ever.
Until recently, such a turnaround seemed unimaginable. North Korea emerged from the COVID-19 pandemic poorer and more isolated than ever: the country kept its borders closed for a long time, international sanctions continued to hamper trade, and disputes over its nuclear program further alienated it not only from the West but also from its traditional allies, China and Russia.
But the resumption of trade with China, large-scale state development programs, and military cooperation with Russia have reversed that trend, giving North Korea its most sustained economic growth in years.
For Moscow, Pyongyang is no longer a burden but a useful military partner, while Donald Trump’s return to the White House has once again made direct diplomatic contacts with Washington possible. North Korea is perhaps the most extreme case of rejecting the post-Cold War world order, and its rapid emergence from isolation suggests that order appears to be starting to crumble.
Unforeseen growth
North Korea’s economic recovery began with the country’s gradual opening. Pyongyang responded to the COVID-19 pandemic with near-total self-isolation, which collapsed its trade with China — from $2.8 billion in 2019 to $539 million in 2020 and just $318 million in 2021. In 2022, freight traffic gradually resumed, and by 2023 annual bilateral trade reached approximately $2.3 billion.
The restoration of ties with the outside world once again gave North Korea access to fuel, fertilizer, machinery, and consumer goods, while simultaneously opening up the Chinese market for North Korean exports. Meanwhile, as growth in agricultural output helped pull the economy out of the pandemic-induced slump, Pyongyang directed significant resources into construction and industry.
Domestic policy played an important role in this turnaround. North Korean leader Kim Jong Un made construction and regional development a state priority. In 2021, the authorities launched a program to build 50,000 new apartments in Pyongyang over five years. In 2024 an even more ambitious 20x10 Regional Development Policy followed, providing for the annual modernization of factories in 20 cities and counties over 10 years. The 20x10 program focuses primarily on the production of food, clothing, and everyday consumer goods.
This approach is atypical for North Korea, whose economic development has historically been extremely uneven. The capital, Pyongyang, and strategic heavy-industry sectors received a disproportionately large share of state investment compared with the provinces, which were left to survive on outdated production and the informal economy.
The 20x10 policy is an attempt to narrow that gap by setting up production of basic consumer goods in the provinces, developing the food industry, modernizing agriculture, and improving housing conditions. Still, it remains unclear whether the new enterprises will be operational amid an acute shortage of electricity, equipment, and raw materials. Either way, the authorities’ pivot toward provincial development is already yielding tentative results.
Satellite imagery shows that construction began just weeks after the 20x10 policy was announced, and in its first year factories genuinely appeared in all participating localities. In 2025, the program expanded to include hospitals, leisure spaces, agricultural infrastructure, and other local projects. All planned facilities were opened within the year.
In February 2026, the authorities announced the successful completion of the housing construction program in Pyongyang. Most of the new housing is concentrated in the newly created Hwasong district, where farmland gave way to dense high-rise development.
Foreigners who have visited the capital in recent years have said that the city’s current appearance differs dramatically from its pre-pandemic image. The online sector of the economy has become more visible: mobile apps for ordering taxis, QR-code payments, grocery and medicine delivery services, and new shops and restaurants have all appeared. Wide streets have, for the first time, started approaching the limits of their capacity, as North Korea has seen an influx of imported cars, mostly from China, brought into the country despite UN sanctions.
While the expansion of foreign trade and the pivot toward regional development laid the groundwork for North Korea’s economic upturn, the resources largely came from military cooperation with Moscow. Since the summer of 2023, Pyongyang has become a major supplier of artillery shells and ballistic missiles for Russia.
The resources for North Korea’s economic upturn largely came from military cooperation with Moscow
According to South Korean intelligence, by August 2026 North Korea had delivered more than 15 million shells to Moscow. The Institute for National Security Strategy (INSS), affiliated with South Korean intelligence, estimates North Korea’s revenue from arms exports alone at more than $10 billion. For a country with an annual GDP of about $27 billion, that is an extraordinarily large sum.
The subsequent dispatch of about 15,000 troops to Russia brought in another hundreds of millions of dollars, though INSS confirms that only a small portion of these funds has reached North Korea’s budget. The rest may have been compensated through military technology, components, or materials, or may still remain an outstanding debt.
But the amount of revenue reaching the budget is not the most important part here. Far more significant is the fact that cooperation with Russia is blunting the impact of sanctions imposed on North Korea. The restrictions introduced after 2016 were effective largely because Moscow and Beijing were willing to take part in isolating Pyongyang, helping limit its access to fuel, equipment, foreign currency, labor, and export markets it depended on.
Now Russia plays almost the opposite role: it has become a major buyer of North Korean defense-industry output, the very thing the sanctions targeted, and a supplier of resources they were meant to restrict access to. That is why North Korea’s current economic growth is not simply a recovery from the pandemic-induced downturn, but reflects far deeper shifts in the country’s international standing.
Pyongyang’s place in the new world
North Korea never lacked powerful allies. When the country was nearly destroyed by the U.S.-led coalition during the Korean War, communist China entered the war largely out of fear that a state loyal to Washington would emerge on its border. Today, the alliance with North Korea remains China’s only mutual defense commitment.
The problem lay elsewhere: Pyongyang lacked powerful partners who actually needed its assistance. Since the end of the Cold War, North Korea had little to offer Russia and China in exchange for economic and diplomatic support: a scarce labor force, a domestic market too poor to consume costly imports, and a steady stream of international scandals tied to its nuclear program and regular foreign-policy provocations. Russia’s full-scale war against Ukraine proved to be the turning point. The very traits that once made North Korea a diplomatic liability for its partners have now become assets.
This shift is clearly visible in Pyongyang’s relations with Moscow. In 2017, Vladimir Putin stated that Russia “unequivocally condemns” North Korea’s nuclear tests and “fully complies with UN Security Council resolutions” regarding the country. In 2024, the Russian president traveled to Pyongyang to sign a mutual defense treaty, and Moscow purchased billions of dollars’ worth of North Korean munitions.
These changes have also affected the international institutions originally created to contain North Korea. In March 2024, Russia blocked the renewal of the mandate of the UN Panel of Experts monitoring compliance with sanctions against North Korea, effectively eliminating one of the UN Security Council’s core mechanisms for overseeing sanctions pressure on Pyongyang.
In March 2024, Russia blocked one of the core UN mechanisms for enforcing sanctions against North Korea
For North Korea, the benefits of cooperation with Russia are not limited to revenue from arms exports. Moscow provides Pyongyang with diplomatic support at the UN and also grants access to fuel, materials, and modern weapons.
According to some South Korean and Western sources, the partnership also includes the transfer of military technology — from air defense systems to missiles and satellites. The Korean People’s Army (KPA) still relies mainly on Soviet-era designs and domestically upgraded versions. For Pyongyang, the partnership with Russia is one of the few realistic ways to develop its military capabilities in the absence of domestic capacity to do so.
North Korea’s growing independence appears to have prompted China to work harder to preserve its own influence, which had been declining since the start of the pandemic and as Pyongyang’s ties with Moscow deepened. In June 2026, Chinese President Xi Jinping visited North Korea for the first time in seven years. He pledged that China would continue to support the country and agreed to deepen cooperation between the two nations across various fields.
In effect, Moscow has forced Beijing to compete for influence over Pyongyang by giving it a genuine alternative source of support. Kim can now draw economic support from China and military and diplomatic benefits from Russia, all while reducing his dependence on each power and preventing either from taking its influence for granted.
Under these circumstances, Pyongyang no longer needs a thaw with the U.S. as urgently as it did in 2018, when the first talks between Donald Trump and Kim Jong Un took place — the first-ever meeting between U.S. and North Korean leaders. Back then, North Korea was almost completely cut off from the outside world, and Washington could bargain for concessions in exchange for sanctions relief and normalized relations.
The U.S. now has significantly fewer opportunities for such an exchange. Pyongyang has retained its nuclear arsenal, expanded its missile program, restored a substantial share of foreign trade, and built ties with Moscow and Beijing, obtaining access to many of the economic and strategic benefits that normalized relations with the U.S. once offered. This does not rule out negotiations or possible steps toward rapprochement. But the starting position has clearly shifted in Kim’s favor: Trump is willing to talk to North Korea again, but Pyongyang needs it far less.
Trump’s gambit
Trump has emphasized his readiness to resume talks with Pyongyang since his reelection, taking the most significant step in this direction on Aug. 16, when just hours before the start of the annual joint military exercise with South Korea, Ulchi Freedom Shield, he ordered a substantial reduction in its scale.
Trump has shown readiness to engage with Pyongyang since returning to the White House
Trump said the exercises were costly and were sending North Korea a “totally inappropriate and hostile” signal, and that during his presidency Pyongyang had behaved “harmlessly and respectfully.” The drills were shortened from 11 to five days, and the field-training period was also reduced.
Additionally, Washington later canceled a joint amphibious exercise with Seoul that had been planned for September. Given Pyongyang’s position, which regards the maneuvers as a rehearsal for invasion and demands their end along with the withdrawal of U.S. troops from South Korea, Trump’s decision resembles a goodwill gesture — especially since he did not even demand a personal meeting with Kim.
It is unclear whether the cancellation was inspired by Trump’s evident displeasure that Seoul did not support Washington in the war against Iran, but the U.S. president has taken similar steps before. After his first meeting with Kim in Singapore in 2018, Trump announced the suspension of the Ulchi Freedom Shield exercise along with several smaller maneuvers.
At the time, he also justified the decision by citing the cost of the exercises and echoed Pyongyang’s narrative about “provocations.” The external circumstances, however, were fundamentally different: 2018 saw the start of what was likely the most significant thaw in relations between North and South Korea since their division.
Even before the exercises were canceled, South Korean President Moon Jae-in and Kim Jong Un signed a declaration stating that “there will be no more war...on the Korean Peninsula,” and that the sides would pursue reconciliation, economic cooperation, and even possible reunification.
Since then, however, Pyongyang has formally renounced reunification, declared South Korea a hostile state, and even destroyed sections of the roads and railways that Moon and Kim once promised to connect.
North Korea enters any potential talks with a growing nuclear arsenal, a new mutual defense treaty with Russia, and little regard for the sanctions regime, which was once Washington’s chief bargaining chip. Where Washington could once have demanded major concessions from Pyongyang in exchange for ending U.S.-South Korean exercises, that now becomes merely a starting point for dialogue. Kim, meanwhile, has yet to offer even the vague commitments that preceded the 2018 summit.
That said, Pyongyang does not reject the possibility of resuming talks. According to South Korean intelligence, a new meeting between Trump and Kim could be arranged “at any time,” but on fundamentally different terms than in the past: North Korea now views its nuclear arsenal not as a bargaining chip but as an inherent part of the state and its primary guarantee of sovereignty.
In analysts’ view, the U.S. war with Iran confirmed a conclusion Pyongyang had already reached: the attack on Tehran was possible only because Iran lacked reliable nuclear deterrence, meaning the arsenal must be preserved. Given Washington’s push for North Korea’s complete nuclear disarmament, which is also backed by South Korea, sweeping compromises look increasingly unlikely, and any future agreement will most likely only involve limits on further buildup and use of the nuclear arsenal itself.
The Korean rebalancing
Despite these new significant advantages for North Korea, the balance of power on the Korean Peninsula still clearly favors its southern neighbor. Despite its huge size and central place in the state structure, the Korean People’s Army (KPA) lags behind the Republic of Korea Armed Forces technologically, relying on significantly outdated arsenals.
The economic gap is even wider: in 2025, South Korea’s nominal gross national income exceeded North Korea’s by a factor of 56. South Korea’s annual defense budget alone is larger than the estimated value of North Korea’s entire economy, offering Seoul the opportunity to modernize its military, replenish losses, and maintain modern weapons systems on a scale Pyongyang cannot match.
The one area where the gap between the two countries is narrowing is manpower. In 2025, South Korea’s birth rate stood at just 0.8 children per woman. A demographic decline that for decades represented only a potential economic problem has now begun to affect the pool of potential conscripts as well.
From 2019 to 2025, the number of 20-year-old men in South Korea fell by 30%, and the active-duty military shrank by 20%, to 450,000 troops. Various estimates place the personnel shortfall at approximately 50,000 soldiers. Seoul has managed to soften the impact through military reorganization, expanded recruitment of contract soldiers and women, and technical modernization. But these measures do little to change the basic arithmetic of conscription: each new generation produces significantly fewer recruits than the previous one.
From 2019 to 2025, the number of young men in South Korea fell by 30%, and the size of the active-duty military by 20%
For North Korea, this problem currently looks less acute — but only in comparison with the South. According to UN estimates, North Korea’s 2025 birth rate stood at 1.8 — significantly higher than its neighbor’s, but still below the replacement level. When the small generation born during and after the famine of the 1990s began reaching conscription age, the authorities reportedly eased physical requirements for military personnel and began increasingly recruiting women into the army.
After 2020, the working-age population began shrinking by nearly 1% a year. The state is increasingly sending young people to work in mines and on farms, while mobilizing married women for factories and other production facilities facing tangible labor shortages.
The demographic balance between the two countries is shifting in two directions at once. Seoul is losing conscription-age population significantly faster than Pyongyang, allowing the North to retain one of the few advantages not offset by its neighbor’s economic superiority.
In the long run, however, North Korea will face the same challenge with far fewer resources to soften it. North Korea’s seemingly stable demographic edge is better understood as a reprieve. Still, it could meaningfully shift the military balance during the period before its population decline catches up with South Korea’s.
In the long run, North Korea will face a demographic decline without sufficient means to soften it
Since the late 1980s, virtually every long-term trend has favored South Korea: its economy grew and its military modernized, while North Korea only grew poorer and more isolated. The gap between the two countries remains enormous today, but time is no longer unambiguously on Seoul’s side. South Korea is beginning to face the demographic consequences of the very economic development that put it so far ahead of its neighbor. For North Korea, meanwhile, a rare moment has arrived in which several factors that once worked against it have started to ease.
Economic growth, a military partnership with Russia, renewed interest from China, and reduced vulnerability to Western sanctions have likely put North Korea in its strongest international position since the end of the Cold War. If Donald Trump and Kim Jong Un sit down at the negotiating table again, the U.S. will no longer be dealing with an isolated pariah state, as in 2018, but with a country that, amid the breakdown of the old world order, has forged a path to survival on its own terms.

Clean Arctic volunteers collect 235 tons of waste in Yakutia's 3 districts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:19:25 UTC
22:19:25 KST

MOSCOW, September 15. /TASS/. The Clean Arctic Public Environmental Project finalized big-scale clean-up missions in Yakutia's Arctic Zone. Volunteers and local residents collected 235 tons of waste in Verkhoyansk, Tiksi, Ese-Khaya and Batagai villages, the project's press service said.
"As for Yakutia, our project always has the most extensive cleaning missions there. The region's Governor Aisen Nikolaev personally controls this process, and this explains such a significant result," the press service quoted the Clean Arctic Environmental Project's leader Andrey Nagibin as saying. "This year, the project will cover twelve districts at a time. I believe, by the end of the season, the numbers will be impressive. After all, we have collected 235 tons of waste in three districts alone."
In the Bulunsky District, the cleanup mission was organized in the coastal area near the Tiksi village bay. The volunteers cleaned one hectare of territory and removed 14 tons of scrap metal and waste. They used equipment, provided by the Tiksi seaport.
"It is important for us that the history of Yakutia's participation in the Clean Arctic federal environmental project has begun precisely with the Tiksi settlement in the Bulunsky District. In 2021, the project started there, and today it unites residents of the Arctic territories, representatives of enterprises, institutions, and volunteers. Our returning to environmental activities in Tiksi means that we continue the started work, and we can confirm that caring for the Arctic nature becomes a common responsibility. I would like to thank all the participants for their contribution to keeping our northern coast clean," Yakutia's Minister for the Development of the Arctic and the Peoples of the North Vladimir Chernogradsky said.
In Yakutia's Verkhoyansk District, the Clean Arctic project's cleanup missions were in three locations: in Verkhoyansk, where the participants used specialized equipment to remove about 60 tons of metal structures and building remnants. In the village of Ese-Khaya, within one day, the mission participants collected and removed about 160 tons of waste, including wood, concrete and scrap metal. The biggest mission was in the village of Batagai, where volunteers and local residents cleaned the park, the Yana River coastline, as well as territories along Ammosov and Lenin Streets. The total cleaned area made four hectares, and about one ton of waste was removed from there.
About the project
The Clean Arctic Public Environmental Project has been cleaning the northern territories since 2021. The volunteers focus on objects, left since the USSR times. Waste removal is a complex technological work, where the movement teams work carefully minding the Arctic nature. Over all seasons, the project's almost 10,000 volunteers have collected 22,000 tons of waste and cleaned 1,100 hectares of land. The project's general partner is the Rosatom State Corporation, and TASS is the general information partner.

CHP drops appeal against ruling on 2023 convention
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:18:54 UTC
22:18:54 KST

Republican People’s Party spokesperson Müslim Sarı addresses a press conference at the party headquarters. (AA Photo)
The Republican People’s Party has withdrawn an appeal against a court ruling that invalidated its 2023 leadership election, saying the move was intended to prevent delays to its regular congress schedule.
Announced by CHP spokesperson Müslim Sarı on Sept. 14, the decision has raised questions about the fate of the appeal pending before the Court of Appeals and its potential impact on the legal status of the party’s 38th ordinary convention in November 2023.
Sarı said the decision to withdraw the appeal was made at a Sept. 10 meeting of the party’s Central Executive Board. He argued that ousted CHP leader Özgür Özel and other members who filed the appeal were no longer parties to the case because they had left the CHP and formed a new political party.
Özel’s New Party argues that withdrawing the appeal would not prevent the Court of Appeals from reviewing the case.
New Party senior lawmaker Murat Emir criticized the CHP’s decision, saying it appeared to be a defense against a possible ruling by the top appeals court.
The withdrawal has also reignited debate over the legal status of the CHP’s 2023 convention and which delegates would be eligible to participate in a new vote.
The CHP announced a new regular congress schedule on Sept. 10. Under the timetable, the party plans to submit membership lists before publishing them for public review on Oct. 28.
The election of district congress delegates in neighborhoods is scheduled for Nov. 11, with district congresses set to begin Dec. 12. Provincial congresses are expected to be completed by Feb. 14, 2027, and the CHP plans to renew its main convention as early as March.
A regional appeals court in Ankara had ruled that the CHP’s November 2023 vote was subject to “absolute nullity” and ordered Özel and the party leadership removed from office as a precautionary measure. The ruling reinstated former leader Kemal Kılıçdaroğlu.
On July 24, Özgür Özel resigned from his post as CHP lawmaker, breaking away with 90 fellow MPs to establish the New Party.

Why the era of cheap government debt is over
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:17:00 UTC
22:17:00 KST

Why the era of cheap government debt is over
September 15, 2026
When the world's largest pension fund changes its investment strategy, the financial world pays close attention, especially when it plans to drastically reduce its holdings of US government bonds.
According to Reuters, Norway's sovereign wealth fund wants to cut its US bond holdings, which totalled around $215 billion (€186 billion) at the end of June, by $80 billion.
Global investors are increasingly viewing the US debt binge with suspicion, especially as the country's national debt surpassed $40 trillion in August. This week, the yield on 30-year US Treasury bonds climbed to nearly 5.4%, the highest level since 2007.
How much debt is too much?
For highly indebted countries like Japan, where debt exceeds 200% of economic output, borrowing money on capital markets is getting increasingly expensive. The same applies to Italy, France and the UK.
Germany is in a much better position. At around 65% of gross domestic product (GDP), its debt-to-GDP ratio is only about half that of the US.
Borrowing to modernize its armed forces and infrastructure, however, means that ratio is set to move toward 80% over the coming years.
Why US debt keeps rising
The US now spends over $1 trillion annually on interest payments — more than $3 billion per day — according to calculations by the Congressional Budget Office. Since 2024, Washington has spent more annually on servicing its debt than on its entire military.
The Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis calculated that the US national debt has risen by around 650% over the past 30 years, from $5.2 trillion in 1996.
This year, the US budget deficit is on course to reach nearly 6%. Yet, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent aims to cut it by half — a goal seen as unrealistic given the huge cost of the Iran war plus a shortfall in revenue due to corporate tax cuts and the tariffs struck down by the Supreme Court.
"That is a very difficult path," Carsten Roemheld, capital market strategist at Fidelity International, told DW. "Nervousness is also rising sharply within the US administration — that is clearly evident. If this trajectory continues, it will be very difficult to sustain."
This is particularly striking when you consider how rapidly US debt is rising.
The $10 trillion mark was surpassed during the 2008 financial crisis, while $20 trillion was reached in 2017. By early 2022, the figure had already hit $30 trillion. It took just four and a half years to reach the next record high of $40 trillion this summer.
Why bond investors are worried
Bessent has therefore resorted to a special measure to drive down US bond yields.
On September 9, he announced he would triple the volume of long-term US government bond buybacks from the previous $2 billion to as much as $6 billion.
"The measure is, of course, far too small on its own to truly keep yields in check over the long term," said Roemheld.
"The bond market [now] demands discipline," Kim Crawford, global fixed income portfolio manager at JPMorgan Asset Management, told the Financial Times.
That discipline is, however, sorely lacking and many analysts say governments have shown little appetite for spending cuts.
US still dominates capital markets
Despite all the problems, most economists agree that there is currently no way for investors to bypass the US.
"The European capital market is not yet an alternative to the US market," said Carsten Brzeski, chief economist at ING Bank, noting that China and other emerging markets are neither able nor willing to take over this role.
"That is why I believe investors — even though they currently fear higher inflation and question the sustainability of US sovereign debt — will not turn away from the US," Brzeski told DW. "At least not as long as the US economy is growing."
Fidelity's Roemheld also sees no immediate danger for the US, which he says is unlikely to default and can simply print money to pay off the debt.
"But this course of action will undermine confidence," Roemheld said. "And one outlet for this is the dollar exchange rate, which suffers when international investors become less willing to continue providing dollars to the US."
AI investment drives up interest rates
However long the US war against Iran drags on — driving up military spending and energy prices — there are strong indications that the US will have to pay higher interest rates on capital markets
According to Robert Sockin, chief US economist at asset manager PGIM, this is partly due to artificial intelligence or "AI-related corporate bonds increasingly crowding out government bonds," as he put it.
Roemheld agrees that as major tech companies issue bonds to finance AI infrastructure they are creating competition for government debt.
"The four major 'hyperscalers' alone — the companies building platforms for the US AI industry — have already raised over $800 billion in debt this year, a figure expected to rise further over the next year or two," he told DW.
This is an attractive alternative for many investors who previously held US Treasuries for safety only to see their outlook deteriorate in recent years, says Roemheld.
If as an investor I can now get "long-term bonds from high-quality companies like Alphabet or Microsoft, sometimes with spreads of 100 basis points over Treasuries, then that represents genuine competition," he told DW.
This piece was originally published in German.

MOSCOW, September 15. /TASS/. Russia ranked second only to the US in terms of the value of liquefied natural gas (LNG) supplies to the European Union (EU) in July with a 18.6% share, according to TASS calculations based on Eurostat data.

EU’s purchases of Russian pipeline gas, LNG fall 2.6% in January-July

MOSCOW, September 15. /TASS/. The European Union (EU) purchased Russian pipeline gas worth 3.4 bln euro and liquefied natural gas (LNG) worth 5.15 bln euro between January and July 2026, according to Eurostat data and TASS calculations. Total purchases of Russian gas by the EU fell by 2.6% in the reporting period to 8.6 bln euros.
In July, EU countries paid 607 mln euro for LNG from Russia. The main importers were France (365 mln euro), Belgium (92 mln euro), and Spain (78 mln euro). The EU also imported Russian pipeline gas worth 512 mln euro in July.
The share of Russian LNG in total liquefied gas imports by EU countries stood at 18.6% in July. The United States ranked first with a 53.9% share.
The share of Russian pipeline gas in the total value of the European Union’s pipeline gas imports was 10.9% in July. Norway took first place with 36.3%, followed by Algeria with 21.7%, and the UK with 19.3%.

Unknown
