2026-09-15
13:15:24 UTC
22:15:24 KST

Australian minister for Trade and Tourism Don Farrell delivers a speech during the opening session of the OECD ministerial Council meeting at OECD headquarters in Paris, on June 3, 2025. | Geoffroy Van Der Hasselt/AFP via Getty Images
Canberra is watching Canada’s attempt to build a closer relationship with the European Union and is aligned with Ottawa on the issue, according to Don Farrell, Australia's trade minister.
“We are on the same page," Farrell told the Sydney Morning Herald on Tuesday, adding, "We will take great interest in what [Canadian Prime Minister] Mark Carney does."
Carney has pivoted towards Europe as Canada attempts to grapple with increasingly hostile trade relations with Washington. He said on Sunday that Ottawa is seeking a "unique alliance" with the EU, following a Wall Street Journal report suggesting he has floated making Canada an “associate member” of the bloc.
Advertisement
Farrell said he would be open to all possibilities in potential new arrangements with the EU, but did not elaborate on what these arrangements might be. Commenting on U.S. President Donald Trump's tariff policy, he said he wasn't confident that a Democrat president would reverse trade barriers. “So we are stuck with them. And that makes the EU even more important to us,” he added.
Farrell's remarks come as his prime minister, Anthony Albanese, attempts to get Australia's own EU trade deal through parliament. “The passage of this new deal through the parliament is not guaranteed,” Farrell said last month. Labor holds 30 of the Senate’s 76 seats, while the opposition Liberal-National Coalition has 27 and has threatened to oppose the agreement over agricultural access and climate policy.
The EU matters to Australia in practical terms: Trade in goods and services between the EU and Australia topped €91 billion in 2024, making the bloc Canberra’s third-largest goods trading partner after China and Japan. Goods trade has grown by almost 50 percent over the past decade.
Under the deal, 97.8% of Australia’s goods exports would enter the EU duty-free once it takes effect.
Australia’s interest in closer European ties is not entirely new. In 2025, Canberra reopened non-binding talks about associating with Horizon Europe, the bloc’s research and innovation program — a step toward scientific cooperation, not EU membership.

The history of Ceuta and Melilla and why they are Spanish territory
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:15:22 UTC
22:15:22 KST

Ceuta and Melilla have been under Spanish rule since the 15th and 16th centuries and have never been part of the Moroccan state. The UN does not treat them as territories awaiting decolonisation, despite Rabat's objections.
"Ceuta and Melilla are not just Spanish, they are quintessentially Spanish and untouchable", said Spain's Defence Minister Margarita Robles, speaking days after the surge of irregular migrants from Morocco who stormed the Spanish border last July.
The sovereignty of Ceuta and Melilla regularly returns to public debate, fuelled by Rabat's claims and by renewed migratory tensions on the North African border. After the migration crisis that unfolded in Ceuta, several Moroccan politicians seized the moment to call for both Spanish autonomous cities to become part of Morocco.
The narrative that both cities are "colonial remnants" is often deployed as a political argument, but history, international law and the United Nations (UN) itself contradict this premise.
Since when have Ceuta and Melilla been Spanish?
To understand the special status of both enclaves, it is necessary to go back to the 15th and 16th centuries, a time when the concept of "colonialism" did not yet exist in the modern terms we understand today.
Melilla was incorporated into the Crown of Castile on 17 September 1497 by an expedition led by Pedro de Estopiñán and authorised by the all-powerful Catholic Monarchs, Isabella and Ferdinand. The capture served a strictly defensive objective: to secure the Strait and protect the peninsular coasts from Barbary piracy.
Ceuta was initially conquered by Portugal in 1415 and came under Spanish sovereignty in 1580 following the dynastic union under Philip II, after he inherited the Portuguese throne. When Portugal regained its independence in 1640, Ceuta freely chose to remain linked to the Spanish monarchy, a decision legally ratified in the Treaty of Lisbon of 1668.
Both cities have spent between 358 and 529 years without interruption under Spanish sovereignty and have never belonged to the Kingdom of Morocco.
Did Morocco exist when they were incorporated into Spain?
Not in the form of a contemporary unified state. The Alaouite dynasty, which currently rules the country under Mohammed VI, began its reign in 1666.
This means that Melilla, for example, had already been part of the Castilian Crown for more than 160 years before the founding of the dynasty that underpins modern Morocco.
The nuance between 19th-century expansions and the Protectorate
To carry out a rigorous analysis, it is necessary to consider a nuance highlighted by various historians: around 90% of the current urban area of both cities stems from territorial extensions carried out throughout the 19th century and the early 20th century. This coincided with the era of European expansion in Africa and with the establishment of the Spanish Protectorate in Morocco (1912-1956).
The size of the cities grew through border treaties, but their sovereignty was already established 300 years earlier and they were never managed as a temporary colony, but as national territory.
However, there is an underlying legal distinction:
- The legal title to sovereignty over the original territory is several centuries older than Moroccan independence.
- Neither Ceuta nor Melilla ever formed part of the Protectorate. While Moroccan territory was under temporary administration, the two enclaves retained without interruption their status as national territory under full Spanish sovereignty.
The key position of the UN and the international community
The ultimate legal backing for Spain's position comes from the multilateral arena.
In 1975, the Moroccan government formally asked the UN to add Ceuta and Melilla to the list of "territories pending decolonisation" (which does include Western Sahara, occupied by Morocco and whose citizens Spain intends to grant nationality to). The UN rejected the request.
To this day, the United Nations does not consider them colonies or include them in any list of territories to be decolonised. Strategic allies such as the United States, despite its current closeness to the Moroccan regime, formally recognise both cities as part of Spain's sovereign territory.
Furthermore, with the entry into force of their Statutes of Autonomy in 1995, they were consolidated as Autonomous Cities, fully integrated into the European Union and represented in the Spanish Congress and Senate.
Claims about the alleged "colonial" nature of Ceuta and Melilla may reflect Rabat's short-term geopolitical strategies, but they do not stand up to historical scrutiny or to the legal framework of international law.

Ukraine’s Parliament Dismisses Prosecutor General
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:15:10 UTC
22:15:10 KST

Ukraine’s parliament on Tuesday, Sept. 15, dismissed Prosecutor General Ruslan Kravchenko, ending his tenure after he submitted his resignation amid an anti-corruption investigation involving officials inside the Prosecutor General’s Office.
A total of 317 lawmakers voted to approve President Volodymyr Zelensky’s motion to dismiss Kravchenko, according to the Judicial and Legal Newspaper.
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
Zelensky submitted the motion on Monday and said Kravchenko’s dismissal was “the only path” forward. He also signed a decree suspending Kravchenko from office for the duration of martial law.
Dismissal follows Operation Carthage
Kravchenko’s resignation came as the National Anti-Corruption Bureau of Ukraine (NABU) and the Specialized Anti-Corruption Prosecutor’s Office (SAPO) investigated a criminal network allegedly operating inside the Prosecutor General’s Office.
The probe, known as Operation Carthage, centers on allegations that officials took bribes to shield fraudulent call centers and laundered the proceeds.
Kravchenko was not formally charged in the case and has denied allegations against him.
On Monday, Kravchenko said he had signed a notice of suspicion against NABU Director Semen Kryvonos, accusing him of wrongdoing. NABU and SAPO rejected the allegations.
The dismissal therefore formally ends Kravchenko’s tenure as Ukraine’s top prosecutor, but the investigation that preceded it remains ongoing.
Putin Resists Energy Truce Announced by Trump
What Happens Next
The prosecutor general is appointed and dismissed by parliament on the president’s motion.
Kravchenko took office in June 2025 after parliament approved Zelensky’s nomination. His dismissal comes amid renewed scrutiny of Ukraine’s law enforcement institutions and the government’s handling of high-profile anti-corruption investigations.
Following Tuesday’s vote, Kravchenko no longer serves as Ukraine’s prosecutor general.

Ukrainian Drone Threat Pushes Putin’s Valdai Forum From Sochi to Moscow Region
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:13:54 UTC
22:13:54 KST

- Category
- War in Ukraine
Ukrainian Drone Threat Pushes Putin’s Valdai Forum From Sochi to Moscow Region
Russian leader Vladimir Putin’s annual Valdai Discussion Club meeting has been moved from the Black Sea resort city of Sochi to the Moscow region amid concerns that Ukrainian drones could pose a security threat.
We bring you stories from the ground. Your support keeps our team in the field.
According to the Financial Times on September 15, four people familiar with the decision said Russia’s presidential security service pushed for the relocation because of concerns over possible Ukrainian drone attacks. Around 120 participants had initially been invited to Sochi before being informed in August that the venue had changed.
The Valdai Discussion Club confirmed on September 14 that its 23rd annual meeting will take place in the Moscow region from September 28 to October 1. Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov and the forum’s organizers did not respond to the FT’s requests for comment on why the event was moved.
Putin traditionally uses the annual Valdai meeting to outline his views on Russian foreign policy and the international order. Russian Foreign Minister Sergey Lavrov, Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak, and presidential aide Maxim Oreshkin are also expected to attend this year’s gathering.
The decision comes as Putin has sharply reduced his visits to Sochi, where he maintains the Bocharov Ruchey presidential residence. According to the FT, Putin has not appeared in the city since the previous Valdai conference ended in October 2025.
Before Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine, Putin regularly traveled to Sochi. The FT reported that he visited the city 24 times during the final year before the invasion but made only two trips in each of 2024 and 2025.
The reduced travel and relocation of the Valdai forum come as Ukrainian forces expand long-range drone operations against military and strategic targets inside Russia, including in the Krasnodar region surrounding Sochi.
On August 9, Ukrainian forces struck a Russian S-400 air defense position near Gelendzhik, according to Ukraine’s Unmanned Systems Forces commander Robert “Madyar” Brovdi.
On September 4, another reported Ukrainian drone attack targeted an oil depot and an S-300 or S-400 air defense unit near Sirius, southeast of central Sochi. Putin’s Bocharov Ruchey residence is located several dozen kilometers from the area.
The FT reported that security concerns linked to the growing reach of Ukrainian drones were behind the decision to move this year’s Valdai gathering away from Sochi.

Russian Military Losses Surpass 300,000 Killed and Wounded Since Beginning of 2026
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:13:28 UTC
22:13:28 KST

- Category
- War in Ukraine
Russian Military Losses Surpass 300,000 Killed and Wounded Since Beginning of 2026
Russia has lost 301,030 military personnel killed or wounded since the beginning of 2026, Ukraine’s General Staff reported on September 15.
We bring you stories from the ground. Your support keeps our team in the field.
To illustrate the scale of the losses, the General Staff said the figure is equivalent to the personnel of nearly 150 motorized rifle regiments, assuming an average strength of around 2,000 troops per brigade.
Russia sustained its heaviest personnel losses during the three summer months, when 124,170 troops were reported killed or wounded. Of those, 39,290 were recorded in June, followed by 42,860 in July and 42,020 in August.
Losses have continued into September. As of the beginning of September 15, Russian forces had lost another 20,360 personnel since the start of the month, according to the General Staff.
The figures come amid broader assessments pointing to the scale of Russia’s personnel losses since the beginning of its full-scale invasion. British military intelligence estimates that around 500,000 Russian soldiers have been killed since February 2022.
Most of those deaths have occurred since January 2023, a period during which Russian forces captured only around 2% of Ukrainian territory, Air Chief Marshal Sir Rich Knighton, chief of the UK armed forces, told Sky News on September 11.
“I think these numbers illustrate clearly the senseless and futile waste of human life for such little progress, and against such an illegitimate aim of President Putin,” Knighton said in a prepared statement to journalists.
Ukraine’s General Staff, meanwhile, has reported that Russia’s overall military personnel losses, including both killed and wounded, have reached around 1.5 million since the start of the full-scale invasion, describing the toll as the Russian military’s highest in a single war since World War II.
According to the General Staff, the figure exceeds the combined losses sustained by Soviet and Russian forces in all wars and armed conflicts during the second half of the 20th century and the early 21st century.
As Russian forces continue to suffer substantial battlefield losses, Moscow is moving to increase the size of its military. Russia could mobilize up to 600,000 additional personnel by the end of 2027 in an effort to replace losses and maintain sufficient manpower for continued offensive operations in Ukraine.

Ministers of Defence of Poland and Italy on security and military cooperation
Poland
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
15:13:09 CEST
22:13:09 KST

Ministers of Defence of Poland and Italy on security and military cooperation
15.09.2026
Military mobility - this is the postulate that our countries have been making for a long time. The European Union should immediately decide to allow unimpeded movement of military vehicles if any country needs help and invites the armies of their allies to provide it. It is not acceptable for any region or location to block the passage of allied troops if help is necessary - said the Deputy Prime Minister W. Kosiniak-Kamysz.
Deputy Prime Minister Władysław Kosiniak-Kamysz met with the Minister of Defence of the Republic of Italy Guido Crosetto on 15 September in Warsaw. Deputy Minister Magdalena Sobkowiak-Czarnecka also took part in the meeting.
- I am grateful to the Minister for the two sentences said during today’s meeting. First, that stronger Poland means stronger Italy. I am thankful for these words. Poland and Italy do not want to be lonely islands. We want to cooperate, to act in aid of security. This is the rule that will guide us. (…) The other sentence was that the defence of the eastern flank is the defence of Italy. Not just the defence of the Italian border, not just the defence of the Polish border, but collective, allied, joint defence. (…) I am grateful for stating things so clearly and for putting it in practice, when you send your planes or systems. I also remember vessels from the Italian Navy that protected the Baltic Sea and cooperated in our mission. That was in the beginning, when we both became Ministers.
- said the Polish Minister of National Defence after the meeting.
The meeting of the Ministers of Defence of Poland and Italy was another opportunity to discuss the perspectives of cooperation in the areas of security and defence for both countries. Talks were focused both on current security situation, including the situation in Ukraine and intensifying actions of Russia targeting the security of the North Atlantic Alliance, especially violating its airspace, as well as current bilateral relations and the possibility of new joint initiatives. An exchange of experiences in the cooperation of defence industries so far was an important part of the meeting.
The discussions have also concerned actions on the level of the European Union to increase defence capabilities. Cooperation around the SAFE instrument, the aim of which is supporting European defensive investments, was one of the topics. Working together in this area could be an additional space to develop joint projects and bolster European defence industries.
- Another topic is economic cooperation. We are in the second phase of the SAFE mechanism and discuss joint procurement. We have already purchased tanker transport planes together with Spain. Today we have talked about advanced with Minister Crosetto about advanced attack trainer helicopters. This is one of the needs the Polish Armed Forces have, included in the SAFE programme. Italy could support its industry, including the one located in Poland - I want to remind you, Italian investments are in Świdnik, for example, and it is thousands of people overall. We want to purchase attack trainer helicopters as quickly as possible, and Italy is an important partner for us in this endeavour. We are entering the final phase of negotiations on this matter. I am counting on great openness. It is very important for us to place production in Poland, something the Minister has assured me of
- the Deputy Prime Minister informed.
The continuation of dialogue on the ministerial level confirms the interest of both sides in further developing defence relations, including looking for more areas of cooperation for defence industries. Matters tied to the security of the entire North Atlantic Alliance were an important part of the talks. Poland and Italy, although they are responsible for security on different NATO flanks, see the need to strengthen the defensive capabilities of European countries and increasing their contribution to the security of the whole Alliance.
- We have also received support for the process of creating the headquarters for the programme of oil pipeline extension programme in Poland. This decision was made at the Summit in Ankara. NATO will expand oil pipelines from Western Germany to Poland. This is very important, we have argued for that for more than ten years. We want the headquarters for this project to be placed in Poland. We can count on the support of Italy in this matter at future meetings of NATO Ministers of Defence. Poland and Italy are friends, our relations have never been so close. The development of joint defence capabilities is on the highest level. The next few months will see even closer cooperation and joint actions of the Polish and Italian Armed Forces, for which I am grateful
- noted the Minister of National Defence.
As Deputy Prime Minister Kosiniak-Kamysz noted, topics regarding security in the context of the situation in Ukraine were among the things discussed. The Polish Minister of National Defence said that Poland and Italy have the same position on supporting Ukraine. Both countries support it and will continue to do so.

Gas stations in the Kyiv region will gain access to real-time data on the movement of enemy targets — Regional Military Administration

2026-09-15
16:12:34 EEST
22:12:34 KST

Gas stations in the Kyiv region will gain access to real-time data on the movement of enemy targets — Regional Military Administration
Kyiv • UNN
Gas stations in the Kyiv region will be connected to the Defense Forces' air awareness system. Employees will see the movement of enemy targets in real time.
Security at filling stations in Kyiv region will be strengthened during air attacks. As reported by Timur Tkachenko, head of the Kyiv Regional Military Administration, filling station employees will be able to see up-to-date information on the movement of enemy aerial targets in real time, UNN reports.
Filling station complexes in the region will gain access to a special digital air-awareness system used by the Defense Forces. Access to it will allow responsible filling station employees to see up-to-date information on the movement of enemy aerial targets in real time and make more prompt decisions regarding the operation of filling station complexes during a threat
According to him, filling stations will no longer have to rely solely on the general air-raid alert announcement. They will have more information about the specific threat and will be able to respond in advance to protect employees and people located on the station's premises.
The enemy's latest attacks show that filling stations and other fuel infrastructure facilities are also under threat. Therefore, we must stay one step ahead in the new conditions. Our task is to provide business entities with the maximum number of available tools so that they can see the danger earlier and respond to it in a timely manner
Tkachenko also urged drivers to respond responsibly to danger signals and follow filling station employees' instructions regarding operations during alerts.

Poland reinforces protection of border crossings with Ukraine

Poland reinforces protection of border crossings with Ukraine
WARSAW, Sept 15 - Poland is reinforcing its border crossings with Ukraine, Defence Minister Wladyslaw Kosiniak-Kamysz said on Tuesday, after Russian drone attacks just miles from the NATO member's guard posts at the frontier over the weekend.
A Russian drone hit a passenger train on Ukraine's border with Poland on Sunday while another hit a truck at a nearby petrol station.
Border guards later said they had had to suspend crossings, evacuate people from vehicles and seek shelter.
"Recent days show that Russia's actions threaten the security of all of Europe. In accordance with our government's decision, the infrastructure used by the Border Guard is being strengthened," Kosiniak-Kamysz wrote on X.
"Soldiers of the 18th Engineer Regiment are already carrying out tasks, thanks to which the checkpoints and infrastructure near the Polish-Ukrainian border will be reinforced."
The Polish army command said in a separate post that engineer regiments are building border fortifications using collapsible, wire-mesh lined containers that can be filled with sand or gravel and act as a blast wall or retaining structure.
Construction work also includes observation and guard posts near the Polish-Ukrainian border close to crossings in Dorohusk, Medyka, and Korczowa, it said. REUTERS

Testosterone scripts for women rise, along with denials and delays
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:11:59 UTC
22:11:59 KST

Testosterone scripts for women rise, along with denials and delays
Sept 15 - Women increasingly turning to testosterone therapy to manage menopause symptoms are running into unexpected prescription delays and denials at pharmacies, according to interviews with more than half a dozen doctors, nurses and patients.
There is no U.S.-approved testosterone product for women, although its off-label use is on the rise, primarily to treat persistently low sexual desire related to menopause, the only condition for which medical guidelines and evidence of efficacy currently exist.
Some clinicians are also prescribing it for menopause-related symptoms such as fatigue and loss of muscle strength, despite a lack of medical guidance for that use.
The Menopause Society and half a dozen other medical organizations only back testosterone use for hypoactive sexual desire disorder in menopausal women.
"We do not have any evidence in women that it will improve muscle mass or bone density or mood or well-being or really any other metric," said Dr. Stephanie Faubion, director of Mayo Clinic's Center for Women's Health and medical director of The Menopause Society.
"What testosterone is being touted for in the lay public and on social media is not really what we have data for," she said.
SURGING PRESCRIPTIONS
Testosterone's rise comes as more women turn to hormone therapy overall, also driven by social media and, more recently, by the FDA, which backed estrogen and progesterone use for menopause symptoms as safe and effective. Some clinicians prescribe testosterone alongside these therapies.
An exclusive data analysis for Reuters by healthcare analytics firm Truveta shows the rate of women receiving a testosterone prescription jumped 146%, nearly tripling, from January 2023 to July 2026.
The U.S. Food and Drug Administration will hold a public workshop on Thursday to review evidence for testosterone use in menopausal women, which has been promoted by Health Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. and Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth, who has made an issue of testosterone levels among the troops.
While healthcare providers can prescribe approved medicines any way they see fit, an FDA spokesperson said these off-label uses have not been evaluated by the agency for safety, efficacy or dosing.
WORKAROUNDS FOR WOMEN
With no approved testosterone for women, patients must divide packaged versions designed for men often at many times the doses they're seeking.
Jennifer Lanoff, a women's health nurse practitioner in Washington, D.C., said Amazon Pharmacy rejected prescriptions from her practice for the first time this year.
"We've done (off-label testosterone prescriptions) forever. It's clinical guidelines recommended. Amazon has not given us a problem before," she said. "I don't know exactly what happened or why they rejected. They say it was a new policy."
Lanoff said she has had some success rewriting prescriptions under which patients discard leftover gel rather than splitting doses for later use.
Amazon Pharmacy said it does not dispense some testosterone prescriptions that require women to customize doses in order to ensure safe and compliant dispensing. It added that the policy is not specific to testosterone or based on a patient's sex or gender identity.
The company said it refers customers to compounding pharmacies that mix ingredients for specialized doses.
Compounded testosterone can cost about $100 for a three-month supply compared with $30 after discounts for a 10-month supply at a commercial pharmacy, Lanoff said.
Most insurance companies do not cover testosterone for women, the doctors, nurses and patients said.
Magen Price, a nurse practitioner and founder of Mystic Valley Sexual Wellness, said more than one-third of the 80 patients in her practice who currently use testosterone have encountered issues with their prescriptions, including at CVS and Walgreens pharmacies.
The issues that began last year have worsened, Price told Reuters.
When Amy Swan, a 55-year-old graphic designer outside Boston, tried to fill her first testosterone gel prescription in March, she expected a simple pharmacy visit.
Instead, she spent weeks trying to get a pharmacy to honor a GoodRx discount coupon that would reduce the cost by 25%.
"Hours of my time were lost just being able to get this one prescription," Swan said.
CVS said its pharmacies regularly fill prescriptions for topical testosterone gel for women. Other retailers with large pharmacies including Walgreens, Costco and Walmart did not respond to requests for comment. Kroger declined to comment.
Kevin Schipper, a pharmacist and director of product lifecycle management at consulting firm ProPharma, said pharmacies may decline prescriptions due to concerns over dosing accuracy and medication safety, as well as stability, storage and contamination if single-use packaging is opened and reused.
Because it is a controlled substance — similar to aspirin with codeine — each state also has record-keeping requirements for the government.
TRIALS FOR SAFETY
Over 30 trials studying testosterone use for various health conditions in women are underway or planned, according to ClinicalTrials.gov, including a Marius Pharmaceuticals-sponsored sexual dysfunction trial due to start as soon as later this year.
Aviva Bio is developing a next-generation testosterone product that would address breast cancer risk associated with use of the hormone.
Products currently used by women include branded and generic testosterone gels, such as AbbVie's AndroGel, Keenova Therapeutics' Testim and Upsher-Smith's Vogelxo.
Upsher-Smith declined to comment on whether it plans to develop testosterone products specifically for women. AbbVie and Keenova did not respond to requests for comment.
Cynthia Stuenkel, a clinical professor of medicine at the University of California, San Diego, said she hopes the FDA will prioritize evaluating a testosterone product designed for women.
"This would certainly ease the ability of clinicians to more confidently prescribe testosterone and for women to more easily and confidently use testosterone specifically dosed for them," she said. REUTERS

Gault&Millau honours Belgium’s top chocolatiers
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:11:51 UTC
22:11:51 KST

Just four months after opening, Chocolatier O in Geel has been named Discovery of the Year by the Gault&Millau restaurant guide. The jury praised the combination of refined flavours and striking appearance in Olivier Vandoninck’s pralines.
Vandoninck worked as a marketer before opening his chocolate shop. “I think that experience as a marketer has helped me to think more carefully about what the customer wants,” he says. “It’s not just about chocolate or creating the best filling, but about the overall experience. After all, most people give pralines as a gift to someone.”
For its 10th edition, Gault&Millau selected the 145 best chocolatiers in Belgium and Luxembourg. A jury assessed the flavour, texture, creativity, finish and presentation of their creations in a series of blind tastings.
It’s not just about chocolate or creating the best filling, but about the overall experience
Vandoninck’s pralines stood out because of their colourful exterior. The colours are always matched to the filling, and Vandoninck keeps his range limited to 12 flavours.
“I’m doing everything on my own and I want to be able to offer products that are as fresh as possible,” he says. “When I used to visit a chocolatier, I’d always get choice overload when I had to choose between 40 flavours. That’s why I keep it simple for the customer.”
Flavours range from a traditional hazelnut praline to salted caramel or calamansi – an Asian citrus fruit – with ginger.
“The aim was certainly to be included in Gault&Millau,” he says. “But to not only be featured in the guide within four months, but also to be named Discovery of the Year, is truly the reward for all the hard work.”
Gault&Millau also selected a Chocolatier of the Year for Flanders, Brussels and Wallonia: Chocolatier M in Knokke, Arthur Amblard in Brussels and Deremiens, Prouvy & Jamoigne in Chiny.
Amblard trained as a baker and pastry chef at Ceria in Anderlecht. The 28-year-old has a workshop in the Zavel district where he makes chocolate with no added sugar, inspired by his father, who has diabetes.
“Without compromising on pure taste, Arthur Amblard proves that innovation and craftsmanship can go hand in hand,” the Gault&Millau guide says.

El Nino reaches super status: Where will it bring rain, drought and heat?
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:10:26 UTC
22:10:26 KST

El Nino reaches super status: Where will it bring rain, drought and heat?
Forecasters say the Pacific Ocean warming pattern could be the strongest on record. Here is what that means for you.
A powerful El Nino is taking shape in the Pacific, and it is reshaping weather across the globe.
El Nino is a natural climate pattern caused by warmer-than-usual water in the central and eastern Pacific.
Data from the United States National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration (NOAA), released on September 14, 2026, shows El Nino has breached the temperature rise threshold of 2C (3.6F), thus becoming “very strong”, informally known as a super El Nino.
This year’s El Nino began developing in June 2026 and is expected to peak by early 2027, with forecasters warning it could be one of the strongest ever recorded. NOAA’s Climate Prediction Center expects it to persist through March-May 2027.
According to the World Meteorological Organization (WMO), El Nino happens every two to seven years. It usually starts between March and June, peaks between November and February, and can last up to 18 months.
How could El Nino affect your country?
A very strong El Nino tends to bring heavier rain to East Africa, South America and the southern US, drought to South Asia, Southeast Asia, Australia and Southern Africa, as well as warmer temperatures almost everywhere.
Select your country from the dropdown below to see where rainfall is likely to increase or decrease.
Where could it rain more?
El Nino typically brings heavier rain to East Africa, South America and the southern US.
East Africa is expecting heavier rain than usual between October and December. The IGAD Climate Prediction and Applications Centre, based in Ngong, Kenya, puts the chance of wetter conditions in southern Ethiopia, central and southern Somalia, and northeastern Kenya at 90 percent. These rains can supply up to 70 percent of the region’s annual rainfall, so heavier rain also means a higher risk of flooding.
El Nino typically brings more rain to parts of South America and the southern US too, with wetter conditions expected along the coasts of Ecuador and northern Peru in early 2027, then spreading to southern Brazil, Uruguay and northeastern Argentina between September 2026 and January 2027.
Where could it turn drier?
El Nino typically brings drier conditions to South Asia, Southeast Asia, Australia and Southern Africa.
India and Pakistan’s summer monsoon has fallen short. Rainfall across India was 15 percent below average between June 1 and September 9, according to the India Meteorological Department.
Indonesia faces a drier season and higher wildfire risk. Its meteorological agency, BMKG, recorded 5,019 hotspots by late July, with Sumatra and Kalimantan at the highest risk.
Australia’s Bureau of Meteorology expects below-average rainfall across southern and eastern parts, with warmer temperatures raising the risk of wildfires.
The South African Weather Service expects below-normal rainfall and above-normal temperatures across most of the country through spring and summer.
How could El Nino affect food supplies?
The World Food Programme (WFP) warns that El Nino could push at least 49 million more people into acute hunger by the end of 2027.
That would raise the total across the 45 countries it analysed from 225 million to 274 million. Eastern and Southern Africa and Latin America face the largest increases.
The United Nations Food and Agriculture Organization says maize, rice and other rain-fed crops are most at risk. Dry conditions threaten harvests from Southern Africa and the Sahel to South and Southeast Asia.
“El Nino is a massive threat to the food security of millions who are already vulnerable,” said Carl Skau, WFP’s acting executive director.
Will El Nino make it hotter?
Yes. The phenomenon usually warms the planet, with the biggest effect felt the year after it develops.
The WMO expects warmer-than-normal temperatures almost everywhere from September to November. It also sees an 86 percent chance that at least one year between now and 2030 beats 2024 as the hottest on record.
“There is an El Nino predicted for the end of 2026, which increases the chances of the following year, 2027, being the next record-breaking year,” said Leon Hermanson, who led the forecast.
What about snow?
El Nino’s biggest effect on North American winters comes through the jet stream – the fast, high-altitude winds that steer storms.
During El Nino, the strongest part of this jet shifts south and stretches farther east, pushing storms across the southern third of the continent.
NOAA scientists call El Nino “the great snowfall suppressor” for most of the continent.
In past El Nino winters, snowfall dropped below average around the Great Lakes, interior New England, the northern Rockies, the Pacific Northwest, western Canada and Alaska. The southern US, especially at higher elevations, tended to get more.
NOAA notes these are past patterns, not a forecast, and can vary widely year to year.
The link is weaker in Europe. The United Kingdom’s Met Office expects a higher chance of wetter, stormier conditions in Northwest Europe this autumn and early winter, while the European Centre for Medium-Range Weather Forecasts has linked El Nino to colder late winters in Northern Europe.
What else could change?
A strong El Nino usually calms the Atlantic hurricane season. NOAA’s August outlook gave a 75 percent chance of a below-normal season, noting that every strong event in recent history has been linked to below-average activity.
Forecasters stress that no two El Nino events are alike. The WMO warns that the strength of an event does not translate directly into the scale of its impacts in any single region, since conditions in the Indian and Atlantic oceans also play a role.
“El Nino is being supersized before our eyes,” UN Secretary-General Antonio Guterres said in a WMO statement. “The science leaves no room for doubt: the planet is in uncharted waters, and those waters are heating up.”

Saudi crown prince lands in Egypt to meet Sisi
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:08:30 UTC
22:08:30 KST

Breadcrumb
Saudi crown prince lands in Egypt to meet Sisi: Egyptian state-linked media
Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman arrived in Cairo on Tuesday ahead of meetings with Egyptian President Abdel Fattah al-Sisi, Egyptian state-linked media said, as the kingdom vowed to respond to attacks by Yemen's Houthis.
The regional powers are expected to discuss the threat posed by Houthi control over the Bab al-Mandeb Strait, a key shipping route vital to both Saudi oil exports and Egypt's Suez Canal revenues.
The de facto Saudi ruler "arrived in Egypt on an official visit", reported Al-Qahera News, which is close to Egyptian intelligence services. Riyadh's foreign ministry said Sisi received the crown prince for a "working visit".
Last week, the Houthis seized control of Yemen's Red Sea coast and the Bab al-Mandeb Strait, threatening the last direct route to Asian markets for the world's largest oil exporter.
Bab al-Mandeb, the southern gateway to the Red Sea and Egypt's Suez Canal, has become essential for Saudi oil exports this year, with the Strait of Hormuz blocked by the US-Israeli war on Iran.
Since the Houthis declared a naval blockade on the kingdom in July, Saudi Arabia has relied more heavily on Egypt to export oil, chiefly through its overland Sumed pipeline.
Crude is transported from the Saudi Red Sea port of Yanbu to Egypt's Ain Sokhna, and carried overland through the pipeline to the eastern Mediterranean at Sidi Kerir.
But a recent drone attack, which Riyadh said was launched from Iraq, forced it to shut the East-West pipeline that feeds Yanbu, pushing global oil prices above the $100 threshold.
Disruption to Bab al-Mandeb could land Egypt with heavy losses too, with Cairo reliant on Suez Canal tolls for crucial foreign revenue.
Revenues from the waterway are currently around half of what they were before the Houthis started attacking ships in 2023, in what they said was an act of solidarity with Palestinians during Israel's genocidal war on Gaza.
Egyptian Foreign Minister Badr Abdelatty said last week Cairo's Suez Canal losses stood at $11 billion.

Guidance: JSP 769: Zero Tolerance to Sexual Exploitation and Abuse

JSP 769: Zero Tolerance to Sexual Exploitation and Abuse
A guide for all Ministry of Defence service and civilian personnel on preventing and addressing sexual exploitation and abuse.
Documents
Details
JSP 769 directs efforts to prevent and address sexual exploitation or abuse by Service personnel and Civilian employees acting on behalf of Defence, and outlines appropriate assistance and redress that is available to victims. It includes a prohibition on transactional sex at all times when conducting Defence activity outside of the UK.

First entirely newly built municipal school in Sofia in 30 years opens doors
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:08:24 UTC
22:08:24 KST

News
First entirely newly built municipal school in Sofia in 30 years opens doors
Tuesday 15 September 2026 15:23
Tuesday, 15 September 2026, 15:23
PHOTO BTA
Font size
A site in Sofia designated decades ago for a Home for Bulgarians Abroad is now giving more than 400 children the opportunity to attend a new school, starting on September 15, 2026. The newly built 204th Primary School “Angel Karaliychev” opened its doors in the capital’s new Manastirski Livadi neighbourhood, on the site of the unfinished Home for Bulgarians Abroad.
The decision to build a Home for Bulgarians Abroad, called “Rodna Stryakha” (Native Hearth), was taken in 1981 as part of the celebrations marking 1,300 years of Bulgaria. Donations were collected from Bulgarians living abroad to fund the construction, but the building was never completed. The site was subsequently provided to Sofia Municipality by the “13 Centuries Bulgaria” National Endowment Fund for the construction of a school and kindergarten. Thus, a place originally conceived as a link with Bulgarians abroad has been given a new public purpose as an educational space for the next generation.
On the first day of the school year, Patriarch Daniil blessed the pupils, parents and teachers, stressing the role of education not only in imparting knowledge but also in shaping character.
Sofia Mayor Vasil Terziev attended the festive ceremony for the start of the school year, saying:
"Sofia is once again a city of knowledge, curiosity and new opportunities. This September 15 holds special significance for me and for Sofia. We welcomed the new school year at the 204th Primary School in the Manastirski Livadi-Zapad neighbourhood, the first entirely newly built municipal school in Sofia in the past 30 years. The school now also has its name, that of beloved Bulgarian writer Angel Karaliychev."
Edited by Miglena Ivanova, R. Petkova
This publication was created by: Rositsa Petkova

Russia drops to fourth place in terms of share of total value of gas imports to EU in July
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:08:21 UTC
22:08:21 KST

MOSCOW, September 15. /TASS/. Russia dropped to fourth place in terms of its share of the total value of EU gas imports totaling 14.1% in July from second place in the previous month, according to Eurostat data and TASS calculations.
In total, the European Union purchased 1.1 bln euros worth of gas from Russia in July.
The US took second place with its LNG supplies with 22.1% and 1.8 bln euro. Norway moved into first place with 23.5% and 1.9 bln euro. Algeria ranked third with a 15.9% share and the volumes of supplies to Europe worth 1.3 bln euro. The UK took fifth place with 11.4% and 905 mln euro.
Overall, from January to July 2026, Russia’s share of EU gas imports stood at 17% (ranking third among all suppliers), compared to 16.7% in the previous year. Total EU purchases of Russian gas during this period fell by 2.6% to 8.6 bln euro. The United States led in gas supplies to the European Union with a 27.9% share, followed by Norway in second place (18.3%), and Algeria in fourth (16.1%).

US hosts Lebanon, Israel envoys for talks in Washington
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:08:03 UTC
22:08:03 KST

Breadcrumb
Lebanon, Israel envoys to meet in Washington as south burns
Lebanon's and Israel's ambassadors to Washington are expected to meet at the US State Department on Tuesday, as Israel continues its attacks and occupation in southern Lebanon and questions grow in Beirut over the value of negotiations that have so far failed to secure an Israeli withdrawal.
Lebanese Ambassador Nada Mouawad and Israeli Ambassador Yechiel Leiter are expected to discuss recent developments and the implementation of a US-sponsored framework agreed between Lebanon and Israel in June.
Lebanese official sources told The New Arab that the meeting was intended to keep communication between the two sides open following recent setbacks in negotiations. They said no decisions or agreements were expected to emerge from the talks.
Lebanon will reiterate its demand for an end to Israeli attacks and military operations, including the bulldozing, destruction, demolition and booby-trapping of areas in the south, the sources said.
Beirut is also expected to push for agreement on a second "pilot zone" to be handed over to the Lebanese army.
The meeting comes despite growing doubts in Lebanon over negotiations with Israel, which has continued military operations inside the country while refusing to fully withdraw from Lebanese territory.
A US State Department official said earlier that the ambassadors would discuss "current developments" and the continued implementation of the trilateral framework, describing it as the only viable mechanism for restoring Lebanese sovereignty and Israeli security.
The next round of formal negotiations is due to take place in Rome in October. The eighth round of negotiations between Lebanon and Israel had been scheduled for September but was postponed until October.
A Lebanese government source told TNA that Israel had shown little enthusiasm for negotiations and continued to favour military action in southern Lebanon.
"Israel refuses to withdraw from Lebanon and insists on remaining in the south," the source said, adding that Israel sought to maintain its presence for as long as possible as part of a wider regional project.
US ambassador visits southern Lebanon
Against this backdrop, US Ambassador to Beirut Michel Issa visited southern Lebanon on Tuesday accompanied by representatives from the Lebanese government's Council for Development and Reconstruction.
Arriving under heavy security, Issa visited the Our Lady of the Annunciation Monastery in Zawtar al-Gharbiya in Nabatiyeh district. An Israeli drone reportedly flew overhead during the visit.
Issa later stopped in Qaaqaaiyat al-Jisr, where he inspected a newly constructed bridge replacing one destroyed in an Israeli airstrike.
The visit comes as Washington seeks an expanded role in shaping post-war security arrangements in southern Lebanon while continuing to back Israel, whose forces remain inside Lebanese territory.
Issa, who is of Lebanese origin, recently met Lebanon's vice president of the Supreme Islamic Shia Council, Sheikh Ali Al-Khatib. The meeting was viewed by some as an attempt by Washington to engage Lebanon's Shia community while maintaining its campaign against Hezbollah and pressing for the group's full disarmament.
Hezbollah is designated a terrorist organisation by the United States. The group has rejected demands to surrender its weapons while Israeli forces continue to occupy Lebanese territory and carry out attacks.
Zawtar al-Gharbiya is among three villages where the Lebanese army deployed this summer under the June framework agreement. The US-sponsored plan calls for Israel to hand over designated "pilot zones" to the Lebanese army, which is then expected to ensure they have been cleared of Hezbollah weapons and military infrastructure.
Implementation has proceeded slowly amid continued Israeli attacks and occupation. Beirut has repeatedly demanded that Israel halt its military operations and withdraw from Lebanese territory.
Israeli attacks continue
The diplomatic activity comes as Israeli forces continue attacks across southern Lebanon.
Since midnight on Tuesday, Israel has carried out artillery shelling in Wadi Zibqin, al-Mansouri and Kfar Tibnit, as well as detonations in Kounine, Baraachit and al-Mansouri. Israeli operations have remained concentrated around Nabatiyeh despite the Lebanese army reinforcing its deployment in the area.
Fears have also grown over a possible Israeli push towards Nabatiyeh after Israeli forces captured the strategic Ali al-Taher ridge overlooking the city.
Israel's military currently occupies a border zone extending several kilometres into Lebanon, which it calls the "Yellow Line". Dozens of Lebanese towns and villages in the area have been destroyed, preventing large numbers of displaced residents from returning home.
In al-Bayyada in the Tyre district, Israeli forces have been burning what remains of homes, according to Lebanon's state-run National News Agency, while the nearby village of al-Mansouri has faced months of attacks.
On Monday, a resident of Baraachit was killed after Israeli forces opened fire with machine guns in the area.
According to Lebanon's Health Ministry, more than 4,300 people have been killed since all-out fighting between Israel and Hezbollah resumed on 2 March. Since the conflict began in October 2023, close to 9,000 people have been killed in Lebanon and more than 30,000 wounded.

Coalition talks likely as no party dominates Philippines’ Bangsamoro region
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15

Coalition talks likely as no party dominates Philippines’ Bangsamoro region
With 98.56 percent of party-list votes counted, no party has received a majority, according to unofficial results.
Unofficial election results in the Muslim-majority autonomous region of Bangsamoro in the southern Philippines suggest no single party is on course to dominate parliament, meaning that coalition talks may be needed to choose the region’s first chief minister.
The election has been largely shaped by rival camps within the Moro Islamic Liberation Front (MILF), a former separatist movement that negotiated autonomy with the Philippine government.
Recommended Stories
list of 3 items
- list 1 of 3Philippines set for historic vote in Bangsamoro after decades of conflict
- list 2 of 3BRICS meets in India: What’s on the agenda, why it matters
- list 3 of 3Philippines ferry fire death toll rises to 35 after bodies recovered
One camp, the United Bangsamoro Justice Party (UBJP), is led by longtime Front chairman Al Haj Murad Ebrahim, while the other, the Bangsamoro Federalist Party, is led by Abdulraof Macacua, a former Front commander appointed interim chief by Philippine President Marcos Jr in 2025.
According to partial and unofficial results from the Parish Pastoral Council for Responsible Voting, a church-backed election watchdog, 98.56 percent of the party-list votes have been counted so far.
According to the unofficial results, Macacua’s camp is leading the vote with 35.2 percent, ahead of Murad’s bloc at 32.4 percent.
The BARMM Grand Coalition, the third party on the ballot, has gained 20.5 percent of the vote so far and could emerge as a key player in balancing power in coalition negotiations.
Reporting from Marawi in the southern Philippines, Al Jazeera’s Barnaby Lo said the unofficial results suggest a hung parliament is likely.
“The parties will now have to negotiate to form a coalition government in order to move forward,” Lo said.
According to election officials, the election turnout was about 80 percent, with about 1.8 million votes cast from 2.39 million registered voters.
Voters are electing party, district and sectoral representatives who will form the 80-member parliament tasked with selecting the region’s first elected chief minister, in a vote widely viewed as the most significant milestone since a 2014 peace agreement between Manila and the Front.

European states mull prison terms for settlement trade: Israeli media
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:07:11 UTC
22:07:11 KST

A man walks past placards during an anti-Israel demonstration outside the Houses of Parliament in London. (AFP Photo)
European countries are moving toward prison sentences that could be as long as 10 years for individuals and companies involved in trade with Israeli illegal settlements built on occupied Palestinian land, Israeli media has reported.
Norway is preparing a legal framework to boycott settlement products, including prison sentences of up to three years for individuals or companies dealing with them, reports said on Sept. 14.
A channel quoted Norwegian Foreign Minister Espen Barth Eide as saying he expected his country’s parliament to approve the draft law in its current form.
“Yes, I really think so, and the arguments have become stronger as the situation in the West Bank has worsened,” Eide said in response to a question on the issue.
“There are many other countries that will do the same thing that the government has already proposed through this law,” he added.
According to the reports, the Netherlands is also working on a similar law, under which the penalty could be as long as six years in prison.
A Dutch decision banning the import, purchase or sale of goods from illegal settlements is expected to take effect on Sept. 22, it said.
The media said that in Britain, the penalty for commercial dealings with settlements could reach up to 10 years in prison.
The moves come after 12 Western countries announced last week their intention to impose restrictions on trade with Israeli settlements.
The countries are France, Britain, Canada, Denmark, Finland, Iceland, Ireland, Norway, Poland, Portugal, Spain and Sweden.
These 12 countries called on Israel to immediately halt settlement expansion and violence by occupiers.

Lai backs Taiwan’s major AI infrastructure drive
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:06:16 UTC
22:06:16 KST

TAIPEI (Taiwan News) — President Lai Ching-te (賴清德) said Tuesday that Taiwan was advancing the Ten Major AI Infrastructure Projects, which aim to train at least 500,000 AI professionals by 2040.
Lai said the economy grew 8.76% last year and 14.15% in the first half of this year, its strongest first-half performance in five decades, per CNA. He said full-year growth was expected to exceed 11%, which would be the best result in 39 years, while Taiwan could join the world’s 20 largest economies next year.
The AI projects will promote smart applications across industries and support the development of quantum computing, silicon photonics, and robotics. A NT$100 billion (US$3.2 billion) fund will help small and medium-sized businesses upgrade and improve their competitiveness.
Lai said the government was seeking more investment from Taiwanese and foreign companies. Its three major investment programs have been extended through next year and expanded to include companies worldwide.
Stronger economic growth has also increased tax revenue, allowing the government to devote more resources to national defense, Lai said.
The president said the government was expanding education support so children could pursue their ambitions regardless of their family income or where they lived. He added that Taiwan would deepen cooperation with countries worldwide, particularly Japan, Malaysia, and the Philippines.

Tallinn Fringe Festival 2026 now into its last week
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:06:00 UTC
22:06:00 KST

Tallinn Fringe Festival 2026 now into its last week
The Tallinn Fringe Festival (TFF) 2026 is into its final week now with performances running across the city until Friday.
This year's festival is the 10th and features comedy, theater, music, dance, circus and cabaret, with both international and Estonian artists appearing. The closing days offer audiences a last chance to see this year's stars.
Festival director Dan Renwick was hard at work publicizing the event through a series of personal challenges, including a Marathon "Joggle" — running and juggling simultaneously — with split across two half marathons in Helsinki and Tallinn.
"I wanted to find a slightly ridiculous way to draw some attention to the Fringe and encourage people to support what we're doing," Renwick said of his challenge. "It's been great to see people getting behind it, and if the challenges have made even a few more people curious about Tallinn Fringe or inspired them to support the festival, then it's been worth it," he went on.
Audiences can also support the festival directly during its final week. Financial support helps Tallinn Fringe continue providing performance opportunities for artists and bringing international acts to the city. Details are available from the festival's website.
TFF is an open-access festival, with performances staged in bars, theaters, clubs and other venues across the city. Its program is created through collaboration between artists and venues, allowing visitors to build their own festival experience. This year's event started August 18 and runs exactly one month, to this coming Friday, September 18.
The full remaining itinerary and ticket information are available on the Tallinn Fringe website here.

====================================================================================================

‘You’re Simply Forced to Sit Within These Four Walls’: Kyiv Residents Living Under Constant Drone Attacks
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:06:00 UTC
22:06:00 KST

For much of the war, Kyiv’s air-raid routine followed a grim but predictable rhythm: Russian drones arrived at night, missiles struck before dawn, and the city dusted itself off to restart in the morning.
That pattern has now been shattered by recent events.
JOIN US ON TELEGRAM
Follow our coverage of the war on the @Kyivpost_official.
Since Aug. 27, Russia has been sending waves of drones toward Kyiv throughout the day and night, including faster, jet-powered models and larger Geran-4 and Geran-5 drones, alongside frequent ballistic attacks.
There was only a brief pause on Sept. 6-7, when Russia and Ukraine agreed to a temporary hold on strikes on Moscow and Kyiv as US President Donald Trump’s envoys Jared Kushner and Steve Witkoff visited Moscow.
For those two days, Kyiv briefly returned to something resembling normal life.
Then the drones came back.
The result is not simply a city under attack. It is a city where the line between an emergency and everyday life is disappearing.
Air raid alerts can last for hours. The subway stops running across the Dnipro River. Bridges close to traffic. Schools move children into shelters. Kindergartens send toddlers into basements. Shops and cinemas shut. Public institutions suspend their work.
Every alert carries an economic cost: delayed workers, interrupted production, closed businesses, suspended deliveries and disrupted public transport.
Russia is also targeting the logistics networks that keep Kyiv and other Ukrainian cities supplied, with warehouses, delivery infrastructure, and gas stations increasingly coming under attack.
Russian Casualties in 2026 Top 300,000, Ukraine Says
For Kyiv residents, the consequences are deeply personal.
“That is how we live every day”
Viola lives in Kyiv with her nine-year-old daughter, Oleksandra, while her husband serves in the military in southern Ukraine.
Her daughter started fourth grade in September. Viola says she feels safer knowing the girl is at school than she would on the first floor of their apartment building.
But the school spends much of the day in its shelter.
“There can be up to 10 air raid alerts a day,” Viola said.
She works from home, which makes the situation somewhat easier. But staying in or leaving the apartment has become a calculation.
Before going anywhere, she checks news channels and the Shahed flight map. If drones are not heading towards their area, she and her daughter leave for school, the store, the post office or wherever they need to go.
The most frightening moments come when the warning arrives after they have already left.
Recently, Viola and her daughter were returning from school when she received a report that a Shahed was entering their area. They were near a car wash, garages and a large shopping mall.
“There were practically no reliable shelters and nowhere to hide,” she said.
They simply walked faster toward home, listening for the drone and looking for somewhere they could take cover if air defenses began firing.
“That is how we live every day.”
At home, the family has adapted to the possibility of a major attack. Viola keeps an emergency backpack packed with documents, medication, masks, a flashlight, emergency blankets, tourniquets, spare keys, food and water.
Shoes are kept nearby in case they need to evacuate quickly. So are carriers for the family cats. The car is kept fueled.
Even ordinary household routines now require preparation.
“We have to check that all your gadgets, EcoFlow, lamps and batteries are charged,” she said.
Her daughter also has her own ways of coping. They play board games, read books, watch movies downloaded from Netflix, go for walks with friends and light candles and fairy lights.
But the exhaustion remains.
“You want to sleep through the whole night in your own bed, plan trips outside without looking at air raid maps, and have children study somewhere other than shelters and freely live their childhood,” Viola said.
“For now, we are adapting to constant change.”
‘You Aren’t Able to Hear the Teacher’
For 15-year-old Maria, the disruption is most visible at school.
She studies at the lyceum, where an air raid alert used to mean one thing: classes stopped and students went to the shelter.
The shelter is small and crowded, its conditions are far from comfortable.
“The toilet in the shelter is not great. It is dirty and always smells,” Maria said.
A year ago, she said, there was no toilet at all – just a bucket and a curtain.
Students are not allowed to leave school on their own during an air raid alert. Parents have to collect them.
Maria now follows a blended schedule, spending one week studying remotely and one week at school.
During remote learning, classes continue under a lower-level alert as long as students are in safe places. If the danger level rises to red, the lessons end.
In-person lessons are supposed to continue in the shelter. Maria does not see how that can work.
“Everyone is shouting and you’re not able to hear the teacher,” she said.
The shelter is also hot and poorly ventilated.
“Sometimes I feel sick if we have to sit there for two hours or more,” she said.
‘We Feel Guilty’
For parents of younger children, the choice can be even harder. Liubov, 34, is an English teacher and the mother of a four-year-old daughter.
Her daughter has barely attended kindergarten over the past three weeks because children can spend almost the entire day in the shelter.
Liubov’s daughter attends a private kindergarten where children have lessons, meals and activities even while sheltering. Compared with many other children in Kyiv, their situation is relatively good.
But the parents still feel guilty.
“We as parents still feel guilty about making her stay there all day,” Liubov said.
Whenever they can, they keep their daughter home.
Both Liubov and her husband work from home, so they take turns looking after their child and trying to keep up with work.
“It’s hard because the work still needs to be done, and juggling everything is challenging,” she said.
They also try to take their daughter outside every day, but stay close to home so they can return quickly if a major attack begins.
When they hear explosions or air raid alerts, they try not to panic in front of the child – but they have also stopped pretending that the noise is simply thunder.
The girl has begun to ask questions about what is happening in Ukraine.
“It’s a difficult balance – trying to give her a sense of safety and normality while knowing that we can’t completely shield her from what is going on around us,” Liubov said.
‘I’m Already Running Out of Strength’
For Anastasia, 31, the past three weeks have been almost unbearable.
Her four-and-a-half-year-old daughter attends kindergarten, but the facility does not have a properly equipped shelter.
During alerts, the children cross the road to a residential building and shelter in its basement. That was manageable when it happened occasionally.
It is very different when children spend four or five hours there at a time, which since Aug. 27, Anastasia said, has become the reality.
When she realized the situation was not improving, she took her daughter out of kindergarten and began keeping her at home.
That solved one problem and created another as Anastasia works full time.
“Being with a child 24/7 and maintaining my functionality and effectiveness at work is extremely difficult,” she said.
“So far, thank God, I’ve managed. But I can already feel that I’m running out of strength.”
Her daughter is also struggling with being confined to the apartment.
Anastasia cannot simply take her outside during the workday. Even after work, going out may be too dangerous when drones are moving over the city in large numbers.
At home, she says, at least there are concrete walls between them and falling debris. Outside, there is nowhere to know for certain that you are safe.
The disruption has spread into the most ordinary parts of life.
Going grocery shopping requires planning. Going to a shopping mall has become difficult. Even ordering a package can feel risky as Russian attacks increasingly target logistics infrastructure.
Sometimes, Anastasia said, the situation creates a feeling of absolute despair.
“You see empty shelves in stores, you can’t take your child to kindergarten or any extracurricular activities, you can’t take her anywhere to have fun, not even to a children’s play area.”
“You’re simply forced to sit within these four walls, look at each other and wait for all of this to end.”
“And whether it will actually end – that’s completely unclear.”
‘Now Half of My Family Became Refugees’
For Mariia, 32, the constant attacks eventually forced a decision about where her family could live.
Leva arrived in Kyiv on Aug. 23, initially planning to stay for about a month.
The first few days were unusually quiet. She said the calm coincided with Russia putting on a show of restraint around the visit of foreign diplomats.
Being back home felt good.
She wanted to spend more time with her family and her one-year-old niece. She began considering staying longer, or even partially moving back.
Then the attacks intensified.
Her family has now left Kyiv for Uzhhorod, where they found an apartment and moved out within days.
The family still owns an apartment in Kyiv. They want to come home.
But they do not believe it is safe to raise a small child there under the current conditions.
“It’s impossible to sleep here with kids because parents worry about their safety nonstop,” Leva said.
The alternative, she said, is spending night after night in the metro or a dusty basement – and even that does not guarantee safety.
Her brother and sister-in-law made the decision to leave with their young daughter.
“They want to come back home,” Leva said. “Just because they like it here and because they want my niece to grow up with her grandparents, aunts and uncle, cousins around.”
But for now, they are gone.
“Now half of my family became refugees.”
Her mother and younger brother left for Germany after the full-scale invasion began.
Leva says there is something particularly painful about the fact that being able to leave is itself a privilege.
“I’m supposed to be happy that my family can be in a safer place now. Because for most of the people there is no such an option.”
“But I just want them to be home, because they want to be home.”
A City That Cannot Stop
The effects are not limited to families with young children.
Myroslava, a culture correspondent at Kyiv Post, said a Sept. 2 Russian drone attack on the National University of Food Technologies came while classes were taking place.
Some 160 students and faculty members were unharmed because they were in the shelter.
But the attack reopened a broader question for Kyiv’s universities: should students continue attending classes in person when the city is under near-constant attack?
The decision is left to individual institutions.
For Myroslava’s family, the question was personal. Her daughter is a fourth-year architecture student at the Kyiv National University of Construction and Architecture in Solomianskyi District, one of the areas facing a high level of threat.
Until recently, professors had required students to attend in person.
Only on Sept. 9 did the university administration decide to move classes online for a limited period until Sept. 25.
The same pressure is being felt across Kyiv’s cultural life.
Museums and theaters have struggled to operate under the dense schedule of air raid alerts.
Myroslava and her family eventually managed to attend an anniversary exhibition dedicated to composer Borys Liatoshynsky at Ukrainian House – on their fourth attempt.
The previous attempts were disrupted by security concerns.
Relearning the lessons of survival
For much of the war, Kyivans learned how to live with air raid alerts.
People knew what to do when the sirens sounded. They knew where to shelter. They knew that eventually the alert would end, the trains would start to run again, shops would reopen and the city would return to work.
Now that rhythm is breaking down. The question for Kyiv residents is no longer just how to get through one more night of attacks.
It is how to live when the next interruption can come at any moment – and when there may be no clear end to the interruption at all.

Former spokesman of Greek ultra-nationalist party Golden Dawn, Ilias Kasidiaris, speaks to his supporters after his release from prison. (AFP Photo)
The former spokesman of Greek neo-Nazi party Golden Dawn, Ilias Kasidiaris, was released from prison on Sept. 15 and vowed a return to politics, having already drawn attention from Elon Musk.
Kasidiaris, 45, had been handed a 13-year sentence as a leading member of the violent outfit that for years carried out beatings of migrants and political opponents, and the 2013 murder of anti-fascist rapper Pavlos Fyssas.
Raising his fist and holding a folded Greek flag, Kasidiaris said he was returning to a “new, strong national party, such as those dominating all of Europe.”
“Today I am back. And from what I see, I am not alone,” he said outside Domokos prison in central Greece.
He called his incarceration “an underhanded and dirty conspiracy” by his political opponents, and added that he would announce the name of his new party on Sept. 14.
Dozens of cheering supporters, several holding Greek flags, were waiting for him.
“Ilias, we are with you,” many chanted. “We will make you prime minister.”
Formerly a Golden Dawn MP from 2012 to 2019, Kasidiaris was jailed for six years and worked off the rest of his sentence while behind bars.
He had previously spent a year in pre-trial detention in 2014 before his incarceration in 2020.
Even during his incarceration, the former food chemist never ceased his political activity on social media, particularly on his YouTube channel, which has 162,000 subscribers.
In July he drew the attention of Musk, whom he has praised as “the most eminent personality in the world.”
The multibillionaire, who backs far-right groups in Europe including AfD in Germany, noted on X on July 17 that Kasidiaris’ continued incarceration was “messed up.”

Palestinian woman prisoner gives birth in Israeli detention
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:02:37 UTC
22:02:37 KST

Breadcrumb
Palestinian woman prisoner gives birth in Israeli detention amid continued abuse, medical neglect
The Palestinian Commission of Detainees and Ex-Detainees Affairs said on Monday that a Palestinian woman prisoner had given birth to a baby girl inside Israel’s Damon Prison, where there have been recent reports of abuse and torture.
In a statement, the commission identified the detainee as 35-year-old Dana Joudeh from the town of Iraq al-Tayeh near Nablus in the northern West Bank.
It added that Joudeh is scheduled to be released on Tuesday together with her newborn daughter, following the completion of a six-month administrative detention order. She is now reportedly in a hospital in Ramallah.
Israel has placed hundreds of Palestinian prisoners in administrative detention without charge or trial. The orders can be renewed indefinitely.
The Palestinian Prisoners’ Society reported that Dana Joudeh has another child and was detained while pregnant and then placed in detention for six months. 
The Commission of Detainees and Ex-Detainees Affairs said that detaining Joudeh while she was pregnant and placing her under administrative detention, “left her to struggle through her pregnancy without any health or medical care, and constitutes a crime that violates all international norms and laws.”
The commission added that “forcing her to give birth to her baby inside the prison constitutes a double crime that violates all humanitarian values.” It said that the Israeli prison administration “deals brutally” with female Palestinian prisoners, adding that what Joudeh experienced was an example of this.
“How can this organized terrorism continue its practices in this way without the international community taking any action? Will the occupying state [Israel] continue to remain above international laws and conventions?”
Last June, the Palestinian Prisoners’ Media Office reported that women prisoners held at Damon were being subjected to physical abuse and torture, including being forced to lie down on the ground while their hands were tied behind their back and beatings were administered.
Women are also given poor-quality food and are neglected medically in Israeli prisons.
According to the Palestinian Prisoner’s Society, more than 9,400 Palestinians are currently held in Israeli prisons, including 370 children and 92 women.

Paris ex-mayor says 'collective failure' led to wave of child abuse
PARIS, Sept 15 - Former Paris Mayor Anne Hidalgo said on Tuesday "collective failure" at institutions had opened the door to a wave of child abuse at state nurseries and primary schools in the French capital over more than a decade.
Police have been examining more than 200 allegations of mistreatment, violence and sexual abuse of young children during lunch breaks, rest periods and after-school activities.
Hidalgo told a Senate hearing the number of victims was unacceptable and that she understood the sense of abandonment amongst families.
'INSTITUTIONS FAILED TO PROTECT OUR CHILDREN'
"If we are standing before you here today, it is because our institutions... despite the actions taken and despite a genuine awareness of the sexual abuse suffered by our children in this country, have failed to protect our children. And this is a collective failure," she told senators.
The first allegations of abuse surfaced in 2014. A year later, the Paris General Inspectorate, an inter-departmental supervisory body, published a report warning of the risk of such offences being committed by city employees against minors.
Parents groups say their concerns about abuse fell on deaf ears in City Hall for years. Paris Public Prosecutor Laure Beccuau told France Inter radio last week she has opened investigations into Hidalgo and current mayor Emmanuel Gregoire over their alleged failures to assist a person in danger of abuse.
On Tuesday afternoon a Paris court sentenced a former after-school activity supervisor to five years in prison with four years suspended.
The offender had admitted sexually assaulting nine girls aged six to 10 at a Paris elementary school.
Senator Agnes Evren, who led the Senate's hearing, listed shortcomings in City Hall's actions detailed in the Inspectorate's report, including a four-year pause in systematic reporting of complaints, and said the Inspectorate's recommendations had only been partially implemented.
Parents' collective SOS Periscolaire expressed disappointment at Hidalgo's answers, which it said failed to deliver facts.
"We are unfortunately not surprised by these answers, which always point to the failures and responsibilities of others, but never her own," SOS Periscolaire said in a statement.
Barka Zerouali, the cofounder of a second parents' group, #MeTooEcole, told Reuters: "Once we drop our child off at school they are supposed to be safe, all the more so when we're talking about a public service." REUTERS

South African police probe killings of seven women, Ramaphosa vows action
JOHANNESBURG, Sept 15 - South African police said on Tuesday they were investigating the deaths of seven women whose bodies were found near Johannesburg over the past two months, with some stripped of their clothes and showing signs of sexual violence.
South African President Cyril Ramaphosa has vowed "no stone will be left unturned" in the investigation into their deaths, which have caused widespread alarm.
The victims are mostly aged in their 20s or 30s, and one was identified as a woman who disappeared after leaving home for an afternoon run.
Violence against women is one of the country's most entrenched social problems, despite years of government pledges to tackle it.
Police are investigating whether the killings in and around Ekurhuleni, east of Johannesburg, were carried out by a single perpetrator, multiple suspects or copycat criminals.
Some bodies were found naked, others partially clothed. One body was decomposing when discovered.
The killings have sparked widespread condemnation from civil society and sporting bodies. Police have not yet established a motive for the killings.
South Africa has one of the highest murder rates in the world, averaging about 60 a day, according to official crime statistics. Violent crime is a persistent source of public anger and anxiety as the country prepares to hold municipal elections in November. REUTERS
