Luana Lopes Lara Is on the Frontlines of Evolving Prediction Markets
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:45 UTC
22:01:45 KST

Luana Lopes Lara is inherently an optimist—perhaps because Brazilians, she says, have a more positive outlook than people from other countries. That trait has served her well as chief operating officer of Kalshi, the New York-based prediction market that has boomed in recent years as people take up placing money on everything from what words the President will use in his speeches to the price of a Costco hot dog.
“I probably wouldn’t be here if I hadn’t had this Brazilian culture and background,” says Lopes Lara, 30. “I’m very optimistic and positive, but it’s not a blind optimism—it’s more like I believe that in the end, things will work out.” She still leans on some Brazilian traditions, like always trying to get out of bed on her right foot and then taking a few hops on it, eating lentils for New Year’s, and jumping over seven waves in the ocean for good luck.
Lopes Lara put her optimism to work when the company decided to sue Kalshi’s regulator, the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission, arguing that the company should be allowed to list political event contracts. Kalshi won the case in September 2024, which allowed the platform to feature the 2024 elections, with users favoring Donald Trump over Kamala Harris when polls were forecasting an even race. Since then, the company’s valuation has grown to $22 billion, and in December 2025, Lopes Lara unseated 31-year-old Scale cofounder Lucy Guo to become the world’s youngest self-made woman billionaire at 29, according to Forbes.
Lopes Lara’s parents, a math teacher and an electrical engineer, pushed their two daughters to be financially independent, so they would never need to rely on partners for money. But her parents’ vision of success included going to a public university in Brazil and getting a job as a doctor, engineer, or lawyer, she says. She wanted more for herself: “I always wanted to do bigger things.”
Lopes Lara was accepted into the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Harvard, Yale, and Stanford while she was performing as a ballet dancer in Austria, having trained at the only official affiliate school of the Russian Bolshoi Theater outside of Moscow. She chose MIT, she says, because people there seemed more passionate about what they were pursuing than students did at Harvard. It was at MIT that Lopes Lara got to know her Kalshi co-founder, Tarek Mansour, when he sat next to her in the first row in an algorithms class so he could learn from her, she says.
These days, Mansour handles many public-facing tasks as CEO and Lopes Lara handles internal operations. The role has forced her to unlearn some of the perfectionist tendencies she honed for so long as a ballerina, she says, where tilting your finger a centimeter in the wrong direction can ruin the aesthetics. “For a startup, you need the opposite,” she says. “You need to move very fast.”
The speed at which Kalshi is moving has caught the eye of regulators outside the federal government, resulting in court challenges in states including New York and Washington. So far, its federal regulator has remained steadfast in holding that Kalshi can legally operate. Lopes Lara says she believes Kalshi’s case will prevail—after all, it was a President Biden-appointed judge who first gave the company the green light to list elections, and it’s the Trump Administration that continues to support the company. “At the end of the day, it’s because we’re right on the law,” she says. Many state attorneys general disagree, making her position, unquestionably, what an optimist might say.

How Netflix Star Sofia Carson Brings Her Identity to Work
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

In the 2025 Netflix romance My Oxford Year, Sofia Carson plays Anna De La Vega, an American student studying abroad at one of England’s most prestigious universities who, like Carson herself, is of Latin American descent. Anna’s Colombian, Argentine, and Cuban heritage is woven naturally into her character’s everyday life instead of being treated as a barrier she has to overcome. It’s a distinction that Carson, who has four consecutive global #1 Netflix hits to her name, says is nonnegotiable when it comes to the roles she chooses to take on.
“All the Netflix films I have been a part of that have gone on to make streaming history have been led by myself, a Hispanic American actress telling the stories of Latin women,” says Carson, who also starred in the music-heavy romance Purple Hearts (2022), action thriller Carry-On (2024), and romantic drama The Life List (2025). “Within those stories, that heritage isn't a tattooed stereotype, but rather an organic part of existence.”
Born in Fort Lauderdale, Fla., to Colombian immigrant parents, Carson, 33, got her big break starring in the massively popular 2015 Disney Channel musical Descendants and its sequels. “I think back to my earliest memories and it wasn’t a choice that I made, it was always who I was,” Carson says of her lifelong Hollywood ambitions. “I remember being a little girl, as young as 3, and music, storytelling, performing, dancing, that was just what I did. It was undeniable.”
She has since parlayed that initial success into a diverse, multi-hyphenate career—establishing herself as one of Netflix’s most valuable movie stars, serving as an executive producer on several film projects, and building a recording catalog, including a self-titled album and songs from her films, that has surpassed 4 billion streams worldwide. Beyond her work on screen and in music, Carson is an advocate for young people’s education and empowerment through her roles as a U.S. Ambassador for UNICEF and the first global ambassador for the Latin GRAMMY Cultural Foundation.
“I recognized very early on in my career that I had a responsibility to use my voice to stand for goodness and to stand for justice,” she says.
Next up, Carson is set to co-star in the Wagner Moura-directed indie dramedy Last Night at the Lobster as part of an ensemble cast featuring award-winning actors like Elisabeth Moss and Brian Tyree Henry. “What’s most important to me is to shift the misunderstanding that to be a Latin woman is to look one way, to be one thing, to be one [type of] woman, body, skin color, accent,” she says. “In reality, we are a kaleidoscope of cultures, of talents, of thoughts, of passions. That has been such an important guiding light in my career.”

Mónica Ramírez Is Teaching Migrant Women How to Organize
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

Growing up around the rows of corn and soybeans in Fremont, Ohio, Mónica Ramírez spent her earliest days being looked after by her maternal bisabuela, or great-grandmother, Virginia Guardiola. From her home in town, Guardiola served as an informal, one-woman welcome committee for farmworkers who arrived in Fremont, disoriented and alone. Guardiola would tell these newcomers where to find jobs and fill them in on what they needed to know about Ohio.
From her great-grandmother, Ramírez says she learned “the importance of having information to share, so that people could actually get jobs—and get safe.” Ramírez saw the vulnerability of those who followed the harvest to a place like Fremont. Many were women living far from their families, who often worked without legal status, which made it risky for them to speak out against exploitative foremen in the fields or predators in the worker barracks. It mattered, she says, for them to have “trusted community members.”
Some 40 years later, Ramírez is an internationally recognized attorney and feminist, with degrees from Ohio State and Harvard. The founder of the advocacy group Justice for Migrant Women, Ramírez has made powerful allies, including celebrities like Eva Longoria and America Ferrera; she was Laura Dern’s guest at the Oscars in 2018. Yet to this day, she still lives within miles of the agricultural fields in Fremont.
When Ramírez founded Justice for Migrant Women in 2014, she’d spent a decade as an attorney helping farmworkers fight for their rights in court. But Justice for Migrant Women is not a legal aid clinic. Instead, it performs the service Ramírez saw her bisabuela perform in Fremont: it provides information. Ramírez helps workers access the knowledge they need to organize and fight for their own rights.
She sees herself more as a neighbor than a charity giver, and the neighborliness goes both ways. One of the women who most inspired Ramírez was a farmworker named Bernice, who had been sexually victimized in a labor camp and later evicted. Homeless and injured, Bernice decided to return to her home county rather than file a criminal complaint, despite Ramírez’s offer to fight for her. In all their calls, Bernice always began conversations by asking Ramírez the same question: “Are you happy?” For Ramírez, it was a model. Today, she says she starts every new conversation by considering the dignity of the person she is speaking with, taking account of the fullness of their life.
Ramírez sees a role her organization can play in the twists and turns of politics and culture, working to ensure that Justice for Migrant Women is there for people who need support as the political landscape shifts and new challenges arise. In 2017, as the Me Too movement took off, Ramírez helped write the “Dear Sisters” letter, which ran in TIME under the headline “700,000 Female Farmworkers Say They Stand With Hollywood Actors Against Sexual Assault.” It read, in part: “Even though we work in very different environments, we share a common experience of being preyed upon by individuals who have the power to hire, fire, and blacklist.” And amid the Trump Administration’s mass deportation campaign, as some farmworkers feel they have to choose between enduring sexual abuse in silence or coming forward and risking the trauma of detention and family separation, Ramírez is working in community meetings and trainings to help them understand their rights.
One of Ramírez’s projects, the “Humans Who Feed Us” dinner series, brings people together and highlights the interdependence of our broader communities. In Ohio and around the country, she sets large tables for farmworkers, line cooks, delivery drivers—immigrant workers who support the nation’s food system—and then invites business leaders and other community members to join them.
“We’re too far apart, and I think that that space between us is what’s leading to hate and misunderstanding,” Ramírez says. She sets the table the way her great-grandmother would for any newcomer in town. “Back in the day,” she says, “people relied on their neighbors.”

====================================================================================================

Ana Valdez Is Making Business Leaders Recognize Latino Economic Power
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

Sometimes, when Ana Valdez walks into a C-Suite office and introduces herself, she can see the person taking in her Mexican accent and her last name with some surprise. “They were expecting somebody different,” Valdez says. It can get “old, and a little annoying” to re-introduce herself—to explain she’s a CEO who has worked for the United Nations in Geneva and President Bill Clinton’s Administration in Washington, D.C.—but, Valdez says, in some ways, she’s happy to do it: “I like this part of my job. I’m proud to represent whom a Latino should be regarded as.”
Today, as president and CEO of the Latino Donor Collaborative, that representation is Valdez’s job in more ways than one. The LDC is a clearinghouse for original research and what Valdez describes as “data-driven Latino economic advocacy.” In practice, this is what that advocacy looks like: Valdez walks into CEOs’ offices and tells them that, when they think about their Latino customers, they need to transform the image that comes into their heads. Valdez puts the LDC’s data in front of them, and explains that, according to their research, U.S.-based Latinos represent a $4.4 trillion economy. If measured as their own country, U.S. Latinos would rank as the fourth-largest economy in the world.
She is often met with disbelief. “People are saying, ‘It can’t be that my housekeeper produces $4.4 trillion,’” Valdez recounts, laughing. “I mean, literally, these are the kind of comments I hear.”
Valdez grew up in Mexico City and studied at the prestigious Tecnológico de Monterrey, housed on a modernist campus south of Monterrey’s skyscrapers. Later, back in Mexico City, she worked as a senior account executive with Nielsen. It was there that Valdez saw the power of market research to change the look of entire cities. Nielsen would tell multinational companies where untapped customers lived, and those companies would then flood those markets with commercials, new stores, and billboards to reach them.
In 1996, Valdez became an immigrant, moving to the U.S. to work for the Clinton Administration. She moved to Los Angeles to support Clinton and Vice President Al Gore’s reelection campaign in ’97 and has called L.A. home ever since. Even with a high-powered background, Valdez had to fight the feeling that she was starting over in the States. In Mexico, saying she went to Tec meant something; in the U.S., it went over people’s heads. That personal experience, as much as her background, made her a natural fit for the LDC.
The LDC was founded in 2010, when a small group of business leaders and former politicians decided to push back against “harmful stereotypes” about Latinos among the business community. The officially nonpartisan organization pursues this goal using market research—the kind of work Valdez did at Nielsen. It might not look like a march in the streets or a political campaign, but the central thesis of LDC’s work is that transforming the way businesses see their Latino customers can change the world: it evolves the TV people watch, the stores they walk into, the language they use.
Valdez began as a development consultant with the LDC before becoming president and CEO in 2015. She says that, in boardrooms and corner offices, her pitch is easy: ditch Latino stereotypes and get rich, or hold onto them and fail. She points to the growing popularity in the U.S. of Latin music and Mexican food-and-beverage brands, from Bad Bunny to Modelo beer. “For those decision makers that know the numbers, and are tracing the source and the root of pop culture today, they’ll be in great position in the coming years,” Valdez says. “Those who don’t recognize it,” she warns, “will not.”

====================================================================================================

Jose Alvarado Brought Puerto Rican Pride to the NBA Championship
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

As the New York Knicks mounted one of the greatest comebacks in sports history, erasing a 20-point fourth-quarter deficit in Game 4 of the NBA Finals in June against the San Antonio Spurs, Knicks reserve guard Jose Alvarado refused to overthink the moment.
Alvarado, who at a generously listed 6 ft. checks in as one of the shortest players in the NBA, electrified the celebrity-soaked Madison Square Garden crowd; his defensive effort, two critical three-pointers, and a driving layup down the stretch helped the Knicks chip away at San Antonio’s seemingly insurmountable lead. “I kid you not, I was not once nervous in that game,” says Alvarado, 28. “Not once. It was like, ‘Let’s do it, let’s go have fun, and let’s enjoy this moment.’ And that’s exactly what I did.’”
Three nights later, Alvarado fulfilled an impossible dream: he won an NBA title with his hometown team. The Knicks ended their 53-year championship drought, and Alvarado, a hard-nosed spark plug of Puerto Rican descent who grew up honing his game in the playgrounds of Brooklyn and Queens, came to embody the resilience of not only the team he suited up for but also the people of his city—especially his fellow Puerto Ricans, whom he’s so proudly represented in the global spotlight.
At every stage of Alvarado’s career, he was written off. “Everyone was telling me, ‘Coach, you’re in the ACC. You can’t take this guy,’” says Josh Pastner, the former Georgia Tech coach who recruited Alvarado out of Christ the King High School in Queens. “‘He’s too small. He’s not quick enough. Come on, man.’” Alvarado was named conference defensive player of the year in 2021; Georgia Tech won the ACC tournament championship that season. “Winning is more important than breathing to him,” says Pastner, who now coaches at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas.
“I don’t pass the eye test,” Alvarado admits. He knows he doesn’t look the part. But he’s now an NBA champion who signed a three-year deal, worth more than $14 million, with the Knicks this summer.
So: “I wouldn’t trust the eye test.”
Alvarado credits his success to his New York City outer-borough upbringing. “I got my toughness and my grittiness from Brooklyn,” he says. “I got a lot of my skills playing great opponents from Queens.” Because of the COVID-19 pandemic, which disrupted the seasons of many college players, in 2021 Alvarado could have returned to Georgia Tech for a fifth year of eligibility. He asked Pastner for $60,000 in name, image, and likeness money—chump change in today’s lucrative college sports economy. But five years ago, NIL rules hadn’t been formally put in place, so Pastner didn’t pay him. Alvarado bet on himself and went into the NBA draft. “Thank God I did,” he says.
“I wish I paid him the 60 grand,” says Pastner. “I mean, guys are asking me for a million dollars who average one point and can’t win a game. I would maybe have a Final Four underneath my belt.”
Although Alvarado didn’t get drafted in 2021, the New Orleans Pelicans signed him to a contract: in the Big Easy, he carved out a role as a defensive specialist, earning the nickname Grand Theft Alvarado.
Then, in February of this year, Alvarado learned that the Pelicans had traded him to the Knicks. “I automatically got water eyes because it was like, ‘Wow, I’m going to my hometown team,’” says Alvarado.
Leaving his teammates in New Orleans was bittersweet, but Alvarado fit right in with the Knicks: less than a week after the team acquired him, Alvarado scored 26 points, with eight three-pointers and five steals, against Philadelphia in just 19 minutes off the bench; he was the first reserve NBA player in history to record such sterling stats. These efforts, and the Knicks’ title, now offer him cult-hero status in New York. “After the championship, I can’t walk the streets,” he says. In late June, Alvarado brought the NBA championship trophy to Puerto Rico, where fans snapped his picture as he arrived at the airport. The governor honored Alvarado at a ceremony.
Alvarado also led Puerto Rico to an appearance in the 2024 Olympics, and was named Athlete of the Year at the 2025 National Puerto Rican Day Parade in New York City. He plans on returning to the Olympics with Puerto Rico in 2028 in Los Angeles.
As for this upcoming NBA season? The reigning champion isn’t planning to take it easy. “What makes the story better,” asks Alvarado, “than to do it again?”

Xochitl Gonzalez’s Novels Put the Spotlight on Latina and Black Creatives
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

When Xochitl Gonzalez was reading literature to research her third novel, she noticed something that didn’t quite feel right: there weren’t enough scenes featuring characters of color having a ball together. Even in tough times growing up, the writer recalls, her family always found reasons to celebrate. “My grandfather recycled bottles to pay for my communion party when I was 7,” says Gonzalez, 48. “So often, our stories are more about suffering than joy. But what I knew—and what shaped so much of my personality—was being at parties.”
So for her third novel, the best-selling author of Olga Dies Dreaming and Anita de Monte Laughs Last wrote a book rich in colorful party scenes. Last Night in Brooklyn, a Great Gatsby retelling set in 2007, conjures a version of Brooklyn just before a new wave of gentrification made it unrecognizable. The novel, which was published last year, follows Alicia, a young woman trying to shrug off the weight of her mother’s traditional values. Alicia wants to let the city sway her toward new experiences and new people—particularly her neighbor La Garza, a local fashion designer and legendary party host. La Garza’s glamorous gatherings pull Alicia into a world full of young Latino and Black creatives working to make their mark on a rapidly changing world.
Gonzalez, who was born to a Puerto Rican mother and Mexican American father, and raised in Brooklyn by her maternal grandparents, infuses her books with explorations of both identity and culture. Her love of New York City shines through her stories about characters discovering who they are and where they belong. And she likes imagining how her novels might literally fit into readers’ busy routines. She knows from her own experience as a reader hustling around the city that a book that’s easy to carry or a well-narrated audiobook can make a difference in how many people engage with her work. “I’m making art that’s meant to be consumed, so I try to keep in mind the lives that people are living,” she says. “Beautiful language means a lot to me—I also want to deliver a satisfying story that keeps people wanting to turn the page, because we’re in competition with people’s time. When I’m editing my own work, I’m like, ‘Is this book worth lugging around the city? Is it worth the schlep?’”
In a past career, Gonzalez worked for about 13 years as a wedding planner. Orchestrating events for different kinds of clients across New York gave her insights into human nature and power. She decided to become a writer in her early 40s, funnelling her breadth of experience into stories filled with characters finding their way through the world and toward their dreams while contending with the intersections of privilege, race, and class, much like she has. Her next book, Need Blind: A Memoir of Class and Climbing in America—her first nonfiction book, coming in 2027—will examine this journey even more closely. “I was able to take all of that time observing people and how things work,” says Gonzalez, who was a Pulitzer finalist for her columns in The Atlantic. “Now, I can use it.”

====================================================================================================

These Two Brothers Are Fighting to Protect Immigrant Families After a Tragedy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

For years, the Salgado house in Houston has been the place where the family gathers—for birthdays, holidays, and especially for New Year’s. “It's a house that my dad built for all of us,” says Ronaldo Salgado, 29. His father, Lorenzo Salgado Araujo, moved to the U.S. from Mexico more than 30 years ago with his wife and began working in construction, saving up for a home large enough to host future generations: their three sons and any grandchildren they might one day welcome.
This summer, the house was full again. Not for a celebration, but to mourn the death of Salgado Araujo. On the morning of July 7, the 52-year-old small-business owner was shot and killed by an Immigration and Customs Enforcement agent while driving his crew to work. In the aftermath of the shooting, Ronaldo and his brother Lorenzo Jr., 27, estimate that 14 relatives from across Texas, Mexico, Alabama, and North Carolina convened at their family house. Now, it’s quiet. Salgado Araujo’s wife spends long hours at the altar the family created, sitting in silent mourning.
In their grief, the family is also awaiting answers. What they do know is that Salgado Araujo had breakfast and left to pick up his brother and other members of his crew. They know that two unmarked SUVs approached and pursued Salgado Araujo’s van, and that, eventually, an agent shot through the passenger-side window, striking him in the abdomen. Video footage shows him lying on the ground beside the van, surrounded by officers and calling for help. He died in the hospital.
There are critical questions that still need to be answered. ICE’s version of events conflicts with statements from the witnesses who were in the van that morning. An ICE spokesperson told TIME that Salgado Araujo refused directives from agents, rammed one of their cars, and attempted to run over an officer, who fired in self-defense. The passengers, all of whom were detained by ICE and have since been released, have said that no officers were in front of the vehicle or in any danger when he was shot. The agents were not wearing body cams. A department spokesperson stated that Salgado Araujo was not the person agents were looking for that day. The FBI, the Texas Rangers, the Harris County District Attorney’s office, and the ACLU, in partnership with the family, are all investigating the events of July 7. Harris County DA Sean Teare has said a grand jury will determine any charges. In an interview with TIME, Houston ACLU attorney David Donatti says the organization intends to file a claim.
“The lack of answers is an indignity in of itself,” Lorenzo Jr. says.
He and his brother don’t believe their father would have driven away if he’d been able to tell the SUVs were government vehicles. The family had a plan in place, on the off-chance Lorenzo Sr., who was undocumented, encountered ICE agents. He was supposed to call Ronaldo or his wife at the first opportunity. He kept paperwork in his van from his lawyers, meant to show he was pursuing the ability to work legally in the U.S.
“It’s that fear that people go to work with every day, that they’re going to be detained and won’t be able to see their family that evening,” Lorenzo Jr. says. “That used to be our biggest fear: my dad would get picked up, and we’d have no idea where he was for a few days.” He hangs his head for a moment and exhales. The family’s reality is so much worse than they ever thought to fear.
“Everyone has the inalienable rights of life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness. For immigrants trying to achieve the American dream, regardless of status, they're entitled to that. Also, they’re entitled to due process,” Ronaldo says. “Unfortunately, the Department of Homeland Security, specifically ICE, isn’t following through on that promise.”
A spokesperson for DHS says, “All illegal aliens receive due process. We are applying the law as written by Congress. If a judge finds an illegal alien has no right to be in this country, we are going to remove them.”
Often, family members of those detained, injured, or killed by ICE are limited in how much they can speak out, for fear of having their own immigration status examined. Ronaldo and Lorenzo Jr. were born U.S. citizens, and feel a sense of responsibility to use their voices to call for change. Ronaldo has spoken at press conferences beside politicians; he and Lorenzo Jr. have appeared on broadcast news and testified at a congressional forum. Ronaldo wants to work on state legislation that will protect immigrant communities.
“I’m trying to use that anger and channel it in a more positive way,” says Ronaldo. “I feel right now that I can’t slow down, that I have to continue taking on these meetings, doing these interviews, doing anything I can just to get my dad's name out there, to shine a light as to what happened, to who my dad was, and the injustices that my dad experienced—that we're still experiencing to this day.”
Salgado Araujo had the equivalent of a middle school education when he came to the U.S. His sons say his greatest pride was their commitment to education. Ronaldo, a high school geography and history teacher, was set to move to Dallas for a “dream job” on Aug. 1. He was the last son still living at home with his parents, and he looked to the examples of his brothers—Lorenzo working as an engineer in D.C., their younger brother in college in Pennsylvania—and wanted to start a life in a new city like they did. The timing made sense: his father was talking about slowing down at work, and his parents were going to enjoy more time together, to enter the next phase of life. “I always had that fear: What if they need me? But it got to a point where I was like, You know what? My mom and dad, they’re fine. They have each other, and they’ll always have each other,” Ronaldo says, voice breaking. He resigned from the job he was set to begin so he can focus full-time on the work he’s doing in his father’s memory.
“This is not anything that any of us ever imagined happening, especially to us, especially to my dad, who was a decent citizen, regardless of status,” he says. “He loved this country, regardless of how much it discriminated against him.”
Ronaldo visits the site where his father was killed at least once a day, and every day he finds someone there who wants to talk. Sometimes it’s a neighbor, but often it’s a traveler from other parts of Texas, from Arizona, from New Mexico, from Minnesota. They tell Ronaldo their immigration stories and about the loved ones they’ve lost, they say that he and his brothers remind them of their own kids, or that Lorenzo Sr. reminds them of their own fathers. A few community members have dedicated themselves to tending to the makeshift memorial, watering the flowers, bringing their dinners outside to watch over things as the day winds down.
“This is all just to try to remember a man who worked all his life to give his family a better life,” Ronaldo says, “and to give his kids something to be proud of.”

How Cástulo de la Rocha Built an Affordable Health Care Empire
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

When Cástulo de la Rocha arrived at the East LA Barrio Free Clinic in 1977, he had just moved back from Berkeley, where he’d gone to law school. “I pulled up in my old beat-up Volkswagen. I had long hair for sure, holes in my pants and things like that. So a very typical hippie,” he recalls. At the clinic, started by activists to provide free health care in Spanish to the Latino community, “I saw long lines of people waiting to get a number—a ticket early in the morning, so that they may see a volunteer physician at 4:30, 5:00 in the afternoon,” he says. “It could have been my mother, could have been my sisters. It could have been one of my family members.”
De la Rocha, who was born in Chihuahua, Mexico, and immigrated to East LA when he was 10 years old, was just a few years out of college and there to be the group’s first employee. Whether he’d still have a job after the first three months depended on whether he, as executive director, could drum up funding. For the next five decades, that’s what he did, as the small clinic expanded into what is now known as AltaMed Health Services, a major provider of health care in southern California and the nation's largest nonprofit Federally Qualified Health Center. Today, de le Rocha is CEO and president, and he estimates that AltaMed employs about 10,000 people and serves 700,000 patients over about 2 million visits annually.
Throughout his time at AltaMed, he has remained inspired by the energy and vision of the clinic’s founders and board members, who were young activists in their early 20s passionate about social justice. “They said we can make a difference,” he says. “There was a need for health care, and as long as we kept our doors open, continued to support the community, people kept coming. Here we are today. AltaMed is a very different organization, but we’ve never forgotten our roots and our commitment to the community.”

Marina Larroudé Is Disrupting the Fashion Industry One Shoe at a Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

Despite having worked in the fashion industry for more than 25 years, Marina Larroudé, the co-founder and creative director of footwear brand Larroudé, has never been one to wear black. She chalks it up to her Brazilian upbringing.
Growing up in São Paulo, it was so hot that “wearing black was not even an option,” says the former fashion director turned shoe designer, 46, who has called New York City home for half her life. Out of both personal taste and necessity, she built herself a wardrobe full of lightweight fabrics in bold patterns and candy-colored shades. She now believes her whimsical style, a proud product of her background, was the key to her success in the fashion industry. “Having a different point of view was actually a strength—I really used that to my advantage,” she says.
Larroudé has taken that same approach with her namesake global footwear brand (pronounced lah-roo-DAY), which offers luxury shoes for between $290 and $590.
Larroudé and her co-founder, CEO, and husband, Ricardo Larroudé, then both unemployed, launched their debut collection in 2020 during the COVID-19 pandemic with just $4,000 and a Shopify site. They offered a selection of four styles: a knee-high boot, a chunky platform, a mule, and a pointed-toe ballet flat. The shoes came in black, ivory, and a few playful patterns, including a daisy print. While the floral pattern was more her style, the mother of two knew from her previous job working as vice president and fashion director at Barneys New York that neutral colors always sold best. Yet something unexpected happened after the COVID vaccine became available and people started going out again: the daisy print sold out. “It was like a revenge shopping situation,” she says.
The self-financed brand leaned into Marina’s style, designing shoes for those who were looking to have fun with fashion without sacrificing comfort. “I don’t want someone to buy a shoe from us and not wear it,” she says, which is why she hired a fellow Brazilian woman as head engineer to construct her shoes, which include wider toe boxes and memory foam soles, from scratch.
Building the construction internally instead of buying an existing model, which is the industry norm, was an expensive move that ended up paying off for the brand, whose famous fans include Taylor Swift, Selena Gomez, Alicia Keys, and former First Lady Jill Biden. As an entrepreneur, Marina believes she has to take risks in order to succeed. “If I play by the old playbook, I’m not going to get anywhere,” she says. “We are here to disrupt what no longer works.”
It’s not the only risk Marina took to grow her brand, which also designs handbags. At Ricardo’s suggestion, she moved away from the wholesale business model—the industry standard which prioritizes bulk orders from major retailers—and adopted a more sustainability-minded direct-to-consumer model. Customers are able to pre-order upcoming styles months in advance, allowing the company to streamline production processes and ensure minimal waste. “We see what the customers are responding to,” she says. “And then we chase that demand.”
By 2023, demand was so great that Marina and Ricardo opened a nearly 100,000 sq ft factory in Sapiranga, Brazil, where production, logistics, marketing, and administration all happen under one roof. In less than five years, Larroudé has grown from a five-person team to an operation of 750 employees. The brand with nine-digit revenue currently manufactures 500,000 shoes a year with a goal to manufacture a million pairs annually by 2028.
As the business continues to expand, supporting the Brazilian people behind the brand remains a priority. “To give prosperity back to a country,” Marina says, including by providing employees with comprehensive health benefits and opportunities for growth, “that, for me, is the silver lining.”

Rachel Zegler Wants to Open Doors for Latinos in the Arts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

When Rachel Zegler was announced the winner of Best Actress in a Musical at the 2026 Laurence Olivier Awards, she initially wasn’t sure she’d won. “It was one of those crazy things,” she says. “Vanessa Williams presented it, and she didn’t do the traditional ‘The winner is ...’ She just said, ‘Get up here, Rachel.’ So I didn’t know what was happening. Then my best friend was like, ‘Go, go, go!’”
As for the role that earned her that honor—playing Eva Perón in Jamie Lloyd’s West End revival of Evita—Zegler doesn’t mince words when it comes to how much those 12 weeks she spent performing at the London Palladium meant to her. “That production and having done it with the people I did is my proudest achievement,” she says. “It was the most incredible summer anyone could ever dream of.”
Zegler, 25, is now poised to bring her acclaimed portrayal of the former First Lady of Argentina to Broadway, with previews for the show beginning in February 2027. The musical’s highly anticipated New York City run will kick-start a chapter in which Zegler is also set to return to the big screen for the first time since starring in Disney’s 2025 live-action version of Snow White, and its assorted controversies—ranging from racist trolling about Zegler’s Colombian heritage to debates over her take on the 1937 animated original and heated commentary about the star’s political views.
“It’s a very important thing for all women, but particularly those in this industry, to learn that you’re never going to please everybody,” Zegler says of what she took away from that period of intense scrutiny. “If you are doing everything you can to be fulfilled and to do good work with good people, the rest of it is really just noise.”
Reflecting on how she was criticized for not being “enough of one thing” for her breakout role playing the Puerto Rican character Maria in Steven Spielberg’s West Side Story and “too much of another” for Snow White, Zegler maintains she will always be grateful for the formative impact of both films, despite the turbulence. “I was thrilled with the response for West Side Story. I won a Golden Globe when I was 20,” she says. “And similarly for Snow White, I was so proud of my performance. It was ultimately a really soul-filling learning experience.”
With a slate of forthcoming features in the pipeline, including indie dramedy She Gets It From Me, psychological thriller NDA, and the Lin-Manuel Miranda–directed Octet, Zegler is now looking ahead to new horizons. “I had one mission this year, which was to work with women and Latinos,” she says. She’s excited for the doors those films will open—“Not just for me, but for the amazing people I got to make them with, who in the past have been disenfranchised in this industry.”
Ultimately, Zegler refuses to let anyone put her in a box. “I love playing fully formed humans with complexities,” she says. “Human beings deserve to be represented for their flaws as well as the things that make them wonderful—I kind of think they’re one and the same.”

Paris ex-mayor says 'collective failure' led to wave of child abuse
PARIS, Sept 15 - Former Paris Mayor Anne Hidalgo said on Tuesday "collective failure" at institutions had opened the door to a wave of child abuse at state nurseries and primary schools in the French capital over more than a decade.
Police have been examining more than 200 allegations of mistreatment, violence and sexual abuse of young children during lunch breaks, rest periods and after-school activities.
Hidalgo told a Senate hearing the number of victims was unacceptable and that she understood the sense of abandonment amongst families.
'INSTITUTIONS FAILED TO PROTECT OUR CHILDREN'
"If we are standing before you here today, it is because our institutions... despite the actions taken and despite a genuine awareness of the sexual abuse suffered by our children in this country, have failed to protect our children. And this is a collective failure," she told senators.
The first allegations of abuse surfaced in 2014. A year later, the Paris General Inspectorate, an inter-departmental supervisory body, published a report warning of the risk of such offences being committed by city employees against minors.
Parents groups say their concerns about abuse fell on deaf ears in City Hall for years. Paris Public Prosecutor Laure Beccuau told France Inter radio last week she has opened investigations into Hidalgo and current mayor Emmanuel Gregoire over their alleged failures to assist a person in danger of abuse.
On Tuesday afternoon a Paris court sentenced a former after-school activity supervisor to five years in prison with four years suspended.
The offender had admitted sexually assaulting nine girls aged six to 10 at a Paris elementary school.
Senator Agnes Evren, who led the Senate's hearing, listed shortcomings in City Hall's actions detailed in the Inspectorate's report, including a four-year pause in systematic reporting of complaints, and said the Inspectorate's recommendations had only been partially implemented.
Parents' collective SOS Periscolaire expressed disappointment at Hidalgo's answers, which it said failed to deliver facts.
"We are unfortunately not surprised by these answers, which always point to the failures and responsibilities of others, but never her own," SOS Periscolaire said in a statement.
Barka Zerouali, the cofounder of a second parents' group, #MeTooEcole, told Reuters: "Once we drop our child off at school they are supposed to be safe, all the more so when we're talking about a public service." REUTERS

South African police probe killings of seven women, Ramaphosa vows action
JOHANNESBURG, Sept 15 - South African police said on Tuesday they were investigating the deaths of seven women whose bodies were found near Johannesburg over the past two months, with some stripped of their clothes and showing signs of sexual violence.
South African President Cyril Ramaphosa has vowed "no stone will be left unturned" in the investigation into their deaths, which have caused widespread alarm.
The victims are mostly aged in their 20s or 30s, and one was identified as a woman who disappeared after leaving home for an afternoon run.
Violence against women is one of the country's most entrenched social problems, despite years of government pledges to tackle it.
Police are investigating whether the killings in and around Ekurhuleni, east of Johannesburg, were carried out by a single perpetrator, multiple suspects or copycat criminals.
Some bodies were found naked, others partially clothed. One body was decomposing when discovered.
The killings have sparked widespread condemnation from civil society and sporting bodies. Police have not yet established a motive for the killings.
South Africa has one of the highest murder rates in the world, averaging about 60 a day, according to official crime statistics. Violent crime is a persistent source of public anger and anxiety as the country prepares to hold municipal elections in November. REUTERS

Luana Lopes Lara Is on the Frontlines of Evolving Prediction Markets
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:45 UTC
22:01:45 KST

Luana Lopes Lara is inherently an optimist—perhaps because Brazilians, she says, have a more positive outlook than people from other countries. That trait has served her well as chief operating officer of Kalshi, the New York-based prediction market that has boomed in recent years as people take up placing money on everything from what words the President will use in his speeches to the price of a Costco hot dog.
“I probably wouldn’t be here if I hadn’t had this Brazilian culture and background,” says Lopes Lara, 30. “I’m very optimistic and positive, but it’s not a blind optimism—it’s more like I believe that in the end, things will work out.” She still leans on some Brazilian traditions, like always trying to get out of bed on her right foot and then taking a few hops on it, eating lentils for New Year’s, and jumping over seven waves in the ocean for good luck.
Lopes Lara put her optimism to work when the company decided to sue Kalshi’s regulator, the U.S. Commodity Futures Trading Commission, arguing that the company should be allowed to list political event contracts. Kalshi won the case in September 2024, which allowed the platform to feature the 2024 elections, with users favoring Donald Trump over Kamala Harris when polls were forecasting an even race. Since then, the company’s valuation has grown to $22 billion, and in December 2025, Lopes Lara unseated 31-year-old Scale cofounder Lucy Guo to become the world’s youngest self-made woman billionaire at 29, according to Forbes.
Lopes Lara’s parents, a math teacher and an electrical engineer, pushed their two daughters to be financially independent, so they would never need to rely on partners for money. But her parents’ vision of success included going to a public university in Brazil and getting a job as a doctor, engineer, or lawyer, she says. She wanted more for herself: “I always wanted to do bigger things.”
Lopes Lara was accepted into the Massachusetts Institute of Technology, Harvard, Yale, and Stanford while she was performing as a ballet dancer in Austria, having trained at the only official affiliate school of the Russian Bolshoi Theater outside of Moscow. She chose MIT, she says, because people there seemed more passionate about what they were pursuing than students did at Harvard. It was at MIT that Lopes Lara got to know her Kalshi co-founder, Tarek Mansour, when he sat next to her in the first row in an algorithms class so he could learn from her, she says.
These days, Mansour handles many public-facing tasks as CEO and Lopes Lara handles internal operations. The role has forced her to unlearn some of the perfectionist tendencies she honed for so long as a ballerina, she says, where tilting your finger a centimeter in the wrong direction can ruin the aesthetics. “For a startup, you need the opposite,” she says. “You need to move very fast.”
The speed at which Kalshi is moving has caught the eye of regulators outside the federal government, resulting in court challenges in states including New York and Washington. So far, its federal regulator has remained steadfast in holding that Kalshi can legally operate. Lopes Lara says she believes Kalshi’s case will prevail—after all, it was a President Biden-appointed judge who first gave the company the green light to list elections, and it’s the Trump Administration that continues to support the company. “At the end of the day, it’s because we’re right on the law,” she says. Many state attorneys general disagree, making her position, unquestionably, what an optimist might say.

How Netflix Star Sofia Carson Brings Her Identity to Work
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

In the 2025 Netflix romance My Oxford Year, Sofia Carson plays Anna De La Vega, an American student studying abroad at one of England’s most prestigious universities who, like Carson herself, is of Latin American descent. Anna’s Colombian, Argentine, and Cuban heritage is woven naturally into her character’s everyday life instead of being treated as a barrier she has to overcome. It’s a distinction that Carson, who has four consecutive global #1 Netflix hits to her name, says is nonnegotiable when it comes to the roles she chooses to take on.
“All the Netflix films I have been a part of that have gone on to make streaming history have been led by myself, a Hispanic American actress telling the stories of Latin women,” says Carson, who also starred in the music-heavy romance Purple Hearts (2022), action thriller Carry-On (2024), and romantic drama The Life List (2025). “Within those stories, that heritage isn't a tattooed stereotype, but rather an organic part of existence.”
Born in Fort Lauderdale, Fla., to Colombian immigrant parents, Carson, 33, got her big break starring in the massively popular 2015 Disney Channel musical Descendants and its sequels. “I think back to my earliest memories and it wasn’t a choice that I made, it was always who I was,” Carson says of her lifelong Hollywood ambitions. “I remember being a little girl, as young as 3, and music, storytelling, performing, dancing, that was just what I did. It was undeniable.”
She has since parlayed that initial success into a diverse, multi-hyphenate career—establishing herself as one of Netflix’s most valuable movie stars, serving as an executive producer on several film projects, and building a recording catalog, including a self-titled album and songs from her films, that has surpassed 4 billion streams worldwide. Beyond her work on screen and in music, Carson is an advocate for young people’s education and empowerment through her roles as a U.S. Ambassador for UNICEF and the first global ambassador for the Latin GRAMMY Cultural Foundation.
“I recognized very early on in my career that I had a responsibility to use my voice to stand for goodness and to stand for justice,” she says.
Next up, Carson is set to co-star in the Wagner Moura-directed indie dramedy Last Night at the Lobster as part of an ensemble cast featuring award-winning actors like Elisabeth Moss and Brian Tyree Henry. “What’s most important to me is to shift the misunderstanding that to be a Latin woman is to look one way, to be one thing, to be one [type of] woman, body, skin color, accent,” she says. “In reality, we are a kaleidoscope of cultures, of talents, of thoughts, of passions. That has been such an important guiding light in my career.”

Mónica Ramírez Is Teaching Migrant Women How to Organize
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

Growing up around the rows of corn and soybeans in Fremont, Ohio, Mónica Ramírez spent her earliest days being looked after by her maternal bisabuela, or great-grandmother, Virginia Guardiola. From her home in town, Guardiola served as an informal, one-woman welcome committee for farmworkers who arrived in Fremont, disoriented and alone. Guardiola would tell these newcomers where to find jobs and fill them in on what they needed to know about Ohio.
From her great-grandmother, Ramírez says she learned “the importance of having information to share, so that people could actually get jobs—and get safe.” Ramírez saw the vulnerability of those who followed the harvest to a place like Fremont. Many were women living far from their families, who often worked without legal status, which made it risky for them to speak out against exploitative foremen in the fields or predators in the worker barracks. It mattered, she says, for them to have “trusted community members.”
Some 40 years later, Ramírez is an internationally recognized attorney and feminist, with degrees from Ohio State and Harvard. The founder of the advocacy group Justice for Migrant Women, Ramírez has made powerful allies, including celebrities like Eva Longoria and America Ferrera; she was Laura Dern’s guest at the Oscars in 2018. Yet to this day, she still lives within miles of the agricultural fields in Fremont.
When Ramírez founded Justice for Migrant Women in 2014, she’d spent a decade as an attorney helping farmworkers fight for their rights in court. But Justice for Migrant Women is not a legal aid clinic. Instead, it performs the service Ramírez saw her bisabuela perform in Fremont: it provides information. Ramírez helps workers access the knowledge they need to organize and fight for their own rights.
She sees herself more as a neighbor than a charity giver, and the neighborliness goes both ways. One of the women who most inspired Ramírez was a farmworker named Bernice, who had been sexually victimized in a labor camp and later evicted. Homeless and injured, Bernice decided to return to her home county rather than file a criminal complaint, despite Ramírez’s offer to fight for her. In all their calls, Bernice always began conversations by asking Ramírez the same question: “Are you happy?” For Ramírez, it was a model. Today, she says she starts every new conversation by considering the dignity of the person she is speaking with, taking account of the fullness of their life.
Ramírez sees a role her organization can play in the twists and turns of politics and culture, working to ensure that Justice for Migrant Women is there for people who need support as the political landscape shifts and new challenges arise. In 2017, as the Me Too movement took off, Ramírez helped write the “Dear Sisters” letter, which ran in TIME under the headline “700,000 Female Farmworkers Say They Stand With Hollywood Actors Against Sexual Assault.” It read, in part: “Even though we work in very different environments, we share a common experience of being preyed upon by individuals who have the power to hire, fire, and blacklist.” And amid the Trump Administration’s mass deportation campaign, as some farmworkers feel they have to choose between enduring sexual abuse in silence or coming forward and risking the trauma of detention and family separation, Ramírez is working in community meetings and trainings to help them understand their rights.
One of Ramírez’s projects, the “Humans Who Feed Us” dinner series, brings people together and highlights the interdependence of our broader communities. In Ohio and around the country, she sets large tables for farmworkers, line cooks, delivery drivers—immigrant workers who support the nation’s food system—and then invites business leaders and other community members to join them.
“We’re too far apart, and I think that that space between us is what’s leading to hate and misunderstanding,” Ramírez says. She sets the table the way her great-grandmother would for any newcomer in town. “Back in the day,” she says, “people relied on their neighbors.”

Ana Valdez Is Making Business Leaders Recognize Latino Economic Power
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

Sometimes, when Ana Valdez walks into a C-Suite office and introduces herself, she can see the person taking in her Mexican accent and her last name with some surprise. “They were expecting somebody different,” Valdez says. It can get “old, and a little annoying” to re-introduce herself—to explain she’s a CEO who has worked for the United Nations in Geneva and President Bill Clinton’s Administration in Washington, D.C.—but, Valdez says, in some ways, she’s happy to do it: “I like this part of my job. I’m proud to represent whom a Latino should be regarded as.”
Today, as president and CEO of the Latino Donor Collaborative, that representation is Valdez’s job in more ways than one. The LDC is a clearinghouse for original research and what Valdez describes as “data-driven Latino economic advocacy.” In practice, this is what that advocacy looks like: Valdez walks into CEOs’ offices and tells them that, when they think about their Latino customers, they need to transform the image that comes into their heads. Valdez puts the LDC’s data in front of them, and explains that, according to their research, U.S.-based Latinos represent a $4.4 trillion economy. If measured as their own country, U.S. Latinos would rank as the fourth-largest economy in the world.
She is often met with disbelief. “People are saying, ‘It can’t be that my housekeeper produces $4.4 trillion,’” Valdez recounts, laughing. “I mean, literally, these are the kind of comments I hear.”
Valdez grew up in Mexico City and studied at the prestigious Tecnológico de Monterrey, housed on a modernist campus south of Monterrey’s skyscrapers. Later, back in Mexico City, she worked as a senior account executive with Nielsen. It was there that Valdez saw the power of market research to change the look of entire cities. Nielsen would tell multinational companies where untapped customers lived, and those companies would then flood those markets with commercials, new stores, and billboards to reach them.
In 1996, Valdez became an immigrant, moving to the U.S. to work for the Clinton Administration. She moved to Los Angeles to support Clinton and Vice President Al Gore’s reelection campaign in ’97 and has called L.A. home ever since. Even with a high-powered background, Valdez had to fight the feeling that she was starting over in the States. In Mexico, saying she went to Tec meant something; in the U.S., it went over people’s heads. That personal experience, as much as her background, made her a natural fit for the LDC.
The LDC was founded in 2010, when a small group of business leaders and former politicians decided to push back against “harmful stereotypes” about Latinos among the business community. The officially nonpartisan organization pursues this goal using market research—the kind of work Valdez did at Nielsen. It might not look like a march in the streets or a political campaign, but the central thesis of LDC’s work is that transforming the way businesses see their Latino customers can change the world: it evolves the TV people watch, the stores they walk into, the language they use.
Valdez began as a development consultant with the LDC before becoming president and CEO in 2015. She says that, in boardrooms and corner offices, her pitch is easy: ditch Latino stereotypes and get rich, or hold onto them and fail. She points to the growing popularity in the U.S. of Latin music and Mexican food-and-beverage brands, from Bad Bunny to Modelo beer. “For those decision makers that know the numbers, and are tracing the source and the root of pop culture today, they’ll be in great position in the coming years,” Valdez says. “Those who don’t recognize it,” she warns, “will not.”

Jose Alvarado Brought Puerto Rican Pride to the NBA Championship
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:44 UTC
22:01:44 KST

As the New York Knicks mounted one of the greatest comebacks in sports history, erasing a 20-point fourth-quarter deficit in Game 4 of the NBA Finals in June against the San Antonio Spurs, Knicks reserve guard Jose Alvarado refused to overthink the moment.
Alvarado, who at a generously listed 6 ft. checks in as one of the shortest players in the NBA, electrified the celebrity-soaked Madison Square Garden crowd; his defensive effort, two critical three-pointers, and a driving layup down the stretch helped the Knicks chip away at San Antonio’s seemingly insurmountable lead. “I kid you not, I was not once nervous in that game,” says Alvarado, 28. “Not once. It was like, ‘Let’s do it, let’s go have fun, and let’s enjoy this moment.’ And that’s exactly what I did.’”
Three nights later, Alvarado fulfilled an impossible dream: he won an NBA title with his hometown team. The Knicks ended their 53-year championship drought, and Alvarado, a hard-nosed spark plug of Puerto Rican descent who grew up honing his game in the playgrounds of Brooklyn and Queens, came to embody the resilience of not only the team he suited up for but also the people of his city—especially his fellow Puerto Ricans, whom he’s so proudly represented in the global spotlight.
At every stage of Alvarado’s career, he was written off. “Everyone was telling me, ‘Coach, you’re in the ACC. You can’t take this guy,’” says Josh Pastner, the former Georgia Tech coach who recruited Alvarado out of Christ the King High School in Queens. “‘He’s too small. He’s not quick enough. Come on, man.’” Alvarado was named conference defensive player of the year in 2021; Georgia Tech won the ACC tournament championship that season. “Winning is more important than breathing to him,” says Pastner, who now coaches at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas.
“I don’t pass the eye test,” Alvarado admits. He knows he doesn’t look the part. But he’s now an NBA champion who signed a three-year deal, worth more than $14 million, with the Knicks this summer.
So: “I wouldn’t trust the eye test.”
Alvarado credits his success to his New York City outer-borough upbringing. “I got my toughness and my grittiness from Brooklyn,” he says. “I got a lot of my skills playing great opponents from Queens.” Because of the COVID-19 pandemic, which disrupted the seasons of many college players, in 2021 Alvarado could have returned to Georgia Tech for a fifth year of eligibility. He asked Pastner for $60,000 in name, image, and likeness money—chump change in today’s lucrative college sports economy. But five years ago, NIL rules hadn’t been formally put in place, so Pastner didn’t pay him. Alvarado bet on himself and went into the NBA draft. “Thank God I did,” he says.
“I wish I paid him the 60 grand,” says Pastner. “I mean, guys are asking me for a million dollars who average one point and can’t win a game. I would maybe have a Final Four underneath my belt.”
Although Alvarado didn’t get drafted in 2021, the New Orleans Pelicans signed him to a contract: in the Big Easy, he carved out a role as a defensive specialist, earning the nickname Grand Theft Alvarado.
Then, in February of this year, Alvarado learned that the Pelicans had traded him to the Knicks. “I automatically got water eyes because it was like, ‘Wow, I’m going to my hometown team,’” says Alvarado.
Leaving his teammates in New Orleans was bittersweet, but Alvarado fit right in with the Knicks: less than a week after the team acquired him, Alvarado scored 26 points, with eight three-pointers and five steals, against Philadelphia in just 19 minutes off the bench; he was the first reserve NBA player in history to record such sterling stats. These efforts, and the Knicks’ title, now offer him cult-hero status in New York. “After the championship, I can’t walk the streets,” he says. In late June, Alvarado brought the NBA championship trophy to Puerto Rico, where fans snapped his picture as he arrived at the airport. The governor honored Alvarado at a ceremony.
Alvarado also led Puerto Rico to an appearance in the 2024 Olympics, and was named Athlete of the Year at the 2025 National Puerto Rican Day Parade in New York City. He plans on returning to the Olympics with Puerto Rico in 2028 in Los Angeles.
As for this upcoming NBA season? The reigning champion isn’t planning to take it easy. “What makes the story better,” asks Alvarado, “than to do it again?”

Xochitl Gonzalez’s Novels Put the Spotlight on Latina and Black Creatives
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

When Xochitl Gonzalez was reading literature to research her third novel, she noticed something that didn’t quite feel right: there weren’t enough scenes featuring characters of color having a ball together. Even in tough times growing up, the writer recalls, her family always found reasons to celebrate. “My grandfather recycled bottles to pay for my communion party when I was 7,” says Gonzalez, 48. “So often, our stories are more about suffering than joy. But what I knew—and what shaped so much of my personality—was being at parties.”
So for her third novel, the best-selling author of Olga Dies Dreaming and Anita de Monte Laughs Last wrote a book rich in colorful party scenes. Last Night in Brooklyn, a Great Gatsby retelling set in 2007, conjures a version of Brooklyn just before a new wave of gentrification made it unrecognizable. The novel, which was published last year, follows Alicia, a young woman trying to shrug off the weight of her mother’s traditional values. Alicia wants to let the city sway her toward new experiences and new people—particularly her neighbor La Garza, a local fashion designer and legendary party host. La Garza’s glamorous gatherings pull Alicia into a world full of young Latino and Black creatives working to make their mark on a rapidly changing world.
Gonzalez, who was born to a Puerto Rican mother and Mexican American father, and raised in Brooklyn by her maternal grandparents, infuses her books with explorations of both identity and culture. Her love of New York City shines through her stories about characters discovering who they are and where they belong. And she likes imagining how her novels might literally fit into readers’ busy routines. She knows from her own experience as a reader hustling around the city that a book that’s easy to carry or a well-narrated audiobook can make a difference in how many people engage with her work. “I’m making art that’s meant to be consumed, so I try to keep in mind the lives that people are living,” she says. “Beautiful language means a lot to me—I also want to deliver a satisfying story that keeps people wanting to turn the page, because we’re in competition with people’s time. When I’m editing my own work, I’m like, ‘Is this book worth lugging around the city? Is it worth the schlep?’”
In a past career, Gonzalez worked for about 13 years as a wedding planner. Orchestrating events for different kinds of clients across New York gave her insights into human nature and power. She decided to become a writer in her early 40s, funnelling her breadth of experience into stories filled with characters finding their way through the world and toward their dreams while contending with the intersections of privilege, race, and class, much like she has. Her next book, Need Blind: A Memoir of Class and Climbing in America—her first nonfiction book, coming in 2027—will examine this journey even more closely. “I was able to take all of that time observing people and how things work,” says Gonzalez, who was a Pulitzer finalist for her columns in The Atlantic. “Now, I can use it.”

====================================================================================================

These Two Brothers Are Fighting to Protect Immigrant Families After a Tragedy
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

For years, the Salgado house in Houston has been the place where the family gathers—for birthdays, holidays, and especially for New Year’s. “It's a house that my dad built for all of us,” says Ronaldo Salgado, 29. His father, Lorenzo Salgado Araujo, moved to the U.S. from Mexico more than 30 years ago with his wife and began working in construction, saving up for a home large enough to host future generations: their three sons and any grandchildren they might one day welcome.
This summer, the house was full again. Not for a celebration, but to mourn the death of Salgado Araujo. On the morning of July 7, the 52-year-old small-business owner was shot and killed by an Immigration and Customs Enforcement agent while driving his crew to work. In the aftermath of the shooting, Ronaldo and his brother Lorenzo Jr., 27, estimate that 14 relatives from across Texas, Mexico, Alabama, and North Carolina convened at their family house. Now, it’s quiet. Salgado Araujo’s wife spends long hours at the altar the family created, sitting in silent mourning.
In their grief, the family is also awaiting answers. What they do know is that Salgado Araujo had breakfast and left to pick up his brother and other members of his crew. They know that two unmarked SUVs approached and pursued Salgado Araujo’s van, and that, eventually, an agent shot through the passenger-side window, striking him in the abdomen. Video footage shows him lying on the ground beside the van, surrounded by officers and calling for help. He died in the hospital.
There are critical questions that still need to be answered. ICE’s version of events conflicts with statements from the witnesses who were in the van that morning. An ICE spokesperson told TIME that Salgado Araujo refused directives from agents, rammed one of their cars, and attempted to run over an officer, who fired in self-defense. The passengers, all of whom were detained by ICE and have since been released, have said that no officers were in front of the vehicle or in any danger when he was shot. The agents were not wearing body cams. A department spokesperson stated that Salgado Araujo was not the person agents were looking for that day. The FBI, the Texas Rangers, the Harris County District Attorney’s office, and the ACLU, in partnership with the family, are all investigating the events of July 7. Harris County DA Sean Teare has said a grand jury will determine any charges. In an interview with TIME, Houston ACLU attorney David Donatti says the organization intends to file a claim.
“The lack of answers is an indignity in of itself,” Lorenzo Jr. says.
He and his brother don’t believe their father would have driven away if he’d been able to tell the SUVs were government vehicles. The family had a plan in place, on the off-chance Lorenzo Sr., who was undocumented, encountered ICE agents. He was supposed to call Ronaldo or his wife at the first opportunity. He kept paperwork in his van from his lawyers, meant to show he was pursuing the ability to work legally in the U.S.
“It’s that fear that people go to work with every day, that they’re going to be detained and won’t be able to see their family that evening,” Lorenzo Jr. says. “That used to be our biggest fear: my dad would get picked up, and we’d have no idea where he was for a few days.” He hangs his head for a moment and exhales. The family’s reality is so much worse than they ever thought to fear.
“Everyone has the inalienable rights of life, liberty, and the pursuit of happiness. For immigrants trying to achieve the American dream, regardless of status, they're entitled to that. Also, they’re entitled to due process,” Ronaldo says. “Unfortunately, the Department of Homeland Security, specifically ICE, isn’t following through on that promise.”
A spokesperson for DHS says, “All illegal aliens receive due process. We are applying the law as written by Congress. If a judge finds an illegal alien has no right to be in this country, we are going to remove them.”
Often, family members of those detained, injured, or killed by ICE are limited in how much they can speak out, for fear of having their own immigration status examined. Ronaldo and Lorenzo Jr. were born U.S. citizens, and feel a sense of responsibility to use their voices to call for change. Ronaldo has spoken at press conferences beside politicians; he and Lorenzo Jr. have appeared on broadcast news and testified at a congressional forum. Ronaldo wants to work on state legislation that will protect immigrant communities.
“I’m trying to use that anger and channel it in a more positive way,” says Ronaldo. “I feel right now that I can’t slow down, that I have to continue taking on these meetings, doing these interviews, doing anything I can just to get my dad's name out there, to shine a light as to what happened, to who my dad was, and the injustices that my dad experienced—that we're still experiencing to this day.”
Salgado Araujo had the equivalent of a middle school education when he came to the U.S. His sons say his greatest pride was their commitment to education. Ronaldo, a high school geography and history teacher, was set to move to Dallas for a “dream job” on Aug. 1. He was the last son still living at home with his parents, and he looked to the examples of his brothers—Lorenzo working as an engineer in D.C., their younger brother in college in Pennsylvania—and wanted to start a life in a new city like they did. The timing made sense: his father was talking about slowing down at work, and his parents were going to enjoy more time together, to enter the next phase of life. “I always had that fear: What if they need me? But it got to a point where I was like, You know what? My mom and dad, they’re fine. They have each other, and they’ll always have each other,” Ronaldo says, voice breaking. He resigned from the job he was set to begin so he can focus full-time on the work he’s doing in his father’s memory.
“This is not anything that any of us ever imagined happening, especially to us, especially to my dad, who was a decent citizen, regardless of status,” he says. “He loved this country, regardless of how much it discriminated against him.”
Ronaldo visits the site where his father was killed at least once a day, and every day he finds someone there who wants to talk. Sometimes it’s a neighbor, but often it’s a traveler from other parts of Texas, from Arizona, from New Mexico, from Minnesota. They tell Ronaldo their immigration stories and about the loved ones they’ve lost, they say that he and his brothers remind them of their own kids, or that Lorenzo Sr. reminds them of their own fathers. A few community members have dedicated themselves to tending to the makeshift memorial, watering the flowers, bringing their dinners outside to watch over things as the day winds down.
“This is all just to try to remember a man who worked all his life to give his family a better life,” Ronaldo says, “and to give his kids something to be proud of.”

How Cástulo de la Rocha Built an Affordable Health Care Empire
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

When Cástulo de la Rocha arrived at the East LA Barrio Free Clinic in 1977, he had just moved back from Berkeley, where he’d gone to law school. “I pulled up in my old beat-up Volkswagen. I had long hair for sure, holes in my pants and things like that. So a very typical hippie,” he recalls. At the clinic, started by activists to provide free health care in Spanish to the Latino community, “I saw long lines of people waiting to get a number—a ticket early in the morning, so that they may see a volunteer physician at 4:30, 5:00 in the afternoon,” he says. “It could have been my mother, could have been my sisters. It could have been one of my family members.”
De la Rocha, who was born in Chihuahua, Mexico, and immigrated to East LA when he was 10 years old, was just a few years out of college and there to be the group’s first employee. Whether he’d still have a job after the first three months depended on whether he, as executive director, could drum up funding. For the next five decades, that’s what he did, as the small clinic expanded into what is now known as AltaMed Health Services, a major provider of health care in southern California and the nation's largest nonprofit Federally Qualified Health Center. Today, de le Rocha is CEO and president, and he estimates that AltaMed employs about 10,000 people and serves 700,000 patients over about 2 million visits annually.
Throughout his time at AltaMed, he has remained inspired by the energy and vision of the clinic’s founders and board members, who were young activists in their early 20s passionate about social justice. “They said we can make a difference,” he says. “There was a need for health care, and as long as we kept our doors open, continued to support the community, people kept coming. Here we are today. AltaMed is a very different organization, but we’ve never forgotten our roots and our commitment to the community.”

Marina Larroudé Is Disrupting the Fashion Industry One Shoe at a Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

Despite having worked in the fashion industry for more than 25 years, Marina Larroudé, the co-founder and creative director of footwear brand Larroudé, has never been one to wear black. She chalks it up to her Brazilian upbringing.
Growing up in São Paulo, it was so hot that “wearing black was not even an option,” says the former fashion director turned shoe designer, 46, who has called New York City home for half her life. Out of both personal taste and necessity, she built herself a wardrobe full of lightweight fabrics in bold patterns and candy-colored shades. She now believes her whimsical style, a proud product of her background, was the key to her success in the fashion industry. “Having a different point of view was actually a strength—I really used that to my advantage,” she says.
Larroudé has taken that same approach with her namesake global footwear brand (pronounced lah-roo-DAY), which offers luxury shoes for between $290 and $590.
Larroudé and her co-founder, CEO, and husband, Ricardo Larroudé, then both unemployed, launched their debut collection in 2020 during the COVID-19 pandemic with just $4,000 and a Shopify site. They offered a selection of four styles: a knee-high boot, a chunky platform, a mule, and a pointed-toe ballet flat. The shoes came in black, ivory, and a few playful patterns, including a daisy print. While the floral pattern was more her style, the mother of two knew from her previous job working as vice president and fashion director at Barneys New York that neutral colors always sold best. Yet something unexpected happened after the COVID vaccine became available and people started going out again: the daisy print sold out. “It was like a revenge shopping situation,” she says.
The self-financed brand leaned into Marina’s style, designing shoes for those who were looking to have fun with fashion without sacrificing comfort. “I don’t want someone to buy a shoe from us and not wear it,” she says, which is why she hired a fellow Brazilian woman as head engineer to construct her shoes, which include wider toe boxes and memory foam soles, from scratch.
Building the construction internally instead of buying an existing model, which is the industry norm, was an expensive move that ended up paying off for the brand, whose famous fans include Taylor Swift, Selena Gomez, Alicia Keys, and former First Lady Jill Biden. As an entrepreneur, Marina believes she has to take risks in order to succeed. “If I play by the old playbook, I’m not going to get anywhere,” she says. “We are here to disrupt what no longer works.”
It’s not the only risk Marina took to grow her brand, which also designs handbags. At Ricardo’s suggestion, she moved away from the wholesale business model—the industry standard which prioritizes bulk orders from major retailers—and adopted a more sustainability-minded direct-to-consumer model. Customers are able to pre-order upcoming styles months in advance, allowing the company to streamline production processes and ensure minimal waste. “We see what the customers are responding to,” she says. “And then we chase that demand.”
By 2023, demand was so great that Marina and Ricardo opened a nearly 100,000 sq ft factory in Sapiranga, Brazil, where production, logistics, marketing, and administration all happen under one roof. In less than five years, Larroudé has grown from a five-person team to an operation of 750 employees. The brand with nine-digit revenue currently manufactures 500,000 shoes a year with a goal to manufacture a million pairs annually by 2028.
As the business continues to expand, supporting the Brazilian people behind the brand remains a priority. “To give prosperity back to a country,” Marina says, including by providing employees with comprehensive health benefits and opportunities for growth, “that, for me, is the silver lining.”
