Rachel Zegler Wants to Open Doors for Latinos in the Arts
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:43 UTC
22:01:43 KST

When Rachel Zegler was announced the winner of Best Actress in a Musical at the 2026 Laurence Olivier Awards, she initially wasn’t sure she’d won. “It was one of those crazy things,” she says. “Vanessa Williams presented it, and she didn’t do the traditional ‘The winner is ...’ She just said, ‘Get up here, Rachel.’ So I didn’t know what was happening. Then my best friend was like, ‘Go, go, go!’”
As for the role that earned her that honor—playing Eva Perón in Jamie Lloyd’s West End revival of Evita—Zegler doesn’t mince words when it comes to how much those 12 weeks she spent performing at the London Palladium meant to her. “That production and having done it with the people I did is my proudest achievement,” she says. “It was the most incredible summer anyone could ever dream of.”
Zegler, 25, is now poised to bring her acclaimed portrayal of the former First Lady of Argentina to Broadway, with previews for the show beginning in February 2027. The musical’s highly anticipated New York City run will kick-start a chapter in which Zegler is also set to return to the big screen for the first time since starring in Disney’s 2025 live-action version of Snow White, and its assorted controversies—ranging from racist trolling about Zegler’s Colombian heritage to debates over her take on the 1937 animated original and heated commentary about the star’s political views.
“It’s a very important thing for all women, but particularly those in this industry, to learn that you’re never going to please everybody,” Zegler says of what she took away from that period of intense scrutiny. “If you are doing everything you can to be fulfilled and to do good work with good people, the rest of it is really just noise.”
Reflecting on how she was criticized for not being “enough of one thing” for her breakout role playing the Puerto Rican character Maria in Steven Spielberg’s West Side Story and “too much of another” for Snow White, Zegler maintains she will always be grateful for the formative impact of both films, despite the turbulence. “I was thrilled with the response for West Side Story. I won a Golden Globe when I was 20,” she says. “And similarly for Snow White, I was so proud of my performance. It was ultimately a really soul-filling learning experience.”
With a slate of forthcoming features in the pipeline, including indie dramedy She Gets It From Me, psychological thriller NDA, and the Lin-Manuel Miranda–directed Octet, Zegler is now looking ahead to new horizons. “I had one mission this year, which was to work with women and Latinos,” she says. She’s excited for the doors those films will open—“Not just for me, but for the amazing people I got to make them with, who in the past have been disenfranchised in this industry.”
Ultimately, Zegler refuses to let anyone put her in a box. “I love playing fully formed humans with complexities,” she says. “Human beings deserve to be represented for their flaws as well as the things that make them wonderful—I kind of think they’re one and the same.”

Nelson German Has a Recipe for Shaking Up the Oakland Restaurant Scene
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:42 UTC
22:01:42 KST

Chef Nelson German doesn’t believe in the idea of the “self-taught” culinarian.
“How can you really teach yourself?” asks the Dominican American executive chef and restaurateur. “Even if you’re learning from watching somebody on YouTube, that person taught you. A dishwasher, a prep cook, a server who instills you with some kind of knowledge—you’re learning from everybody around you.”
German, whose culinary career spans over two decades, cut his teeth training in several esteemed New York City kitchens. His profile as an emerging chef continued to rise after a stint on Season 18 of Bravo’s Top Chef in 2021, and today, German is best known for his steadily expanding culinary presence in Oakland, Calif. German believes his current portfolio, a trio of conceptual eateries inspired by and in celebration of his Afro-Latin roots, was only conceived because he stepped away from New York.
German, 45, grew up in the Washington Heights neighborhood of Manhattan, a cultural enclave for the Latin Caribbean community. In 2010, he relocated to the San Francisco Bay Area, his wife’s hometown. German says the culture shock coming from “Nueva Yol”—where it seems there are members of the Afro-Caribbean diaspora on every block—was jolting.
When homesickness hit a peak, German decided to harness the nostalgia and embrace his cultural heritage by honoring “the ingredients I missed the most” in his restaurants, alaMar Dominican Kitchen and Sobre Mesa.
“As chefs, to be recognized—at least back in the day—we were always taught to focus on European-centric food,” German says. “That [was] the only way to be well-known, to get the recognitions, to get the awards. That’s not true anymore.”
What has now earned German a Michelin recommendation and recognition from the James Beard Foundation is his innovation of traditional Afro-Latin dishes without compromising the flavorful Caribbean spice the culture is known for—in spite of the notion that fine dining must serve a flatter, Western palate. German’s promise to deliver cuisine authentic and accurate to history and cultural memory is in line with what he believes is a growing trend among chefs of color: deviating from the mainstream to create culinary experiences that are culturally and regionally hyperspecific.
The latest addition to German’s distinct offerings is the Ethiopian and Dominican fused Meski, a collaboration with entrepreneur Guma Fassil and Golden State Warriors forward Draymond Green, which opened in April 2025.
“We’re telling a real story,” German says.
The story that he wants to define his legacy is one of community, whether it’s nurturing young minds through culinary training programs or fostering togetherness through Caribbean fare. His first book, Caribbean Cocktails: Drinks and Bites from the Afro-Latino Diaspora, released in June, offers modern takes on familiar libations and is meant to inspire readers to “host the way we do it, as Caribbeans,” German says. Maintaining ancestral traditions is how he honors Sankofa, the Ghanaian principle adopted widely throughout the African Diaspora: “You always look back and celebrate your past while always continuing to move forward, and moving forward with love and kindness,” German says. “Always giving tribute to the people that paved the way for you.”

Julián Castro Is Bolstering Latino Communities One Grant at a Time
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:42 UTC
22:01:42 KST

Julián Castro has gotten a few things from his mother: a last name, a twin brother, a general sense of optimism, and a penchant for serving his community.
Maria del Rosario Castro, a now 79-year-old Mexican American activist, was once a member of La Raza Unida, a prominent Chicano political party in Texas. Her two sons, who were born in San Antonio in 1974, often accompanied her to rallies and meetings when they were young. “[She] raised my brother and me with this sense that you should always do well for yourself, but also do good for others,” Castro says.
For more than a decade, Castro used politics as an avenue to act on that sense. (The same goes for his brother, Joaquin, who has been a Texas congressman since 2013.) His resume includes a stint on the San Antonio city council, five years as mayor of the city, an appointment to President Barack Obama’s cabinet as the U.S. Secretary of Housing and Urban Development, and a 2020 presidential campaign.
But Castro, 51, recently started a new chapter in his career. He’s left politics behind—for now—and has entered the world of philanthropy. Since 2024, he has served as CEO of the Latino Community Foundation, an organization founded in 1989 that funds Latino-serving and Latino-led nonprofits in the U.S. He says that this opportunity has presented him with “a new way to do good for the community.”
One in five Americans are Latino, according to a 2025 Pew Research Center report, and the Latino Donor Collaborative found that this group alone was responsible for 28.2% of all U.S. economic growth in 2024. But this national impact is not reflected in the amount of philanthropic dollars invested back into the Latino community, which often remains below one percent.
Castro says that LCF is in the business of disrupting this status quo. “The country can only prosper if the community does well.”
In March, LCF, which primarily funds nonprofits based in California, announced a plan to expand its grant-making efforts nationally and establish a $250 million endowment—a sizable increase from the organization's current, "modest" $35 million endowment. Castro says that this new plan would ensure an annual grant-making baseline of $10 million and the ability “to serve many more organizations consistently.”
Castro says that as the Trump Administration and Immigration and Customs Enforcement continue their sweeping, sometimes fatal immigration crackdown across the U.S., the work of nonprofits has never been more important. In February, LCF launched the Community Protection Fund, issuing grants to grassroots organizations in California, Nevada, and Minnesota that are either offering assistance to families impacted by ICE’s actions or challenging detention abuses in court. This initiative was a no-brainer for Castro.
“Immigrant families and mixed-status families are under attack by an Administration absolutely determined to target both legal and undocumented immigrants, and sometimes even citizens. And so we decided to double down on investing in immigrant-serving nonprofits,” he says.
Though Castro feels deeply disappointed by how changes in immigration and deportation policies have affected Latino communities in the U.S., he hopes that Americans who feel the same will use this time to imagine a diverse, inclusive, and equitable future: “My hope is that the Latino Community Foundation, as it grows, can help accelerate that progress.”

Lukashenko dismisses rumors of political arrests following release of other prisoners
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:01:41 UTC
22:01:41 KST

MINSK, September 15. /TASS/. During talks with US Special Envoy for Belarus John Cole, Belarusian President Alexander Lukashenko dismissed as baseless reports that political arrests are taking place in the country following the release of people convicted of extremist-related crimes.
"Our 'runaways' are promoting this narrative in the West. From what I hear, I sense they have led you to believe that while we are releasing some people, we are simultaneously detaining twice as many to replace them as part of ongoing repression. I want to state publicly that this is absolutely untrue," Lukashenko said. A video clip of the talks was published by Pul_1, a Telegram channel close to the presidential press service.
"We have no intention of sending new people to correctional labor colonies to replace those who have been released. We do not have such a problem. We are not doing this, nor will we. These are our fellow Belarusians," Lukashenko emphasized.
He also pointed out that Belarus has no political prisoners, as the country’s legislation contains no such legal category.
"If any Belarusian citizens - or foreign nationals present here - violate Belarusian laws, we will certainly respond in accordance with the law. If they need to be placed under investigation or sent to correctional labor colonies, that is where they will be. But this has absolutely nothing to do with politics," Lukashenko stressed.
He said there was "no need to persecute anyone for political reasons" today.

The love of her life died at sea hunting shipwrecks. Now she’s taking the helm
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:00:43 UTC
22:00:43 KST

After Joe died, Jennifer Sellitti felt as though she were living underwater.
Other people became murky, their words muffled. She slept constantly; unconsciousness was a blessing. On the worst days, memories bowled her over.
“It was flashbacks, constantly,” she said. “I’d be having a conversation with you and look completely normal, but I’d really not be paying any attention because I’d be seeing these horrible images over and over again.”
Her brain replayed a grim carousel of intrusive memories from the morning of 29 July 2025. Pulling Joe out of the water, his limp body swinging from the hydraulic winch they normally used for retrieving deep-sea treasures.
Her, kneeling over Joe on the back of his boat, trying desperately to revive him. The death-mask look settling on his face as the life drained out of it. The two moles under his right eye, the ones she liked to kiss when she said goodbye.
She knuckled down, found tricks to stem the torrent. She had always been good at packing uncomfortable feelings into boxes; her job as New Jersey’s top public defender demanded it. But the problem with doing that, she knew, was the potential for those boxes to explode.
One night in early September, six weeks after his death, she stopped off at the dock to check on Joe’s boat – there had been a problem plugging into the shore power grid the day before. It was after a work event, and Jenn was in a ballgown and heels. She stepped aboard and immediately felt like someone had taken a baseball bat to her stomach.
“I don’t think I’d cried that hard since it happened,” she said. She lay down in Joe’s bunk. When she got up, somehow two hours had passed. “It was suddenly midnight and I’m on this boat, and it was like, what next?”
Joe Mazraani had died on a dive, seeking to identify a shipwreck the couple had discovered with their crew the previous summer. Both criminal defense lawyers by day, Joe and Jenn spent every waking hour outside the office plotting their next voyage, triangulating their next quarry. “We’re pirates,” Jenn likes to say.
More specifically, Joe and the crew of Tenacious were technical wreck divers, part salty sea dogs and part maritime detectives. Jenn managed the research and expeditions. She also ran point on hauling their discoveries into the light – not just organizing public appearances and liaising with museums, but connecting with the families of victims and survivors. Making sure their stories were told.
Their company, Atlantic Wreck Salvage, regularly made national headlines with their finds; as members of the crew will tell you, NBC’s Brian Williams interrupted the broadcast of an Olympic opening ceremony to announce they had discovered the last missing German submarine sunk off the coast of Nantucket during the second world war.
Both Joe and Jenn were load-bearing pillars of the operation, but she would be the first to tell you that Joe was the undisputed captain. He had built Tenacious himself, bolt by bolt; his knowledge of the boat had no backup.
So with his death, at 47, his crew, held together largely by his appetite for risk, love of adventure and force of will, was suddenly without an anchor. They drifted away, to other dive boats or towards retirement. Everyone assumed the boat would be sold to a man.
But Jenn had her captain’s license, too. Joe had given her Tenacious in his will. Even amid the grief, a plan was beginning to take hold in her mind. It was probably crazy. No, it was definitely crazy. But it might just ensure their survival.
“The way I see it, I have no other choice,” she said.
The popular image of a shipwreck hunter – a swashbuckler armed with a leather map and a bottle of rum – does not survive much contact with the actual thing. Ditto the shipwrecks themselves.
“Everybody thinks of a big pirate ship just sitting on the bottom of the ocean, perfectly preserved,” said Mike Dudas, a technical diver active since 1988. “It’s more like diving a junkyard.”
The linings of the east coast, particularly on the approach into New York Harbor, are littered with wrecks. These sites, each an alien world unto themselves, are far from hospitable to human intruders. Twisted steel and rotting timbers lie half-buried in a fine gray silt, rusted over or studded with anemones, pecked at by schools of fish, dragged about the sea floor by hapless fishers dropping their nets. It’s gloomy and still until a current materializes without warning, threatening to rip divers away from the debris field. The hulls have been rearranging their interiors for decades: collapsed passageways, fallen bulkheads, rooms you can no longer enter.
“A lot of times, you’re taking your bottles off, pushing them in front of you, and tearing your suit on the way in,” said Chris Ogden, a younger member of the Tenacious crew, “and just hoping you can get out the same way.”
Real shipwreck hunting is similarly unromantic. The vast majority of the process is made up of painstaking research, poring over historical charts, survivor accounts and archival clippings. Then comes the actual search itself, which largely constitutes arguing about sonar data. This phase involves towing a torpedo-shaped device called a towfish behind the boat on an armored cable, at three to five knots, sweeping back and forth across a predetermined search grid for hours on end. Divers call it “mowing the lawn”. If you’re lucky, out of the endless stream of acoustic images of the ocean floor, an anomaly will emerge.
Then you get in the water.
Recreational divers work to a limit of about 120ft (37 metres); technical divers like those aboard Tenacious regularly descend to 300, sometimes pushing toward 400 – squarely in what veteran diver Eric Takakjian calls the “don’t-try-this-at-home category”. That far down, the light disappears fast. Packer heard Simon and Garfunkel in his head every time he traversed an anchor line. Hello, darkness, my old friend.
The math also changes. A diver gets roughly 25 minutes of bottom time, followed by two to three hours of mind-numbing ascent to dissipate the nitrogen that’s accumulated in their blood and tissue. Ascend too quickly and that nitrogen can form bubbles inside the body, causing paralysis, brain damage or death.
The technical diving community of the eastern seaboard is small enough that everyone, at most, knows someone who knows someone who’s died on a dive. The Andrea Doria, a once gorgeous luxury liner, has claimed the lives of nearly two dozen divers since its sinking in 1956. The toll is accepted as the price of admission.
“Call it passion, maybe, but it’s more like a sickness,” Ogden said.
Jenn was standing with her back to the door, studying her bookcase, when she sensed someone was watching her.
It was 2014 and she had been in her new office for three or four days. Her bosses at the public defender’s office had sent her to take over a trial region that had grown, in their estimation, sloppy. The space reflected it, with jumbled records everywhere.
She turned around. A man she had never seen before looked back at her.
“How did you get in here?” Jenn demanded.
“I’m Joe Mazraani.” He seemed amused.
“That’s not what I asked.”
Her secretary materialized in the doorway, flustered – “no, no, he’s OK, he’s a pool attorney, I wanted you to meet him” – and Sellitti turned back to the stranger. He was looking her up and down with a frank appraisal. His eyes landed on her shoes.
“Are those Louboutins?”
“Yes.”
A beat.
“Are you going to shake things up around here,” he said, “or is it going to be status quo?”
Jenn stared at him. “I’m going to shake things up around here.”
He nodded once and walked out of her office.
They became friends not long after, the way two trial animals usually do, working a case together. They had similar philosophies – push, fight, do not make peace with the state on its terms.
Their first date was in early 2015 at a place called Rat’s Restaurant in central Jersey. The maître d’ assumed Joe was Jenn’s professor. Joe, four years younger than Jenn, found this hilarious.
He had never been married, he told her. Every previous relationship had buckled under the weight of the same rival. “There was always the other woman,” Jenn said. Tenacious – the 45-ft fiberglass fishing boat Joe had bought in Maryland on a bitterly cold winter’s day in 2010. He had been diving for far longer, but the boat gave him what he had been missing: the ability to go where he wanted, when he wanted, for as long as the work required.
Joe Mazraani grew up in Beirut in the 1980s. He would tell anyone who asked, and plenty who didn’t, that he was Phoenician. “Our people are sea people,” he would tell Jenn. The Phoenicians were the great maritime civilization of the ancient world, the ones who first charted the Mediterranean, who had pushed past the Pillars of Hercules into open ocean. If you were going to be obsessed with the sea, you may as well have the blood for it.
He was 15 when his family sent him to America. He lived with relatives in New Jersey, then Florida. He went to college on Staten Island, where he lived for a time in a Lebanese church. He was 19, when he took his first dive class.
In the meticulous logs of his first 197 dives, which Jenn found only after his death – Joe had sworn up and down he hadn’t kept any such logs, he had never had the patience – he excitedly recorded horseshoe crab sightings and expressed fears about low visibility. The little PADI (Professional Association of Diving Instructors) booklet had a section for personal information. Under Distinguishing scars/marks, Joe had written: “I AM AN ASSHOLE!” Allergic to: “Idiots”. Medications I must have: “Knowledge”.
He became a lawyer primarily as a means of paying for his habit; an older diver had told him that only the law or medicine could support a serious diving hobby. Doctors, Joe thought, spent too much time on land.
Jenn understood this in a way his previous partners hadn’t: she was the same. The daughter of educators, she had spent years ice climbing, a pastime that involves clambering up frozen waterfalls at altitude. She was familiar with the patterns of being unable to commit to plans more than a day in advance; waiting all week for the temperature to plunge; checking forecasts obsessively; deciding on a dime to drop everything and drive three hours to New Hampshire. She knew what it was like to be in the grip of something that looked, from the outside, like a pathology.
Their first New Year’s together, Joe decanted wine into a barnacle-crusted vase he had raised from the SS Choapa, a Chilean freighter that sank off the Jersey Shore in 1944. They danced beside the kitchen table while Édith Piaf crooned La Vie en Rose. He whispered to her that he loved her, over and over again.
For Jenn, who had been married once before, “love” didn’t do it justice. “What we had was more intense than that,” she said. “It was like looking at another person and finally, fully understanding who you are.” It felt more like the two of them burned together.
Tom Packer, a veteran diver on the east coast scene who had known Joe for years, had taken to teasing him about the burgeoning romance. “When are you going to marry this girl? My God,” he remembered thinking. Joe and Jenn had already decided that marriage and kids weren’t for them, but in a few years’ time they would move out to a small Raritan Valley farm, filling it with marine artifacts at the expense of basic furniture.
His friends could see it plainly enough – the way Joe looked at Jenn, the way she and the boat both fit perfectly into his life. “I was so happy for him,” Packer said. “We all were.”
The first time Jenn set foot on Tenacious was 18 June 2015. She didn’t have her diver’s certification yet, but there was no shortage of things to do on a working boat. She cooked. She led yoga sessions on deck. She learned the service support routines. She logged a daily 20,000 steps on a vessel she could cross in 30 paces.
By then, Joe had already built something of a professional reputation for himself. He was a world-class grump and could be fiendishly uncommunicative, particularly when it came to what he had planned for his next conquest. But he was also known to be one of the most capable and accomplished wreck divers in the region.
His was the crew that had recovered the auxiliary stern helm of the SS Ayuruoca, a Brazilian freighter that belches oil and lies in a deep, silty offshoot of the Hudson River aptly nicknamed the Mudhole. They had raised telegraphs and foghorns, portholes and bells, crockery and silverware and bathroom tiles, the instruments and ornaments of ships that were, in their time, the finest things men could make.
The crew was a rotating cast of around 20 regulars, handpicked by Joe and recruited through reputation and word-of-mouth, trips coordinated through a text chain and the occasional email. Among them was a diver called Tim Whitehead, who readily sold his 2003 Cobra Mustang to buy the right equipment for their expeditions.
“Anybody that dives in Jersey wants to dive on Tenacious,” Whitehead said.
Her first year, Jenn stuck to day trips. In 2016 she did one offshore run – the Andrea Doria, for the wreck’s 60th anniversary. She got her open water certification in 2017, her advanced certification two years later. But what bound her to Tenacious wasn’t the diving; it was the stories. The more she learned about the people on these ships – who they were, why they were there, what became of them – the less she could stop.
One ship in particular ensnared her imagination.
Le Lyonnais was a transatlantic passenger steamship that collided with an American sailing vessel in heavy fog off the coast of Nantucket five years before the outbreak of the civil war. The American ship, the Adriatic, left the damaged liner to sink and its survivors to fend for themselves. After a week at sea beset by storms and freezing temperatures, just 18 of 132 souls aboard survived. The Adriatic’s captain was put on trial, only for the proceedings to be swept aside by the onslaught of war. The victims’ families never received justice, and for more than a century and a half Le Lyonnais’s final resting place remained unknown.
It took eight years for Jenn to find the ship. By the time she was done, she had literally written the book on the subject.
She fell in love with Le Lyonnais in 2016, when a friend and shipwreck detective named Eric Takakjian mentioned its existence to her. “It quickly became my dream to find it,” Jenn remembered. “Every year, I would collect a binder full of the information I’d found and drive up to Eric’s house in Fairhaven, where we compared notes.” They called it, for reasons unclear, Project Mayonnaise.
By 2019, they had narrowed down the ship’s probable location to part of a rugged, uncharted underwater plateau called Georges Bank. Jenn’s hopes sank. Tenacious, plucky though she was, didn’t have nearly the horsepower required to motor all the way from the Jersey Shore to the bank, which lay 200 miles offshore of Fairhaven.
The nearest Coast Guard station may as well have been on another planet. And then there were the conditions. “Everything is just wilder there,” a sea captain once told Jenn. A popular route from America to England in its time, the Bank was a veritable graveyard of ships – but knowledge, even anecdotal, about dive conditions was virtually zilch.
Joe went silent on the matter. Jenn figured it was a lost cause. Then he came back with a plan. New engine. Double the fuel capacity. Side-scan sonar. Give him the winter of 2021 to upgrade Tenacious, he said, and maybe they could find Le Lyonnais.
The crew motored out to Georges Bank for the first time the next summer, “mowing the lawn” and taking measurements of anomalies they didn’t have the conditions to investigate further. In August 2024, they finally identified one of these blips as their culprit, diving down and matching a part of her rigging and engine cylinder to Le Lyonnais’s recorded specifications.
“Being able to find this ship, I felt like I was giving closure to the people who suffered there,” Jenn told the New York Times.
Their celebration was purposefully short-lived, however. “We never rested on our laurels,” Packer noted. “As soon as we’d accomplished something, it was behind us and we were on to the next thing.”
“The problem is how quickly the moment fades,” Jenn said. “The only way to get it back is to find something else. The search never ends.”
The Times’s report noted the crew had one false start on that expedition, first diving down to a “completely different wreck” they suspected might be Le Lyonnais. This Jane Doe ship they dubbed the Big Engine Steamer. They vowed to return and identify her the next year.
It would be Joe’s last dive.
Joe died on a Tuesday. The night before, he and Jenn had stood on the back of Tenacious and talked about how far they had come as the light faded around them. The ocean around them felt like it was all theirs – it would be their playground for the next 10 years, at least. She would retire first, maybe work part-time at his firm. He put his arm around her.
“We were very much in this moment,” Jenn said, “on the edge of everything that we had dreamed of. Truly.”
Jenn didn’t tell Joe, but she had been trying to stifle an uneasy feeling since Thursday, the night before they had struck out for the Bank. Their Portuguese water dog, Pirate, had laid down in the driveway and didn’t get up. Pirate had been old and sick, long since retired from sea life, but his death still came as a shock.
“Joe was really upset,” Jenn recalled, but delaying the trip was out of the question. “He was also a good compartmentalizer,” she said. “Once he got his head in the game, that was it.”
Between her own waves of grief, Jenn tried not to think of Pirate’s death as an ill omen. But her apprehension didn’t abate as they drove up to Nantucket and pulled out to sea. The sky over the ocean looked ominous and foreboding in a way it never had before.
Keeping watch and listening to music on Monday night, she couldn’t ignore it any more; she looked up at the stars and asked for a sign. The next song that started playing was I Am Disappeared by Frank Turner, which is about open seas and pirate ships and people “wrapped up tight in the things that will kill them”. Jenn ripped her headphone jack out so hard that she almost lost her phone overboard.
She tried to tell herself it was only the approaching storm. There is little room for superstition or doubt on a 45-ft boat. Fear is contagious in an environment that small. You keep your worries to yourself.
Joe was in a good mood that next morning, which for him meant garden-variety grumbling instead of barking out rapid-fire orders. Jenn always told him the same thing before he splashed: “Have fun.” Never: “Be careful.” You don’t speak like that on the water. But on 29 July 2025, at 10 in the morning, she kissed him and said: “Be careful.”
He looked at her strangely.
“Of course,” he said, and dropped out of sight.
Seventy-five minutes later, Jenn was pacing the deck, watching the clock, waiting to feel the tell-tale sensation of their hook being freed from the wreck. Four divers, including Joe, were below, and they were behind on schedule, which she remarked on to Eric Takakjian, who was working calmly in the galley at his laptop.
That’s when she spotted Joe’s fins, breaking the surface behind Tenacious’s transom.
He wasn’t supposed to surface for another hour and a half. The nitrogen he had accumulated at depth was still in his body, expanding faster than his body could expel it, a potentially lethal state.
Jenn screamed. Joe, disoriented but conscious, seemed to moan and reach for the equipment line. Takakjian was in the chase boat before Jenn could move. He grabbed Joe, flipped him on to his back. Joe made one more sound and then went still as Takakjian started slicing his gear off. Jenn was half in the water, tugging at his equipment from underneath as Takakjian cut above.
Somewhere in those seconds she understood: it was already too late. There was no getting Joe back down deep enough for the decompression he would need to survive.
They got him onboard with the hydraulic winch and started performing CPR until Jenn said: “Eric. If you’re doing this because you think I don’t know he’s dead, you can stop.” Takakjian took his hands off Joe’s chest. They looked at each other. They were both crying.
It took hours for the three other divers – Packer and two longtime Tenacious crewmates named Andrew Donn and Rick Simon – to surface.
Only Simon had seen anything wrong underwater.
He had run into Joe while ascending, 50ft down, his eyes closed and his tether unclipped from the anchor line. Simon grabbed Joe and performed the standard first move for an unresponsive or distressed diver at depth, hitting his bailout valve, a mouthpiece switch that pulls a diver off the recycled gas loop he has been breathing and on to a shot of open-circuit gas from a dedicated emergency tank.
Joe’s eyes opened. It wasn’t clear if he was fully conscious, but he shot up toward the surface, too quickly for Simon to safely follow. Simon lost sight of Joe in the murky water overhead, but figured he might be heading for the emergency oxygen bottle they had hung at 20ft.
Back on the boat, they radioed the Coast Guard, who told them to bring Joe’s body in. There was no point in sending officers out if he was already gone. “In that moment,” Jenn said, “that felt like the worst thing in the world.”
The crew put Joe in a sleeping bag and zipped it up. The 200-mile return journey was 16 hours of tortuous silence, punctuated only by the sounds of someone breaking out in sobs. At some point, Jenn crawled into the top bunk, unable to bear her usual berth, which faced Joe’s empty one.
It’s over, she remembered thinking. It’s all over.
Jenn spent years learning how to find what was lost. She hadn’t yet learned how to be one of the people left behind.
As she moved through life underwater, the rest of the crew was undone in their own ways. Packer, 67 years old this summer, found himself unable to move for days at a time. He would get dressed to work out, sit down to put his sneakers on, and find himself unable to rise again, let alone walk the 10ft to his basement gym.
Whitehead was ready to sell his rebreather and give up technical diving entirely. Ogden, who had pushed through his grief by immediately getting back in the water, told him to stop being stupid. “I was like this insane cheerleader for three months,” he said.
An autopsy report ruled Joe’s cause of death as barometric trauma: the bends. What actually caused him to go into paroxysms underwater, before he shot to the surface, will probably never be known. The Coast Guard investigation, which stretches on, found that his equipment hadn’t failed. “I’m a nervous diver, so Joe always used to tell me, ‘If you can breathe, you can think,’” Jenn said. “I saw him calm and collected underwater in the most catastrophic situations. So the only conclusion I can draw is that he couldn’t breathe.”
But she could breathe. She could think. She started seeing a therapist for the first time in her life. She was diagnosed with post-traumatic stress disorder, which, yeah, that felt about right.
Her plan began to take shape in the weeks after she collapsed aboard Tenacious in her ballgown and heels. Two trusted friends in the diving world, Dudas and Harold Moyers, came over to install a television at the farm one night, and she decided to lay it all out for them then and there. It was an exacting 10-year plan to rebuild the operation to what it had been, this time with Jenn at the helm, culminating in the scattering of Joe’s ashes on the U-550, the sub whose discovery had interrupted the Olympics on NBC.
Moyers, a captain himself, got a funny look on his face. She waited for him to tell her she was insane, advise her to sell Tenacious.
“I don’t think it’s going to take that long,” he said instead.
In early October, they held a memorial at a friend’s restaurant called the Shipwreck Grill. They sounded the foghorn of the Andrea Doria, one of Joe’s most prized treasures. In front of the crew, colleagues, friends, and Joe’s family, including his parents, who had flown out from Lebanon, Jenn gathered herself to tell them about the dive they had done the previous weekend, their first expedition since Joe’s death.
“The weather was rough. We learned the other boats had turned back …” she said. “It was scary. And just then I heard a voice. It was the first time I really felt him. The first time he had really talked to me since he died. That voice whispered one word: ‘Forward.’ So I put the throttle down, and it wound up being a beautiful day.”
Though she didn’t say it at the memorial, what they had accomplished on that single dive – retrieving the anchor of the Maurice Tracy – had been her plan for the entire fall. As soon as Ogden surfaced with the anchor and the lift bag, he looked to Jenn. So did the rest of the crew. What next?
They went diving again the next day.
In January, the crew of Tenacious held an all-hands muster at her house – still without furniture – and unanimously voted her their captain.
“You just inherited a huge, huge family of brothers,” Takakjian told her, “who are going to help you for ever.”
She told them how it would work with her in charge. She would make the calls on things she was certain about but, unlike Joe, she wanted their input. They would all carry on Joe’s legacy together.
This summer, the plan revolves around the Carolina – Joe’s favorite wreck. No one has found her bell yet. Jenn has a good feeling about it.
“All I know is,” she said, “that the further from shore I am, the closer I am to him.”

Latinos refuse to be put in a box. “In reality, we are a kaleidoscope of cultures, of talents, of thoughts, of passions,” says actor Sofia Carson, one of TIME’s 2026 Latino Leaders.
Since 2023, TIME has marked Hispanic Heritage Month by highlighting influential Latinos in the U.S. across entertainment, politics, business, sports, science, and more.
This year, we selected a group that represents some of the wide range of experiences and backgrounds within the community that makes up a fifth of the U.S. population and continues to grow in economic and cultural influence—from stars of Netflix, like Carson, to champions of the NBA, like the Knicks’ Jose Alvarado; from disrupters of fashion, like Larroudé co-founder Marina Larroudé, to innovators of food, like Michelin- and James Beard-recognized chef and restaurateur Nelson German and leaders of nonprofits, like Mónica Ramírez, advocate and founder of Justice for Migrant Women.
Each has found a way to bring their heritage to their passion and reach new heights as a result, many with an eye toward uplifting others along the way.

Inside an Uzbek wedding: traditions, family and the start of married life
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:00:14 UTC
22:00:14 KST

Civil registration, wedding plov, family blessings and ‘Kelin Salom’ are among the customs that accompany couples as they begin married life in Uzbekistan.
In Uzbekistan, a wedding is a major family occasion, bringing relatives, friends and several generations together. Before the celebrations begin, however, the couple first make their marriage official at the civil registry. Traditional customs can start earlier, with families gathering for meals, blessings and ceremonies that prepare the couple for their wedding day.
From the registry office to the wedding table
On the wedding day, preparations take place separately in the two families’ homes. The groom gets ready with relatives and friends, while the bride prepares at her family home.
The groom later arrives with his friends as part of ‘Kuyov Navkar’, a traditional procession or gathering accompanying the groom to the bride’s home.
Food is central to the celebrations. At the bride’s home, guests gather around a table for wedding plov, Uzbekistan’s signature rice dish, typically cooked with meat, carrots and onions.
Before leaving for the main wedding reception, the bride and groom receive blessings from their parents and older relatives.
For the groom, Abbos Sabitov, the day marks a new stage in his life.
“Seeing all my loved ones together in one place is a great happiness for me. I'm starting a new chapter in my life. I feel a great sense of responsibility, and I'm fully aware of everything that comes with it.”
The gathering then moves into the main celebration, where music, dancing and food bring the two families together.
A celebration for two families
Flowers are exchanged between the newly joined families as relatives and friends take part in the festivities. For Feruza Avazova, the groom's sister, these gatherings provide an opportunity for relatives to meet, share a meal and offer their wishes to the newlyweds.
Uzbek weddings can involve hundreds of guests, with friends and relatives taking part in dances, games and other activities during the reception.
The celebrations also extend beyond the wedding day. Avazova mentions Challar, a post-wedding gathering hosted by the bride’s family for the newlyweds and the groom’s relatives, as well as Beshik Toy, a later celebration held after the birth of a child.
At the end of the wedding, the couple's mothers come forward to offer their blessings. For many families, this is one of the most emotional moments of the celebration.
Welcoming the bride into a new family
The celebrations continue at the groom's home, where his mother welcomes the bride and wishes the couple a happy life together.
For Dildora Adilova, the groom's mother, modern family life also comes with changing expectations.
“I think modern brides should be well-rounded in every way. She should be able to manage the household, have a career and continue her education.”
One of the most recognisable customs that follows is Kelin Salom, a formal greeting performed by the bride as she meets members of her new family.
For Muslima Sabitova, the bride, the ceremony carries a personal meaning.
“Performing the Kelin Salom and meeting the members of my new family gave me a different kind of emotion. I expect love and kindness from my new family, and that they will treat me like their own daughter.”
Wedding customs in Samarkand
Wedding traditions vary between regions. In Samarkand, some ceremonies take place before the main celebration and are closely connected with the first moments of married life.
One such custom is chimildiq, a private space prepared for the bride and groom in the bride's home. Among the rituals associated with it, the couple look at themselves in a mirror and feed each other honey, symbolising wishes for a sweet married life.
After these intimate customs, the wedding becomes public again, with photographs, music, food and gatherings involving both families.
Samarkand's historic architecture provides a backdrop for wedding photographs, with couples often visiting landmarks and studios as part of the day's preparations.
The groom's friend Bobojon Ergashev describes his own role as part of the wedding party.
“My role here is to take care of any problems or issues the groom might have during the wedding, to make sure everything runs smoothly.”
Traditions that begin long before the wedding
For the families involved, preparing for a wedding can take years. Relatives plan the ceremonies, organise meals, prepare clothing and coordinate gatherings involving large numbers of guests.
The customs may differ between families and regions, but many centre on the same elements: family participation, blessings from elders, shared meals and the formal welcome of a new member into the family.
By the time the celebrations end, the wedding has marked more than a single ceremony. It has brought two families together and set the couple on the next stage of their life together.

In northern Greece, cut-price illegal pesticides smuggled in by organised crime networks are tempting cash-strapped farmers, despite mounting evidence of the risks to health and the environment.
Up to 14% of Europe's pesticide market may be counterfeit or illegal, rising to 25% in parts of Greece.
Farmers in Serres say the appeal is purely financial: illegal products can cost a sixth of the approved price. But scientists warn these unregulated substances, often smuggled from Turkey, contain carcinogenic chemicals banned across the EU.
Police describe organised international networks moving products by car, truck and even tourist bus. Since 2020, authorities have seized nearly 8,700 tonnes of banned pesticides, part of a trade worth an estimated $20 billion globally each year, according to TRACIT.

‘Bogland’ explores beautifully strange ecosystems
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
13:00:00 UTC
22:00:00 KST

Bogland explores beautifully strange ecosystems
These water-logged spaces are filled with decay and teeming with life
Bogland
Merritt R. Turetsky
W.W. Norton & Company, $29.99
Thank goodness Merritt Turetsky became captivated by boglands. The ecosystem ecologist evocatively describes the beauty, danger and vitality of these ecosystems in her book, Bogland.
“Buffering the space between forest and river, boglands refuse to be defined as either land or water,” writes Turetsky, of the University of Colorado Boulder. Their soggy, oxygen-starved conditions make it difficult for microbes to break down organic matter, allowing carbon-rich peat to form. It’s that peat that sets boglands apart from all other ecosystems, she writes.
Boglands primarily consist of bogs and fens (and sometimes marshes and swamps). Bogs derive their water from precipitation, are lacking in nutrients and are known for sphagnum moss and carnivorous plants such as sundew and pitcher plants. Fens, in contrast, get their water from streams and groundwater as well as rain. Because fens have access to more nutrients, they support more diverse plant communities.
Anytime Turetsky dove into her own adventures in boglands, I was transfixed. It’s in these moments — trying to stay calm amid insect swarms, reaching her arms deep into the murk and wading through muck with other devoted scientists — that the book shines.
Turetsky’s bug baptism occurred on a college trip to a Nova Scotia bog as hundreds of mosquitoes, black flies and other insects quickly descended around her netted head. The sounds “are seared deep into my memory,” she writes. “After a half an hour I knew I needed to conquer the noise or be driven insane.”
In Brunei, Turetsky meets up with a peatland restoration research team. The group enters the waist-deep water, moves through a rehabilitated area, then reaches a healthy peat swamp. This secret garden, Turetsky writes, “is pure pitcher plant heaven — unlike anything in my northern bogs, with layers of pitcher plants not only growing on the ground but dangling from the trees.” The colors and shapes she describes reminded me of something Dr. Suess would dream up.
But along with their beauty and wonder, boglands harbor potential danger. Turetsky recalls getting lost when she ventured out alone to check something in a study plot. “Even in familiar terrain, boglands are deceiving,” she writes. “I was disoriented even as I felt surrounded by what seemed well-known.” Luckily, it wasn’t too long before she found her team — otherwise, they would have had to commence a search-and-rescue mission.
Turetsky’s devotion to boglands celebrates the contradictions in these ecosystems: Filled with decay but teeming with life; places of refuge and risk; an ally in a changing climate as well as a potential foe.
Peatlands occupy just 3 percent of the world’s land area but store around 30 percent of Earth’s soil carbon, Turetsky writes. “This far exceeds the carbon stored in the world’s forest biomass, despite forests covering a much larger area.”
However, boglands are being drained for agriculture and damaged by wildfires, drought and changing climate conditions. As more are destroyed, there is a serious threat that they could release their carbon stores, adding to greenhouse gas emissions, Turetsky notes.
I have a general understanding of climate science, but I wish Turetsky would have provided more basic information on that topic. Just as she lost her way in the bog, I got turned around by mentions of different warming periods and ice ages. But the bigger message of how boglands formed over geologic time and their contribution to Earth’s climate system still comes through.
Overall, my time spent in Bogland felt expertly navigated by Turetsky. I emerged firmly on team boglands. For me, the book’s ultimate gift was igniting a reverence for these incredible ecosystems. And to my great delight, Turetsky closes with a list of bogs, some with boardwalks, that people can visit. I’m grabbing my bug spray now.
Buy Bogland from Bookshop.org. Science News is a Bookshop.org affiliate and will earn a commission on purchases made from links in this article.

Survey: 77% of Ukrainians support armed resistance to Russia despite threat to energy infrastructure in winter
Ukraine
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
16:00:00 EEST
22:00:00 KST

Survey: 77% of Ukrainians support armed resistance to Russia despite threat to energy infrastructure in winter
A survey has found that 77% of Ukrainians believe that even if Russia attempts to attack energy infrastructure in winter to leave citizens without electricity, heating and sewage, Ukraine should continue its armed resistance to Russian aggression.
Source: a survey by the Kyiv International Institute of Sociology (KIIS), conducted from 21 August to 10 September 2026
Details: Meanwhile, 12% believe that under such circumstances, Ukraine should accept any Russian demands, even if they amount to capitulation.
Another 11% were unable to decide.
The survey found that in all regions, a majority of residents – from 83% in Kyiv to 68% in the east – believe that Ukraine should continue its armed resistance despite everything.
The sociologists note that Kyiv has the highest figure among all regions, despite Russia continuing its missile and drone strikes.
For reference: The KIIS survey was conducted from 21 August to 10 September 2026 using phone interviews in all regions of Ukraine controlled by the government. A total of 1,003 respondents aged 18 and over who were living in government-controlled territory at the time of the survey were interviewed.
Under normal circumstances, the formal margin of error for such a sample (with a 0.95 probability and taking into account a design effect of 1.3) would not exceed 4.1% for figures close to 50%, 3.5% for figures close to 25%, 2.5% for figures close to 10%, and 1.8% for figures close to 5%.
In wartime conditions, in addition to the stated formal margin of error, there is a certain degree of systematic bias.

Iraq has discovered a site inside the country used to launch drones, authorities said, as an investigation continues to identify those responsible for recent attacks on Saudi Arabia.

Saudi Arabia said it would not retaliate “at this stage” after Baghdad pledged to investigate the attacks and prevent Iraqi territory from being used for further strikes.
The discovery comes as Iraq faces pressure to prevent its territory from being used to launch attacks against neighbouring countries.
Baghdad has pledged to investigate the incidents and take measures to prevent further attacks, while Riyadh has indicated that it will refrain from retaliation for now.

China warns of orbital 'battlefield' after US confirms space weapons
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
12:59:00 UTC
21:59:00 KST

China warned Tuesday against turning space into a “battlefield,” while Russia said it must remain free of weapons after the United States confirmed it has deployed arms in orbit.
Analysts said that the revelation by Washington confirmed an open secret but the timing of the announcement risked exacerbating tensions with Beijing and the risk of a weapons race in space.
The announcement marked a new stage in the major powers' rush for military space capabilities, driven by rapid technological advancements but lacking a comprehensive legal framework.
"The U.S. has on-orbit space control weapons capable of defending the Joint Force against hostile adversary action," a U.S. Space Force spokesperson said in a statement that did not provide specifics on the weapons.
China's Foreign Ministry opposed the move, warning against the "weaponization of outer space, turning it into a battlefield, as well as an arms race there."
"We urge the U.S. side to stop expanding its military capabilities and preparing for war in outer space," ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun told a news briefing.
Russia called Tuesday for space to be kept "free of any weapons."
"We are counting on broad international consolidation to continue working toward the complete demilitarization of space," said Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov.
The U.S. statement did not provide concrete details about what weapons are deployed in space and how many.
"Space control encapsulates the mission areas required to contest and control the space domain – employing kinetic and non-kinetic means to affect adversary capabilities," the statement said.
"These capabilities can be employed for offensive and defensive purposes," it added.
Non-kinetic means could include lasers to blind satellites, or even electromagnetic or cyber jamming of their data links.
For Washington, both Russia and China – its two main geopolitical rivals – pose potential threats to U.S. interests in space.
China "develops and operates space and counterspace capabilities as part of a military modernization strategy," according to the U.S. Space Force.
Russia, meanwhile, "views space as a war-fighting domain and believes space supremacy will be a decisive factor in future conflicts," it said.
The militarization of space is as old as the space race itself – as soon as the USSR's Sputnik was launched into orbit as the first probe in space in 1957, Washington and Moscow began exploring ways to both arm and destroy satellites.
India in March 2019 said it had destroyed a low-orbiting satellite, following in the footsteps of Russia, China and the U.S., in a missile test that proved the nation was among the world's most advanced space powers.
Song Zhongping, an independent military commentator based in Hong Kong and former Chinese army instructor, told AFP the announcement was "no surprise at all" as the fact that the U.S. possesses such weapons "has long been an open secret."
"The only difference is that the U.S. used to keep it ambiguous, whereas now it has gone public, which shows that Washington is no longer trying to hide it," he added.
"China will not stand by and do nothing," he warned, noting Beijing already has its own space station in orbit.
"Whether China deploys weapons in space will depend entirely on whether the United States moves in that direction," he said.
The Wall Street Journal said Beijing supplied Iran with satellite imagery that enabled Tehran to precisely target military facilities and kill U.S. service members in Jordan in July – a claim China has denied.
The biggest worry was once about nuclear weapons in space, but the superpowers and other countries signed the Outer Space Treaty in 1967, banning weapons of mass destruction in orbit.
Since then, Russia, the United States, China and India have explored ways to fight in space outside of that treaty, including by targeting rivals' satellites, which are key for military communications, surveillance and navigation.
"There is a regulatory vacuum because Article 4 of the 1967 treaty only forbids weapons of mass destruction and nuclear weapons in orbit," said space law expert Laetitia Cesari.
She said the topic would likely be on the agenda at the next UN disarmament meeting in November.
"Moscow and Beijing have been wanting to work for about twenty years on a treaty to prevent the placement of weapons in space," while "Western states are trying instead to work on the notion of responsible behavior," Cesari said.
The U.S. announcement "will likely give impetus to the restrictive approach that Moscow and Beijing are pushing for," she added.

A commercial vessel is anchored off Yemen’s coast at Bab al-Mandeb, in the straits connecting the Red Sea with the Gulf of Aden and the Indian Ocean on Sept 12.
Saudi Arabia vowed on Sept. 15 to respond “firmly” to attacks by Yemen’s Houthis, after the Iran-backed fighters wounded 13 people in the kingdom, as Riyadh turns to its regional and Western allies for support.
The Houthis have been engaged in renewed fighting with Saudi-backed government forces since Yemen’s civil war reignited in July, when the group declared a maritime blockade on Riyadh and began targeting its ships in the Red Sea.
They seized control of Yemen’s Red Sea coast and the Bab al-Mandab Strait last week, which has become vital as the wider U.S.-Iran war chokes the Strait of Hormuz.
The Houthis have also targeted the oil infrastructure of Saudi Arabia, the world’s leading crude exporter, pushing oil prices above the $100 threshold.
In the wake of developments, Saudi Arabia turns to allies to find support against the Houthis. 
Egyptian President Abdel Fattah al-Sisi received Saudi Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman in Cairo on Sept. 15.
The Mecca Joint Defense Agreement was signed by Pakistan, Saudi Arabia and Türkiye in August. Egypt has recently been reported to be considering joining the alliance, while Türkiye has said the agreement remains open to other countries in the region.
Bloomberg on Sept. 14 reported that U.K. Prime Minister Andy Burnham is weighing Saudi Arabia’s requests for military support against Houthi attacks.
The U.K. fears further Houthi control of the Bab el-Mandeb Strait could have a “catastrophic” impact on global shipping and oil prices, hurting the economy as the new government seeks to ease the cost-of-living crisis.
So far, London has approved only the deployment of British military advisers to Saudi Arabia, despite Riyadh’s requests for operational support, the report said.
It came a week after tnews outlet Axios reported that U.S. President Donald Trump rejected a request Salman for strikes against Houthi fighters in Yemen.
Washington will provide Saudi Arabia with intelligence on the Houthis and targeting data, and more than 200 U.S. military personnel are already in Saudi Arabia providing “non-kinetic support,” according to a U.S. official cited by Axios.
A Houthi strike in western Yemen on Sept. 14 killed eight government soldiers, according to two military sources.
The group also claimed a new large-scale attack using “dozens of ballistic missiles and drones” against King Khalid airbase in Khamis Mushait, in southern Saudi Arabia.
In the early hours of Sept. 15, the Saudi civil defense issued air-raid alerts in six regions, before lifting them.
The Riyadh-led coalition confirmed there had been missile and drone attacks the previous day on Khamis Mushait, Abha and Taif, reporting 13 civilians wounded and damage to seven houses and two vehicles.
Saudi Arabia responded with more than 50 strikes, as it had the day before, according to the Houthis.
The coalition said it would deal with the attacks “firmly to protect the sovereignty of the Kingdom” and would take “all necessary operational measures to deter this... hostile and extremist approach.”

The Iran war has cost $33.4 billion so far, according to a new Pentagon watchdog report. It also confirms a historic first: an F-35A took combat damage
A Pentagon inspector general report found the Iran conflict has cost $33.4 billion through late June, detailing the loss of multiple fighter jets, helicopters and drones alongside severe munitions shortfalls. The report also confirms an F-35A sustained combat damage for the first time in the aircraft's operational history.
American aviation assets also sustained notable combat operational losses throughout the campaign. Additionally, a fifth-generation F-35A stealth fighter suffered damage from enemy fire, marking the first time an aircraft of its model sustained combat damage in operational history.
The operational expenditure of advanced precision weaponry has strained national inventory levels, prompting defense procurement officials to seek streamlined supply chain frameworks and accelerated manufacturing timelines.
In response to the documented equipment losses and stockpile drawdowns, executive leadership has emphasized that national defense production remains at historic levels.

The cost of travel on commuter trains in Ukraine will increase — fares and when they will be introduced have become known
Ukraine
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
15:55:01 EEST
21:55:01 KST

The cost of travel on commuter trains in Ukraine will increase — fares and when they will be introduced have become known
Kyiv • UNN
The cost of travel on suburban trains will range from UAH 30 to UAH 150, depending on the distance. Starting September 15, new fares will apply in three regions.
Ukrzaliznytsia is switching to a new pricing model for suburban trains, with the minimum fare set at 30 hryvnias and the maximum at 150 hryvnias. The new fares will be introduced gradually and will already apply in three regions today, UNN reports.
We are switching to a six-zone fare system. Each zone covers a certain distance, and regardless of how many kilometers a passenger travels within it, a fixed fare will apply
Details
According to Ukrzaliznytsia, the current situation with passenger transportation in suburban services is extremely difficult. A number of administrations chronically fail to pay for the transportation of passengers eligible for benefits. The situation is also being aggravated by the rising cost of electricity, fuel, and other resources in recent years, which further increases the cost of transportation.
These changes will make it possible to partially reduce losses from suburban transportation without significantly increasing fares. Passengers who travel the maximum distances within their tariff zones will not feel any increase in fares at all. The new system will also make it possible to preserve existing suburban services and stops, as well as direct more funds toward the repair and modernization of suburban trains
The following fare schedule will apply:
-  up to 50 km — UAH 30;  
-  51–90 km — UAH 55;  
-  91–130 km — UAH 75;  
-  131–170 km — UAH 95;  
-  171–250 km — UAH 115;  
-  over 250 km — UAH 150.  
Ukrzaliznytsia added that the new fares are being introduced gradually. Starting September 15, they will apply in the Kyiv, Chernihiv, and Zhytomyr regions.
Starting September 22 — in the Odesa, Dnipropetrovsk, Mykolaiv, and Cherkasy regions.
Later, the new six-zone system will also operate in other regions of Ukraine.

Schiess Highlights Space Force’s Role in Epic Fury
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
12:54:43 UTC
21:54:43 KST

NATIONAL HARBOR, Md.—Guardians are playing a critical role in U.S. operations against Iran, applying electronic warfare effects to stifle enemy air defenses and communication systems and contributing other space capabilities vital to the war effort, said Gen. Douglas Schiess in his first major address one since becoming Chief of Space Operations 12 days ago.
In fact, Schiess said, Operation Epic Fury may not have happened at all without the Space Force.
“Our Guardians have been tested several times this past year. When the order came for Epic Fury, the Space Force was a part of the decision calculus on whether we executed the joint campaign at all—we were part of the minimum force package,” Schiess said Sept. 15 at AFA’s Air, Space & Cyber Conference in National Harbor, Md. “This is the minimum force required that a commander considers when sending military forces into harm’s way. The Joint Force made it clear: Without the Space Force’s capabilities, the Joint Force could not—and would not—move forward.”
Since the start of the conflict in late February, U.S. officials have touted space systems’ importance to operations. During a briefing in early March, Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff Air Force Gen. Dan Caine said U.S. Space Command was among the “first movers” during initial strikes on Iran, helping create a path for the joint force to strike more than 1,000 targets in the campaign’s first 24 hours.
“During Operation Epic Fury, our components, notably at U.S. Central Command and Space Command—with support from components at European Command and Pacific Command—demonstrated that Guardians are not supporting from the sidelines as a ‘nice to have,’” said Schiess, who previously oversaw operations for the Space Force prior to becoming CSO. “We are delivering spacepower directly into the fight as a ‘need to have.’”
The Pentagon and U.S. Central Command have provided broad details on space effects that have contributed to the effort, and Schiess’ comments underscore just how important the Space Force has been in U.S. efforts against Iran. A key role of the Space Force in the conflict, U.S. officials have told Air & Space Forces Magazine, is the use of electronic warfare to disrupt Iranian air defenses to clear a path for U.S. strike aircraft.
During his speech, Schiess shared a dramatic example of the Space Force’s EW efforts. A Guardian he referred to only as Specialist Gray, who deployed to CENTCOM with the 42nd Electronic Warfare Detachment in the opening hours of Operation Epic Fury, played a particular role.
“She received a priority target package with one critical objective: reconfigure her weapon system and execute critical non-kinetic fires,” Schiess said.
She continued to operate a key EW console as enemy missiles targeted her site.
“Even as Alarm Red sirens were blaring and incoming missiles targeted her site, she courageously held the line—protecting forces, denying adversary capability, and delivering space effects until her commander had to literally pull her from the console to safety,” he said.
Like every service component in CENTCOM, U.S. Space Forces Central has contributed to U.S. targeting, locating targets such as Iranian jammers for other platforms’ strikes.
The Space Force is also using sensors and radars that can detect incoming Iranian missiles aimed at U.S. bases, providing critical minutes of warning to allow service members to escape to cover and allowing base air defenders to be ready to combat the threats with Patriot and THAAD ballistic missile interceptors, U.S. officials said. Iran has attacked U.S. bases dozens of times since the conflict began on Feb. 28, and the Space Force’s intelligence assets are monitoring Iranian activity.
The Space Force is delivering “combat-credible forces to combatant commands,” Schiess said, to “give combatant commanders like Adm. Cooper in Central Command the space capabilities needed to fight and win.”
Iran has validated the importance of U.S. space assets by hitting back at them. U.S. missile defense and space assets have prime targets for Iranian missiles and drones, which have struck and damaged radar sites throughout the region.
“A bad day in space is felt by everyone—it’s felt by our Joint Force, it’s felt by our nation’s economy, and the citizens all around that rely on space capabilities,” Schiess said. “Guardians, we cannot fail, and we know that we won’t.”

Turkish foreign minister calls for return to pre-war status quo in Strait of Hormuz
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
12:54:34 UTC
21:54:34 KST

Türkiye's Foreign Minister Hakan Fidan on Tuesday called for safe, uninterrupted, and free passage through the Strait of Hormuz and a swift return to the pre-war status quo, while warning against the further spread of the Russia-Ukraine war.
Speaking at a joint news conference with his Azerbaijani counterpart Jeyhun Bayramov in Baku, Fidan said developments in neighboring Iran directly concern both Türkiye and Azerbaijan.
“Iran’s security, stability, and prosperity naturally have a direct impact on our region. In this regard, we want the current conflict to be resolved through diplomatic means as soon as possible,” he said.
Fidan said increasing reciprocal attacks have seriously undermined peace efforts, and called for an immediate end to actions fueling regional tensions.
“At the same time, safe, uninterrupted and free passage through the Strait of Hormuz must be allowed, and the pre-war status quo must be restored as soon as possible,” he said, adding that Türkiye remains in continuous contact with all relevant parties to help resolve the crisis.
Turning to the Russia-Ukraine war, Fidan said Türkiye has made major efforts since the beginning of the conflict to keep the Black Sea out of the fighting.
He said Ankara had largely succeeded in doing so through its strict implementation of the Montreux Convention and efforts such as the Black Sea Grain Initiative.
“However, increasing attacks on civilian vessels in the Black Sea in recent months have seriously harmed the safety of life, property, navigation, and the environment in the region,” Fidan said.
Noting that Turkish and Azerbaijani sailors had also lost their lives in the attacks, he said: “The spread of the war into the Black Sea and the threat it poses to the lives of our citizens and the activities of our companies is unacceptable.”
Fidan said Türkiye has conveyed the necessary warnings to the parties and believes common ground could be found if there is sufficient political will and good faith.
“We will continue to make every effort for peace,” he said, recalling President Recep Tayyip Erdogan’s position that “even the worst peace is better than war itself.”
Risk of wider war
Responding to questions, Fidan warned that the Russia-Ukraine conflict has intensified significantly over the past year and carries the risk of spreading beyond its current geography.
He said Türkiye’s position has remained unchanged throughout the more than four-and-a-half-year war, arguing that it was “a war that should never have started, should not spread, and should end immediately.”
Fidan pointed to Türkiye’s diplomatic efforts, including its role in the Black Sea grain deal, prisoner exchanges, and talks between Russia and Ukraine in Istanbul.
He said Ankara is also working with the parties on proposals aimed at preventing attacks on energy infrastructure and improving security in the Black Sea.
Fidan described the conflict as having transformed over the past year from a “war of attrition” into a “war of destruction,” with commercial facilities and infrastructure increasingly being targeted and civilian casualties rising.
“From time to time, there are statements about the possibility of the war spreading not only in the Black Sea but also to other European countries,” he said.
"We do not even want to think about it, let alone talk about it. The geographical expansion of the war is the last thing we want to see.”
Fidan warned that unless the war ends, energy prices, food security, and the environment will face greater risks, while the danger of the conflict spreading will increase.
Türkiye-Azerbaijan ties, regional connectivity
Fidan also highlighted the strategic relationship between Türkiye and Azerbaijan, saying the two countries remain bound by the principle of “one nation, two states.”
He said economic and commercial cooperation, as well as energy, transportation, and connectivity, constitute key pillars of bilateral ties.
Fidan described the Baku-Tbilisi-Kars railway as one of the most important components of the Middle Corridor and stressed the importance of ensuring its effective use.
He also highlighted plans to strengthen connectivity between Türkiye and Azerbaijan via Nakhchivan, including the Zangezur Corridor and the Kars-Igdir-Aralik-Dilucu railway.
“God willing, the Kars-Dilucu railway will be completed toward the end of 2029. Thus, a train coming from Azerbaijan will be able to reach Türkiye without interruption,” he said.
Fidan said such infrastructure would provide a significant logistical opportunity and strengthen the Turkic world’s access to Anatolia and Europe, while facilitating Türkiye’s access to Central Asia across the Caspian Sea.
On the South Caucasus, Fidan said Türkiye welcomed recent progress toward a final peace agreement between Azerbaijan and Armenia and Baku’s “constructive approach.”
“We want the South Caucasus to be associated not with conflicts, but with peace, transportation, energy, trade, and economic development,” he said.
Fidan also said Türkiye and Azerbaijan would continue deepening defense and security cooperation through joint exercises, training, experience-sharing, and growing cooperation between their defense industries.
He added that the Organization of Turkic States foreign ministers would meet in Ankara on Oct. 29, followed by the organization’s 13th summit on Oct. 30.
“We see this summit as a historic step that will consolidate the Turkic world’s common vision for the future,” he said.

'Way of Being' motto of 2028 Song and Dance Celebration
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
12:53:00 UTC
21:53:00 KST

'Way of Being' motto of 2028 Song and Dance Celebration
Starting September 15, all groups wishing to take part in the 14th Youth Song and Dance Celebration and the 29th Song Celebration and 22nd Dance Celebration, "Olemise viis," in 2028 can register to participate.
Margus Toomla, a member of the management board of the Estonian Song and Dance Celebration Foundation, said the 2028 celebration will be exceptional, as it will bring together the traditions of the Youth Song and Dance Celebration and the general Song and Dance Celebration for the first time in history. This time, the celebration will last a total of two weeks, from June 30 to July 9. "The first week of the celebration will focus on the Dance Celebration and Folk Music Celebration, while the second will be devoted to the procession and Song Celebration."
Although the program is extensive and the celebration will be considerably longer than usual, Toomla is confident everything will run smoothly and that the wide-ranging program will give more people an opportunity to attend one of the Dance Celebration performances or Song Celebration concerts. He said the Folk Music Celebration's more spacious venue at the Estonian Open Air Museum will offer plenty of opportunities to experience the celebration in an authentic setting.
The major celebration's theme "Olemise viis" ("Way of Being" or "Tune of Being") ties the Song, Dance and Folk Music celebrations together into a conceptual whole. Song Celebration artistic director Riivo Jõgi said the phrase "Olemise viis" is appealingly ambiguous, allowing considerable freedom both artistically and philosophically. "Since the Youth and general Song Celebrations will be held together this time and the celebration will last three days, an axis of past, present and future emerged quite naturally."
He explained that the first day will look back in time, with participants jointly performing music that has resonated with people in Estonia over different decades. "This will be a special concert because the audience will be able to sing along with the choir for a large part of it. There has been communal singing at the Song Celebration before, but this time an entire concert has been built around it. The second day will belong to adult singers, while children and young people will take the stage under the Song Festival Grounds' arch on the third day."
"'Olemise viis' will bring the meanings of being Estonian and coming together to the Song Festival Grounds through our shared history. I hope these three days will genuinely form a single whole and that people will leave the celebration thinking about where we come from, what unites us here in Estonia and what we want to carry forward into the future," Jõgi said.
--
Editor: Kaspar Viilup, Marcus Turovski

US urges Europe, Canada to take lead on conventional defense, NATO envoy says
Unknown
Unknown Publisher
2026-09-15
12:52:58 UTC
21:52:58 KST

Yasin Gungor
15 September 2026•Update: 15 September 2026
The US is pressing European allies and Canada to assume primary responsibility for conventional defense in Europe, seeking to free up American military power for potential challenges in the Pacific, US Ambassador to NATO Matthew Whitaker said Tuesday.
"That's going to free up the United States to project power globally and deal with the things like the Pacific situations that we may confront in the future," Whitaker told US television network Fox Business.
Whitaker explained that Washington wants "strong, capable allies that we can rely on," arguing that European partners hollowed out their capabilities after the Soviet Union's collapse in 1991, while American taxpayers carried a burden.
He maintained that while partners are stepping up, progress is not occurring fast enough.
The push aligns with a broader realignment by the administration of US President Donald Trump to shift military resources away from European land defense toward deterring China under the 2026 US National Defense Strategy.
Under this framework, Washington expects wealthy European capitals to provide conventional frontline forces while the Pentagon maintains nuclear deterrence and reinforcement options.
The diplomatic initiative builds on calls made in February 2025 by US Defense Secretary Pete Hegseth urging allies to take ownership of continental security.
The Pentagon subsequently formalized the policy in July by launching the six-month review to reassess troop deployments across the continent.
